Taciturn Parables

Six novellas · 83,884 words · English

The Walnut Wood

It was a morning of perfectly clear sky, the warmth of the sun passing through the needles of the old pines that are still here today. The breeze blew cool, savouring the murmur of the woodland so far from the bustle of the city, a clean air that is light to breathe. The “croo-croo” of the turtle doves. Sitting in a corner of the garden, that sensation stroking my being, eyes shut tight. I can see those two little girls running around their grandmother, I remember those voices full of glee, the toys scattered across the floor, the clatter of pots in the kitchen while my mother made lunch.

That day, my grandmother, my sister and I planted a walnut in the middle of the garden. My grandmother used to say that one day she would no longer be among us, and that the tree that grew from that seed would have to remind us of her. Although she is gone, in a way she was right, and it comforts me that some of the things we did together back then still endure.

Not long afterwards, just as the walnut tree was putting out its first leaf, my grandmother passed away. In those days, to make her absence easier for us to bear, my mother told us that her spirit had left her old body to begin a new life in the tree that was being born. For my sister and me her words were a great relief that helped us understand the magic of life. Tears gave way to smiles. That tree had to grow up healthy and strong so that my grandmother would be happy in her new home. And the two little girls became faithful gardeners who day after day watered the earth, pruned the branches and fed the roots.

The walnut tree had become our confidant. In the afternoons we would sit by the trunk and tell it about our adventures at school and our mischief in the playground. At night, so that it would not feel lonely, we would put the headphones on it and leave the Walkman playing the poetry of Antonio Machado that my grandmother loved so much, “abunda en la tierra un gris de plomo y azul de plata con manchas de roja herrumbre, todo envuelto en luz violada” (the land abounds in a leaden grey and a silver blue, stained with red rust, all wrapped in violet light) and “olivares coloridos de una tarde anaranjada, olivares rebruñidos bajo la luna argentada” (olive groves coloured by an orange evening, olive groves burnished beneath the silvered moon), impossible to forget those lines. It was a lovely time.

Now I look at you, a tree tall and strong, unlike any of the others. All that was long ago and I know you are not among us, yet I prefer to go on thinking that you still live in there, among its leaves, delving into the earth where we were born. And though you do not answer when I ask whether you remember, I know that you still keep that time in your memory.

― Fátima! Lunch.

― Eh… What?

― Lunch, the table’s laid. Come on.

― Coming.

Ω

― Mmm… This is delicious, Mum. You haven’t lost your touch in the kitchen.

― My dear daughter, I don’t lose things. But you haven’t gained any either since you left. Still getting up late, beds unmade, and plenty of other habits you’ve kept intact.

― Mother, I’m visiting. I laid the table, what more do you want when Fátima does the rest.

― You’re incorrigible, I dread to think what your own place looks like.

― Spotless. That’s what I pay a woman for -Nadia declared.

― Well, and you, how are you, Fátima? -asked her father.

― Very well. There’s no shortage of work. Julio and I both have no end of commissions. We’re happy, we can’t complain.

― I’m glad to hear it, and it fills me with joy to have you both here. I feel as though the years hadn’t gone by; incredible as it sounds, I miss arguing with you two.

― I miss you both too, Dad. You can’t imagine how much.

― I saw you sitting in the garden, staring at the walnut tree, lost in thought.

― I was remembering old times, when Grandma was still here and we planted the walnut.

― That was a good many years ago, Fátima, you must have been little girls.

― Of course they were little girls, Gregorio! That tree has been planted there for more than twenty years.

― I don’t remember that either -added Nadia.

― I was six and you were three. No wonder you don’t remember, you never even remember to clear the table.

― If in twenty-three years I haven’t done the bare minimum in this house, it’s precisely because remarks like that wash right over me.

― You’re a lazybones, if you’ll pardon my saying so, my girl.

― Mother, we are what we are. And that little flaw of mine is made up for with money, but today, since the cleaner’s not here, I’ll clear my own glass and plate and leave the rest in your hands. I’ll be upstairs having my siesta, don’t disturb me -Nadia went up the stairs. The slam of the door meant she would not be leaving her room until after six.

― That girl, eh! No respect for anyone -Gregorio exclaimed.

― Now I understand why all you ever wanted was to get out of here.

― Let’s not talk about that now, Mother. I’ve come to spend a few days with you both, don’t remind me of anything that’ll make me run for it.

― Fair enough.

The three of them finished lunch. Once the table was cleared they sat on the sofa in front of the telly, where a documentary about wild animals on the African plains was on. Gregorio had been dozing for some time, his deafening snores rattling the walls. Fátima lay in her mother’s lap, the two of them watching closely as the lions stalked the zebras, WHAAAM!, the cats pounced on them in a flash, the din of the hunt setting mother and daughter’s hair on end.

― Goodness! What a beautiful sight -said Montserrat.

― Yes, it’s lovely. Lovely enough to make you think that while the lionesses do the work, the lion is lying in the sun snoring, like your husband.

― Hush, my girl. Unless you want it to be me and not you who runs out of this house never to return.

― Do you know, Mother? That is a mystery I have never managed to solve.

― Don’t go looking for an answer. Count yourself lucky to have inherited my genes and not your father’s, don’t take up with anyone like him, and avoid having children unless you want them to turn out like your sister -Fátima nodded with a mocking grimace-. By the way, going back to the walnut tree. Do you remember what your grandmother used to tell you?

― Of course I remember. She told us that the wood is a magical place where wonderful beings live, and that we should respect nature more than anything in this world.

― True, she always said that, but that part belongs to the end of the story.

― Actually I remember another walnut too. But nothing else.

― And do you remember where that walnut is? -said her mother, turning her gaze towards the cupboard. Fátima looked without knowing where in that enormous piece of furniture to search.

― Ding… ding… ding… -a little bell tinkled between Montserrat’s fingers.

― Oh! I remember that bell, it was Grandma’s.

― Exactly. Your grandmother gave it to you, it had been put away for years along with the walnut.

― Ahm… Now it’s coming back to me. We kept them in that old tin with the old-fashioned pictures so they wouldn’t get lost. We were supposed to hang the little bell from the tree that grew from the walnut we planted and ring it to call the dwellers of the wood. But I don’t remember what the walnut was for.

― The walnut is a token of identity. By it the dwellers of the wood recognise the guardian.

― And what is the guardian supposed to do?

― Supposedly the dwellers live among us, in these hills, behind every tree, though we can’t see them. If the wood is ever threatened by human greed, the guardian will be the one to raise the alarm so that the animals can flee and find a safe place.

― And do you think Grandma was guardian of the wood?

― I don’t believe in those tales, Fátima, they were only stories for little girls. But to answer your question, I doubt it; in fact I never saw her do anything out of the ordinary, or plant any walnut tree other than the one she sowed with you, or make use of the little bell. She simply respected nature and wanted to pass those values on to her granddaughters through stories, nothing more.

― And what better way, Mum, than fables to make little flowers grow up along the right path. In any case, now that all that past has come back to life, I’ll hang the little bell in the branches of the walnut tree in Grandma’s honour.

If it’s true they mean to raze the wood and the hills so the city can keep growing, the dwellers of the woodland had better know as soon as possible and find themselves somewhere safe. I can’t see any lawyer defending the interests of a single squirrel or deer in the face of such juicy human plunder.

― Hang it up if you like, but at night I want it tied fast. I put up with your father while I’m awake, I’m not going to put up with tinkling while I doze as well.

― All right, it’ll be tied up at night. I’ll go and hang it now.

― Do as you please, but quietly. I’ll try to drop off before Gregorito wakes up -Fátima kissed her mother and slipped out into the garden.

The little bell swayed in the breeze. The young woman watched its rhythm, lying under the tree. Calm. The whisper of the pines kept her company as she fell asleep. Silence. The scorching heat of the afternoon. Something damp and rough brushed her skin and woke her. Beside her was Matías, an enormous black cat with amber eyes, dimmed by the more than fifteen years he carried.

― What’s up? I looked for you this morning when I arrived.

― Meowww… -was the only answer, and after rubbing himself against the whole length of her body, he took a few steps and settled in the wicker armchair from which he kept watch over the entire garden.

― What a layabout you’ve turned into -the cat replied with a haughty look-. All right, all right, I never said a word. Let it not be said that anyone in this family does more than is strictly necessary -she said, teasing.

Ω

It was past six in the evening. She went into the house; her father was still asleep. She climbed the stairs without a sound; the door ajar, at the desk, her mother, absorbed, was writing in a notebook.

― Mum.

― Mmm…

― Is it a sad one or a happy one? -asked the young woman, hugging her from behind.

― A happy one.

― Will you read it to me? -the woman cleared her throat and in a firm voice recited the lines.

Quiero amplitud de horizontes,
(I want breadth of horizons,)
oteros inalcanzables,
(hilltops out of reach,)
quiero aleteo de palomas,
(I want the flutter of doves,)
no el sonido monocorde
(not the single-note sound)
de las campanas que tañen.
(of the bells that toll.)
Quiero praderas de hierba,
(I want meadows of grass,)
barrancos y pedregales,
(ravines and stony ground,)
bosques, cerros, montes, lomas,
(woods, hills, mountains, ridges,)
campo y campo interminable.
(country and country without end.)
Que nadie rece en voz baja
(Let no one murmur low)
una plegaria agobiante.
(a smothering prayer.)
Que el silencio que me ciña,
(Let the silence that girds me)
sólo el alba lo reclame.
(be claimed by the dawn alone.)
Y al alba mi alma paloma
(And at dawn let my dove of a soul)
levante el vuelo y taladre
(take wing and pierce)
los espacios amarillos
(the yellow spaces)
que al infinito se abren.
(that open on the infinite.)
Si alcanzo la libertad,
(If I attain freedom,)
quiero ser aire, en el aire.
(I want to be air, in the air.)

― It’s a beautiful poem, you haven’t lost an ounce of your talent. Dad and Nadia are asleep. Carry on writing, I’ll see to hanging out the washing and sweeping the yard.

― Thank you, love -with a kiss on the cheek, the young woman left the room.

Ω

Fátima was already far away by the time the racket started up in her house. She was sitting on the brow of Monte Colorao, from where the valley could be seen between the mountains. The country houses, each far from the next, the city, and growing between the two, the industrial units and the housing estates.

It was Saturday, and the machines stood idle, but over a fortnight’s holiday the noise of those monsters would soon make itself heard. Three years had passed since her last visit, and although inside the house everything seemed the same, the surroundings where she had spent her childhood and adolescence were much changed. Resigned before the sight of what had once been a lovely wood.

Ω

Once again the four of them were together, having supper, chatting away. And as usual, after supper, it was time to gather in front of the television. The celebrity gossip show that Montse and Gregorio liked so much, watching minor celebrities flay one another alive. Meanwhile Nadia, absent, browsed on her laptop; Fátima, lying beside her mother, read Blasco Ibáñez. Almost unheard of, beyond belief, that so rowdy a family could keep silent for a single minute. It was as though the television set plunged them into the deepest concentration.

― Ting… Ting…

― Fátima, there goes your little bell. Take it off the tree before it rings again.

― Blimey, what ears you’ve got.

― By day, in this house, there’s shouting, cursing, scolding, drum-banging and the cock crowing. But by night, not even your father snores in here -she said, roaring with laughter.

― I’ll take it down right now.

Fátima went out into the yard. The breeze barely stirred the leaves. The little bell hung silent. “Ting… Ting…” Curious, she went up to it. Suddenly, among the leaves, a magpie appeared, trying to make off with the shiny metal; she stepped aside.

― Hey! What are you doing awake at this hour?

The bird, startled by her presence, flew off while she followed it with her eyes until it was lost in the night. Matías was sitting in the armchair; she crouched beside him.

― I’m going to leave the bell hanging there. If the magpie comes back, give it a fright before it rings it again, or Mum will come down in a fury and make a stew out of you and me -the cat gave her an indolent look, accepting his fate without choice.

Before long the lights were going out; everyone was in bed. The moon shone high in the heavens, lighting up the night. “Meow… meow…” Fátima, half asleep, opened the balcony door to let the cat in; like a shot he slid between the sheets and nestled in her warmth, as on so many summer nights. They both fell fast asleep.

They had been asleep a couple of hours when the cat’s yowling woke her. She half opened her eyes and, dragging herself across the sheets, poked her head over the far side of the bed to find Matías cowering among the flowerpots, his eyes wide open. Puzzled, she went out onto the balcony, searching in the direction he was looking; silence; she looked from side to side, nothing, and yet Matías went on trembling.

― There’s nothing there that I can see… -she said, bending to pick him up-. Don’t be scared, no cat-eating fox is getting into this house, easy now.

Just as she laid her hands on his back, the cat leapt at her, digging in his claws, scrambling up her, “MEOWWW!”; thrown off balance, she spun round; a shadow the size of a child crossed the garden and vanished under the walnut tree; she hurried inside and shut the door. The cat was hiding under the bed.

― Let’s think about this for a moment, Matías. Foxes don’t climb trees and that was very big, there aren’t many lynxes round here, I really am a bit confused -the cat was still cowering-. But let’s not be taken in by night-time fears: either I give you paper and a pencil and you draw me a sketch of what you saw, or I switch on the outside light and we find the intruder -the cat curled up even tighter-. Right… we’re agreed, I switch on the light and we look from behind the window.

Fátima crept to the landing and switched on the yard lights. She looked out, shielding herself with the curtains: everything lit up, nothing there, and hardly a corner to hide in. Peering uneasily, stillness, she switched off the light and kept watching, one last look in case the animal showed itself again, nothing. She lay down on the bed. Her arm hung down, stroking the cat, who was still frightened.

― Easy, you wally, it’s gone -she said, teasing.

“THUD THUD THUD!”, footsteps sounded on the roof. Fátima jumped off the bed in fright. “Ting… Ting…” “Fuck!”, she blurted. She waited, still, silent, not daring to go near the window. Slowly, noiselessly, she got into bed, took Matías in her arms, and the two of them stayed like that, looking at each other, uneasy. Nothing more was heard, and the hours of waiting gave way to sleep.

Ω

The sun was warming her face; it was eleven in the morning, Matías was not beside her, the windows were open, Montserrat was singing as she watered the plants. From the back came the strained voices of father and daughter, arguing, as always, over what colour to paint the roof.

Fátima went down to the yard, came up to the walnut tree and studied it. She walked to the farthest corner of the garden and looked at the roof; everything normal; nobody said anything. “Ting… Ting…”, she turned towards the little bell, and her eyes met her mother’s.

― Listen, Gregorita, I told you yesterday to tie up that bell -Fátima gave her a sidelong look, disapproving of the nickname her mother liked to call her by.

― Did you hear it last night…?

― No, I heard nothing, but if it had woken me, I swear you’d have gone flying out along with it.

― Don’t worry, Mother. I won’t forget tonight.

Ω

The afternoon was wearing on. Nadia was lying on the divan in the porch, half dozing. The NeverEnding Story was on television. Fátima came in and, sitting down beside her, asked.

― Hey!

― Mmm…

― Did you hear anything last night?

― Anything like what?

― The little bell.

― No.

― And Matías running across the roof?

― No.

― Right… -she fell thoughtful, confused, her mind wandering.

― Listen…

― Mmm…

― Are you all right?

― Yes.

― What are you thinking about?

― …Nothing…

― I know you, you’re drawing some connection between the Auryn and the little bell.

― No… what rubbish you talk.

― Fátima -she pronounced, with a look that settled it.

― …Well, yes. Something like that.

― Are you by any chance wondering whether Grandma’s stories are true?

― Not exactly.

― I don’t remember any of her stories, but I very much doubt Grandma is inside the tree. That was only to make it easier for us to bear losing her.

― I know. But I don’t mean that in particular, Nadia, I’m talking about the little bell.

― I don’t follow.

― I still remember the part of the story that went like this: “on summer nights, when in utter stillness the wind whistles stealthily, the bell that hangs from the branches will ring, opening the portal between the two worlds”.

― PFFF! You’re tripping. How can you think that? You’re not right in the head, girl.

― … -a furtive glance.

― Forgive me, but I can’t quite see what makes you come up with such a thing. You’re hiding something, tell me what you saw.

― Last night Matías woke me, he was more frightened than I’ve ever seen him. He was staring at something I couldn’t make out, the shadow of something very big, the little bell rang, I switched on the outside light, I saw nothing. I went back to bed, wary, then something very heavy ran across the roof, it stopped and the bell rang again, and I didn’t dare do more.

― Well… I didn’t notice a thing -she fell thoughtful-. If it happens again tonight, tell me. It’s not that I believe in goblins, but I wouldn’t like to run into something nasty in the garden.

― I’m confused, I don’t know what to think.

― Nonsense, Fátima, don’t let your imagination run away with you. Tell me if it happens again.

― All right. I’m going for a ride now.

― I’m staying here…

Fátima went out to the yard. She got on the bike and stopped in front of the bell, striking it, “Ting… Ting…”, and the tree shuddered from top to bottom, shaking its leaves. Frightened, she began to pedal, losing herself along the lanes she had ridden so many times long ago.

Ω

It was summer and the houses were full, people coming and going, children shrieking everywhere; with so many people about it was stupid to think that such a large animal could prowl unseen. So much sensible reasoning that in the end it turned into a prank by one of those children, or a rise in overweight squirrels; to think something like that was the most practical course for a woman of twenty-seven capable of telling reality from fancy.

Ω

Some hours had passed since sunset. Fátima was arriving home. Inside were a couple of neighbours with her parents; she preferred not to go in. Nadia was upstairs.

She dropped onto the divan in the porch; habaneras by María Dolores Pradera were playing softly. Lying there, waiting for them to leave, she closed her eyes, her mind a blank. “Ting… Ting…” Fátima had lost all sense of the moment; she lay motionless. The women’s voices could be heard in the next room. “Ting… THUD, THUD, THUD.” The windows flew open.

― Fátima, Fátima!!

Startled, she leapt up and ran into the yard. Her mother and the neighbours were coming out of the door. Nadia was leaning out, beside herself, her eyes fixed on the walnut tree.

― What’s the matter, love? What’s all this shouting?

― Fátima… the… the tree… -they all looked at the walnut tree, puzzled.

― Did you see it, Nadia?

― Yes!

― What are you two talking about?

― Er… There’s a fox about and it got into the garden -Fátima replied.

― And you think a trifle like that is worth all this screaming, my girl?

― … -she did not know what to say.

― It’s all right, Mum. Nadia, go back inside. Don’t be afraid, it runs off when it sees people.

― That’s the first I’ve heard of it, dear -said one of the neighbours.

― Well, that’s all it was, you may see it among the rubbish. I’ll go and see how Nadia is, I’ll leave you to it -answered Fátima, going upstairs.

― Goodness… what a fright your daughter gave me, as if she’d seen a ghost.

― What can I say… So long in the city must have made them forget the country is full of animals.

― Nadia, are you all right?

― I saw it, Fátima.

― What did you see?

― It was big and fast. That was no fox.

― What is it?

― I don’t know, I only saw something go past the window. I opened it quickly, but it had already vanished among the leaves of the walnut tree. I don’t know what it could be, it was dark, it was very fast.

― Did you hear the bell?

― Several times, but I paid no attention until I heard the footsteps.

― My theory seems to be gaining ground.

― Don’t talk rubbish.

― So… we’ve got a chimpanzee in the neighbourhood?

― Not that, but nothing to do with Grandma’s stories either. There’s no interdimensional portal…

― I’m not saying that… But what if the bell lets us see creatures that are otherwise invisible to the human eye?

― You can’t be thinking about what you’re saying. Until yesterday you didn’t remember a single one of Grandma’s stories, how can you come out with such nonsense?

― I know it’s mad… Anyway, let’s leave it. I’m going to my room.

Ω

The hours went by; it was night, their parents were asleep. The young woman played solitaire, unable to sleep, thinking it over; she understood nothing, that sensation, the bell, those shadows; she tried to recall more details of the story.

Every time it rang, the creatures prowled around the tree. If the tinkling was meant to warn them of some immense danger, why, instead of fleeing, did they appear among them…? Perhaps they want to speak to the guardian… The walnut… I must present myself carrying it… She took the seed out of the tin, tied it on a string and hung it round her neck. After what had happened it seemed feasible. She crossed the landing to her sister’s room; madness or not, she was determined to go out into the yard, walnut at the ready, and ring the little bell, but too afraid to do it alone. She knocked gently, no answer, opened slowly; in the dark, Nadia was posted at the window, watching the wood.

― What are you doing there? -she said, coming closer. Nadia was holding an enormous torch.

― Be quiet and look.

The full moon let the flat land show clearly through the branches. Everything drawn in greys. Stillness. Silence.

― I can’t see anything…

― Wait… -she answered without blinking-. There, do you see it?

― Yes.

Something big was running at full speed through the trees, heading for the house.

― Blimey! It’s coming this way.

― It’s running away from something, look over there at the back.

― It’s bloody enormous!

― It’s a chase. Switch on the torch and shine it in its eyes -Nadia was trying to switch the thing on.

― Come on!

― I can’t, it’s jammed.

The figures were running towards the house. “THUD, THUD, THUD”, the first of them was moving across the roof.

― Give it a good whack! -and with one hard thump Fátima got the torch working.

The light passed through the glass and lit up the face of a colossal beast. Its dazzled eyes met those of the girls.

― OOOOHH!!

Blinded, it fell to the ground and vanished among the shadows. The sisters were stunned, petrified, flattened against the wall on the far side of the room.

― What’s going on? What’s all this shouting? -Montserrat was opening the door. At the sound of their mother’s voice both recovered their composure. Fátima quickly lowered the blind.

― Nothing’s going on -she answered, shaken.

― Are you sure you’re all right? You don’t sound very convinced, look at the faces on the pair of you.

― …Aah… yes… we were watching a horror film, that’s all -said Nadia.

― I see… well, if that’s all, I hope you won’t wake me again for the rest of the night, unless you want me to be the one who gives you a fright, understood?

― Yes, Mum -they answered in unison.

Montserrat went back to bed. Alone again.

― That was very big to be what I think I saw.

― Very big… Yes.

― Big… And it went up the wall, it’s probably somebody in a costume -Fátima concluded.

― I don’t believe in the story, but that was no costume.

― All right. Whatever it is, I have to find out. I’ll ring the bell with the walnut round my neck, they’ll come, and recognising me as the guardian, we’ll talk -she said, heading down the stairs.

― You’re mad, Fátima! Where are you going with that thing out there?

― You stay here, I’m going to talk to them.

― I’m not letting you go out. It’s dangerous.

― Ting… Ting…

Fátima ran down the stairs. Nadia, behind her, stopped her from opening the door.

― I’m convinced by the story now, but let’s not do anything stupid, we’re too old not to think.

From behind the curtains the two of them watched the walnut tree; no sign of the creatures. They shut the windows, locked the door and drew the curtains before going back to the bedroom. They lay on the bed, nervous, watchful.

― What are we supposed to do now? What else did Grandma’s story say?

― Mmm… I don’t remember that much. That the wood is full of animals hidden from man and that the guardian must warn them of danger so they can flee is all very well as a story, but this is beyond anything I expected. I still can’t believe what we saw.

― Fátima, do you think we’re mad?

― Nobody in this house has ever been sane, but I don’t think we hallucinate in tandem. Tomorrow we’ll put an end to this story.

As they talked, exhausted, they gradually fell asleep.

Ω

Well into the morning, shouting began to be heard.

― For heaven’s sake, how can this be? You tell me, Montse, who amuses themselves breaking other people’s things? Bloody hell, honestly. Breaking the aerial, of all things. It was your daughters. Wake them up and tell them to come down and explain this disaster to me.

― You, Gregorio, must be soft in the head. How are your daughters going to climb onto the roof and bend the aerial pole, honestly? How did I ever come to marry a man like you?

― Stop telling me off and tell those two to come down -Montse shot him a stern look, held herself back and burst into unhinged laughter. In the bedroom her daughters were asleep in bed.

― Fátima. Nadia. Wake up. Your father is waiting for you downstairs to make a scene.

― Mmm… -both half opened their eyes-. Why?

― Apparently, according to your father, we have daughters who are fond of bending aerials.

― Us?

― It must have been last night… -said Nadia without thinking.

― What exactly must have been? -Fátima thumped her sister under the sheets.

― Ahm… Yesterday, messing about, I threw a shoe in the air, it got caught on the aerial, and while I was trying to get it back with the broom, it happened. I was going to fix it now.

― I see -she looked at them sternly-. At nearly thirty that kind of nonsense is out of place, but there’ll be no punishment. Go down, sit through the lecture, and when he finishes, smother him in kisses until he shuts up. My head is fit to burst.

― Yes, Mum.

Their father ranted at the top of his lungs as he straightened the metal, repeating himself over and over. Useless to try to explain.

― Now that I’ve fixed it, you’ll go and break it again. Big girls like you… Eh!

― We’re very sorry, Dad -they said, hugging and kissing him.

― No, no, no, don’t you go kissing me, I’m none too pleased with you. I’m off to the village to buy the paper and do the pools, I’ll be back in time for lunch. Goodbye.

― Bye, Dad.

― What a torment that man is, I thought he’d never stop -Nadia complained.

― It’s one of Dad’s charms.

The girls were left in the yard; this was the moment. Fátima held the walnut in one hand and rang the little bell, “Ting… Ting…”; a soft breeze shook the tree. It tinkled again; something was moving among the tall pines that fenced the garden, “Tt, Tt, Tt”, the chattering of a squirrel. They were peering through the branches when, with a quick movement, half a body burst from the foliage; they stepped back in surprise. “Tt, Tt, Tt”, the squirrel must have stood at least one metre twenty, its features slightly human, like a smiling little boy.

Fátima held the walnut up high. The animal came closer, took the walnut and hung it round her neck.

― Tt, Tt, Ttnow we can understand each other -said the squirrel.

― He… Hello! -they stared, dumbfounded.

― My name is Urure. What’s yours?

― Fátima.

― And her?

― My sister Nadia.

― Why are you telling the squirrel our names?

― I can understand him with the walnut round my neck.

― Well, translate, I want to know what he’s saying -and so the elder did.

― You must be the guardian, isn’t that right?

― Not exactly. It was my grandmother who left this for us, but we thought it was a fable.

― Your grandmother… she must have been the little girl our elders talk about. It happened before I was born; she gave the warning and saved us from the machines. Talon, my big brother, saw her when he was very small, but this is the first time I’ve seen humans.

― You’re in danger again, the wood is going to be cleared. Raise the alarm among your people and flee.

― I don’t think that will be possible…

― Why not?

― Well… because the crossing that opened in front of me, the one I came through, is a forbidden link that nobody may pass except the three Corons. There are many doors between your world and mine, but they are all sealed; only the guardian can open them from this side.

― Then go back.

― …I would if I could… Butmmm… after going back to my world the second time, I ran into Valiora; desperate to escape, I came back to this world and he followed me. Last night he nearly caught me, I tripped over that branch -he said, pointing at the aerial- and fell, but something scared him off and I managed to hide. I’ve tried to go back, but he’s standing guard by the crossing.

― How can we get you home?

― Use the bell.

― I’m not even the official guardian, I don’t know how to open doors, it was the wind that did it by chance; otherwise I’d open the one that leads straight to your bosses.

“Crack”, a branch snapped on the other side of the fence, a deep breath, the three of them frozen. The shadow moved along behind the wall of cypresses. The squirrel, shaken, hid behind the girls.

― It’s Valiora… -he stammered.

― I am the guardian, he won’t harm us -she said, chest puffed out, though not very convinced.

The figure was approaching, the gate open. Nadia edged towards the door of the house, the squirrel behind her. Fátima stayed where she was.

― I don’t like this one bit -said the younger.

― I am the guardian of the wood! -Fátima stammered.

With a shove the creature slid the gate aside, revealing its whole body. It looked about with curiosity.

― The wood is in danger, you must warn your people -the animal laughed soundlessly-. Didn’t you hear me? Raise the alarm among your kind.

The fox rose to his full height, towering over two metres.

― Give me the squirrel -he said in a shrill voice, advancing menacingly.

― Stop! I am the guardian.

― Hee, hee, hee -he cut his laughter short, withered the young woman with a look, and like lightning ran at her. In a rush, “SLAAAM”, they got into the house, leaving the creature outside, safe for a moment.

― What’s going on out there? -called their mother from the kitchen at the sound of the door slamming.

Quickly they drew the curtains and covered the squirrel with a tablecloth. Montse put her head round the door.

― You’re big girls now for all this commotion -the two took the blame in silence; their mother went back inside.

Hidden behind the curtains they watched; the fox was still in the yard.

― Why did he attack me if he’s one of you?

― Valiora is a renegade, he doesn’t keep the laws of the wood and that’s why he was cast out.

― Are there more like him? -asked the elder.

― There are three renegades. Zebuj, the falcon, Otoke, the viper, and Valiora, the fox. They live in the arid zone; if they set foot in the walnut wood, the wrath of the Corons would fall on them.

― And you, what were you doing in the arid zone?

― I was in the safe zone, it was he who stole into the wood to hunt. They can slip past any guard; in the old days they were the sappers, charged with living among men unseen to find out their movements.

When food began to run short because our borders were shrinking, they rebelled and wanted to devour the humans. After being punished, they lost their minds and tried to feed on our own people, and in the end they were banished. That is why the elders turned to a human girl as a link between the two worlds.

― Fátima, the fox is still there. We have to find some way of making him disappear before somebody discovers him.

― Urure, go out the back door and warn your people.

― All right, I’ll warn them and bring reinforcements.

Nadia stealthily opened the door and the squirrel slipped away. The fox did not notice. In the distance appeared a horde of motocross bikes followed by cars with electronic music blaring. The beast seemed agitated; the sound, closer and closer, was becoming unbearable; they were passing right in front when the fox, at the end of his tether, fled and hid in the undergrowth. Their father’s car appeared behind them.

― Young people today! They’ll go soft in the head with that music so loud, it’s unbearable, to be this far away and still hear them -Gregorio was saying as the girls bundled him into the house.

Valiora had vanished, but he was still among them.

Ω

The afternoon passed without alarm, giving way to a stifling night. The houses kept silent and the bell was not heard even once. The sisters had stayed alert for as long as they were awake, but the calm let them sleep in peace.

Ω

Montserrat was rummaging in the larder and did not seem to find what she was looking for. She went up to the bedroom; the two sisters were sleeping together.

― Fátima -she whispered.

― Mmm…?

― I’m short of a couple of things for lunch, please go into the village. Your father is sorting out the box room.

― Yes, Mum.

While she was dressing, the younger one woke up.

― Wait, Fátima, I’ll come with you.

― I’ll go and start the car.

The car rolled slowly along the lane. Nadia leaning against the window, eyes half closed, drowsy. The golden field, the tall ears of wheat, something moving swiftly through the thick of it; Nadia, bewildered, woke up and watched intently a red blur bending the crop as it went.

― Fátima, step on it!!!

Valiora was running after them, closing the distance, hurling himself at the car, striking the rear.

― Fuck, fuck! Faster!

With a wrench of the wheel she straightened up and floored it, winning the race. They looked in the mirror; Valiora was giving up, disappearing into the undergrowth.

― That was close.

― We’ve become his prey, Nadia.

― Do you think he’ll hurt Dad, Mum or anybody else?

― We helped Urure and that’s why he’s attacking us. I think he’s afraid of the Corons; otherwise he’d have had half the neighbourhood for lunch.

The two were now walking the aisles of the supermarket, both turning over what had happened and what was to come.

― My guess is that Valiora wants to finish off Urure and us so he can roam this world as he pleases, far from the Corons.

― To prevent that, we’ll come in by the west road, and once home we’ll shelter indoors and wait for Urure to bring reinforcements.

― I’ve thought of something to drive him off if he gets too close. We’ll stop at the pet shop first.

They drove home by the road that skirted the mountain. No sign of the fox. They reached the house. There were neighbours in the streets, children playing. Everything seemed normal.

It was past four in the afternoon. Both posted at the fence, they scanned the valley.

― Where do you think he is?

― Close, but holding back; he knows Urure will have them come for him.

― What if another of those renegades caught the squirrel? They may not come.

― Don’t be hasty, let’s wait, they must be waiting for nightfall so nobody sees them.

― It’s been more than twenty-four hours, Fátima, we should try to open another portal.

― I don’t know how.

― Trial and error -Nadia went up to the walnut tree and tried striking the metal in different rhythms-. There ought to be some signal, like in the car, that says “door open” -after a good while she gave up-. I surrender.

― Let’s go inside. It’s going to be a long night.

Ω

The afternoon began to cool; Fátima was watching the walnut tree from the window, the breeze blowing. “Ting… Ting…” She went out onto the balcony; something in the air smelt different. Nadia appeared beside her.

― Do you feel it?

― Yes, it’s cold.

― You’re an idiot. I mean do you notice anything odd.

― Mmm… it smells… it smells of the hills. Like when the dogs used to come down from the mountain, wild.

― Ow! -cried the elder. Something had hit her on the head; she looked around.

Urure was on the roof; behind him a hare, a hedgehog, a lynx and another, bigger squirrel were peeping out.

― What are you doing up there? Anyone could see you, even Valiora. Get inside -the seven of them went into the bedroom. They drew the curtains-. I thought you weren’t coming.

― Valiora was lying in wait at the crossing, so we had to spread out among the other ways through in the safe zone, hoping one would open. During the afternoon the door Gobago was watching opened, and here we are.

― But you’re children, where are your grown-ups? -Nadia declared, indignant.

― Wait… -said the squirrel, hanging a walnut round her neck-. Now we’ll be able to understand each other.

― Where are the Corons? Somebody who can take Valiora away from here and raise the alarm among your people.

― We are many and brave. We’ll drive off the fox and save the wood, and make our parents proud of us.

― And what’s the plan? -asked Fátima.

― We’ll put the plan into action at nightfall. Right now we want you to show us your world -said the lynx. The girls were struck dumb, flabbergasted.

― …

― I don’t believe this.

― Stay here. Don’t move, don’t make a noise, don’t open the door. We’ll be back when everyone’s asleep.

The sisters went downstairs. Montse was reading Madame Bovary. In the background “Los Panchos” were playing; Gregorio hummed along as he filled in a puzzle.

― Fátima. Nadia. It’s the village fiesta, we haven’t been as a family since you were little. It starts at nine tonight. What do you say we go?

― I don’t think that’s a good idea, Mother… -said Fátima.

― Of course it is! We’ll go into the village and we’ll treat you to dinner at the hotel. You dance, then the spa, then bed. All paid for by us -Nadia declared.

― Actually I’d like us all to go together, I see your father every day as it is.

― Well, I think that for once the girls are paying for something, we should accept. We haven’t done anything like it since our wedding night. It’ll be wonderful -Gregorio affirmed.

― That’s settled, then. Dad, Mum. Get dressed and let’s go -Nadia exclaimed.

The streets were in full fiesta. The night lit up with colours, music and joy, people in fancy dress enjoying the street parades. The four of them relished those hours as a family.

― That’s it. It’s eleven, time for dinner, we’ll walk you to the hotel.

― Are you sure you don’t want to come and eat?

― Thanks, we’ll eat at home. Enjoy the evening. Dad, Mum.

― See you tomorrow, girls.

― Improvised, but fine. We’ve got tonight and half the morning to sort out this mess.

― That was very quick-witted of you, Nadia. Bravo.

― I hope all this is over soon, I’m sick of this story. I’m not even going to tell it to my grandchildren, I’ll chop down the walnut tree and that’s that.

Ω

They drove back. The road was clear. The neighbours were enjoying the night, dining outdoors.

Once home, they waited before getting out of the car. The lantern lit the yard; nobody in sight. Quickly they went in and locked the door. Not seeing him did not mean Valiora was not somewhere near.

They went into the bedroom. A host of green eyes watched them in the dark. The bedside lamp lit the walls.

― We can’t waste time. Go home and warn your people -Fátima declared.

― Nooo!!… Please… Take us to see the coloured stars. We want to see your world.

― They’re not stars, they’re fireworks. The village is holding its fiesta, too many people, it would be dangerously foolish -the five of them gazed at her with pleading eyes-. I said no.

― Nadia, you take us. We’ll hide in the box that moves. Please -they all begged at once.

― I’ve always been very stubborn. I like to satisfy my curiosity at any cost, and mad as this sounds, even coming from me, for you this adventure must be what setting foot on the moon would be for me, so we’re going. We won’t get out of the car and we’ll keep to the outskirts.

― I can’t believe this -she said, open-mouthed.

― Relax, Fátima, everyone’s in fancy dress, nobody will think it odd.

― I hope I don’t regret this.

― There’ll be no trouble. They’ll behave, won’t you?

― Yes.

The car made its way through the streets. The youngsters cried out in wonder, their faces pressed against the windows.

― OOOHH, how pretty!! So many lights, so many people, how tall, how big. What’s that, and what’s that, and that one over there? -one by one, Fátima and Nadia answered the questions.

― What’s it like to live here? -asked the squirrel.

― It’s strange. It’s a world made by men and for men, stripped of nature. Capable of finishing off the human spirit. Only for cosmopolitans -the youngsters did not know what to make of that.

― What Fátima means is that the city is for the people who are born in it. Here you breathe a grey air, a murky atmosphere, a feeling that makes us ill. The pace is fast, overbearing, suffocating. The city smothers anyone without ambition, without success, it’s no good being just one more, people devour one another, there’s no compassion, no sense of the group.

Here there is no such thing as peace, happiness is never whole. It is a place where free souls slowly perish -the youngsters seemed confused, even frightened.

― Let’s go back to the wood, we don’t feel like staying here any more. I don’t like this place -said the hare, and they all carried the motion-. I think I’m starting to feel unwell.

They headed back. The city was no more than a swarm of lights in the distance. The car pulled up in the yard. Stillness.

― Where is the crossing you came in by?

― Inside the boxwood -he said, pointing to the shrub growing in the planter.

Fátima scanned the area. She got out of the car and went round to the back to open it. A repeated clicking. “AAAAAHH!” The youngsters were screaming inside. The fox had his hands on the bonnet, his eyes boring into the car.

― Go away, Valiora. There are too many of them, they’ll be missed and someone will come. There’s nothing you can do.

She backed away from the car. The fox watched her menacingly. Nadia rummaged in the glove compartment, wound down the window and threw something at her sister’s feet; Fátima picked it up and blew, “Peeeeeeeeeeeeeeep!!!”, a toneless whistle. The creature writhed in pain. Nadia got out of the car blowing another whistle. The sound tormented him; Valiora drew off in a rage. Talon went through the crossing while the little ones and the girls, still blowing, got inside and shut the door behind them.

― You’ll have to come out of there some time. Your parents will come back and I’ll be here waiting for them. Give me those mice, close the crossings with me on this side, and all will be at peace.

― Never, Valiora! Talon will come back with the Corons.

― I’m afraid not, guardian. While you were out I found the way in and had Otoke posted on the other side. But don’t fret: in exchange for those children, your parents’ lives.

― No, Valiora -she snapped-, and that’s my last word.

The fox charged hard at the glass, outraged, and prowled round the house working out how to get in.

― We can’t have him still there when Dad and Mum get back. Think of something.

― This has got far too complicated, Nadia. That bell has to ring for the Corons to come. I only hope my idea works. Get the barbiturates from the medicine cabinet, I’ll put the lamb that’s in the fridge on to cook.

The meat was ready. Fátima went up to the door; the fox was keeping restless watch in front of it.

― Valiora, I’ve nothing against you, I’m not even the guardian. All I want is to warn your people, this valley and its mountains are in danger. If you stay here, the machines will raze your world; you must look for a new wood and survive wherever humans can’t reach.

― That is not the point, child. Without woods, there will be nowhere to set up parallels. Our world needs vast stretches of land to shelter us; it is the magic of the Corons that hides us from you.

Once, a spy on human schemes. Today, an exile of more than sixty years. I don’t want to fight you, I don’t want to eat your flesh. Absurd. I only want to be rid of my people, to survive in this world eating carrion and rubbish. I prefer that to raging with hunger, scorned by my own kind in the arid zone. I only want to be as free as my surroundings allow, answering to no one -the sisters were moved.

― I’m sorry, Valiora -said Fátima-. I understand your reasons, but it’s not a decision for you or me to make. Your place is not among men; if they found you, they’d end up hunting you down. Your world and mine have split apart beyond repair.

I know you’re hungry. I’ll drop the meat out of the window, eat it if you like. Sooner or later the Corons will come.

The fox fell to brooding. The group waited indoors. The air seemed calmer. At last the beast devoured the meat and withdrew under the walnut tree. Fátima watched him. A little over an hour had passed when the sleeping pills took effect. She went out into the yard and carefully shook the fox; he was fast asleep.

The six of them dragged the beast to the pool’s underground pump room, down the steps, and the heavy metal door was padlocked shut.

― Talon hasn’t brought the Corons. I’m afraid he’s trapped. Urure, take one of your friends and go back through the crossing you came by the first time. We’ll stay with two of you. We’ll ring the bell and comb the area for new portals. We can’t delay any longer.

― Agreed. Kitar and I will go. Gobago and Mileva will stay with you. I promise to bring our elders -said the squirrel before vanishing with the lynx.

― You go with Gobago. I’ll go with Mileva. I won’t stop ringing the bell and we’ll keep each other in sight. Let’s take our phones. There are still hours before Mum and Dad get back.

― Agreed.

Dawn was showing its first light. The two were already walking far apart. “Ting… Ting…”, it rang again and again, trying to hit upon the right tune. The animals tracked back and forth in search of any portal.

Ω

Past midday. Their parents got out of the car. The garden seemed quiet. The table was laid and lunch ready to serve. There was a note on the hall sideboard; Montserrat read it.

― Your daughters have gone for a walk. Shall we eat or wait for them?

― I’m hungry. Let’s sit down.

Ω

Farther and farther from home, the valley behind them, they were reaching the flat land where the factories stood. The first of them, a sheep farm.

― Beep… Beep… Beep… Yes?

― This isn’t getting us anywhere, Fátima. We’ve covered more than half the wood. Dad and Mum will be home by now -she said, seeing her sister in the distance-. I’ll go as far as the farm and then we head back.

― Fine, I’ll turn back then -she was putting the phone away when the little bell slipped from her hands and rolled off, jingling.

Nadia climbed onto the fence. Behind her the bleating of the sheep, and before her eyes the immense valley, unmoved.

― Can’t you see anything, Gobago?

― Yes. I see it.

― What do you mean? -she asked, looking at the hedgehog. The animal’s eyes were bulging, staring through the wire-. Is there a crossing in there?

― No…

Nadia looked harder. The sheep were moving about more and more nervously. A pair of yellow eyes was watching them from the other side. She grabbed the hedgehog and backed away. That had to be Otoke, the viper.

― Ruuun!!

The serpent hurled herself at the flock, which, terrified, broke down the gate and fled into the valley. Racing after the pair, the reptile came on fast.

― Fátima, run, run!!

The elder looked into the distance: her sister and the animal, a huge flock of sheep and something in among them. The snake. She began to run towards the house.

The two came together as they ran, out of breath. From far off an enormous pheasant came running and drew level with them.

― Don’t be afraid, climb on my back!

The girls and the hedgehog held on as best they could; the hare ran alongside, drawing away from the snake.

― There’s been a revolt. The sappers have escaped. The Corons are on their way -said the bird.

“THOOM… THOOM…” heavy footfalls coming closer and closer. Out of the depths of the wood burst a giant bear, hurling itself at the reptile, and the two locked in a fierce fight.

― We’ll soon be at the Walnut Tree.

― Who warned you?

― We missed the children when we found that several crossings were open. The Corons took alarm and the search began.

A whistle tore the air. Nadia lost her grip on the wing and fell to the ground. The pheasant wheeled round; Fátima hurriedly helped her up. The falcon was flying above them; stooping like an arrow, he seized the hare, who screamed, pierced by his talons.

― Nooooo!! He’s going to kill her -cried Fátima.

― He won’t kill her, they want to bargain for their freedom with the children’s lives.

Ω

At home all seemed quiet. Gregorio had nodded off, while Montserrat followed a David Attenborough documentary. “And here… the wolves… give chase to the wild boar… while the sow… quietly… hides her young…”

“BANG… BANG…” Somebody was pounding on the metal door by the pool. The mother went out into the yard, advancing uneasily. “Fátima…?” she asked, puzzled, “Nadia?” She put her ear to the door, “BANG, BANG”. She straightened up with a start. She went to the tool shed and fetched a small pellet gun. Coming back, she drew the bolt; a violent shove sent the door sliding. Darkness. A shadow. Montserrat, wild-eyed, fired into the air. Valiora was coming up the steps. Gregorio came out into the yard, startled by the shot, and fell on his backside, paralysed at the sight of the creature.

― Gregorio… -the woman stammered.

“Ting… Ting…” rang the bell. The ground roared, the concrete cracking and splitting the surface apart; before them appeared three moles as big as men.

― Enough, Valiora!

― You are going too far. This is beyond any punishment imaginable -added the second mole.

― Out of my way, Dabún! -snapped the fox.

― Mum, Dad! -the sisters were shouting. The pheasant swooped over and landed beside the house. The girls threw their arms round their parents.

― You are wrong. Carry on like this and all you will find is death. There is no blackmailing the Corons. Stop before somebody gets hurt -the pheasant declared. The falcon settled on the roof with the hare in his talons.

― You don’t understand, Larel! But rest easy, we shall wait for those three old ones to arrive. We have no intention of devouring humans, nor any child of the wood. They are merely our bargaining chip -the viper came up alongside the fox.

“Ting… Ting…”. A blinding beam rose in the garden, an upright door, a muffled explosion, and the three Corons appeared before them. The stag with gigantic antlers, the boar with curled tusks and the owl with great white eyes. All three wrapped in robes. So ancient they looked like dry tree trunks. The silence paralysing.

― Enough, Valiora -the owl pronounced-. Halt this madness that runs to leeward. Stand down, my children. Leave this world and come home. It is not our wish to punish you with death and thin still further our already dwindling line.

― NOOO!! Shetaj. The wood is no longer our home. We will not go back to being crawling things, we will not live on carrion. Humiliated by men, banished by our own people.

Go back to the wood yourselves. Live caged, with no lands to dwell in. If you want us to free the little ones, you must grant us our freedom. Let us roam hidden in the land of men, eat their flocks, their rubbish.

Only that, Coron. No death in exchange for no yoke.

― Blasphemies, Valiora -said the stag-. To roam freely in these parts would mean your death and would reveal our secret. This world is no longer ours. Our people still have a long time left.

― Be silent!!… To die at the hands of men or to be an outcast, what is the difference? We are an extinct people. We did nothing to defend our lands when we could, and now all we can do is flee from men like cockroaches. Tell me! What do you intend? To live for ever…? Your time is running out and your magic grows weaker and weaker. When you perish, the wood will vanish and these children will be left at the mercy of humans. What will they do then?

― Forget it, Valiora -the boar went on-. Right or wrong, your coercion will meet with no favourable answer -the fox growled, outraged, furious-. Stop…

Valiora threw himself on Fátima, his jaws open over her throat.

― No, Valiora!! -cried the stag.

― Leave without us or I kill the girl -the stag raised the sceptre he carried; an intense light broke at its tip.

― Release the girl or there will be no mercy for you.

― Sometimes one must know when the moment has come. Death is only the continuation of the road. And today my road goes on free, among men or among the stars.

Those were his last words, and his jaws closed on the girl. The light blazed, blinding everything. A heavy thud on the ground; the fox lay lifeless, Fátima motionless, unharmed.

― Enough now!! -shouted the boar-. Viper, falcon, obey or follow the path of the fallen.

The two beasts held their gaze steady, calm, half smiling.

― We were happy once. Then forced by man into hiding. Not so long ago, bent to your will, Corons. We live in exile; all of us, one way or another, must die for your absurd obstinacy, but this is not a stubborn death, it is an honourable one. We only go ahead; you too will be joining us shortly. My last wish is not in your power -said the serpent.

Both creatures flung themselves at the Corons. The sceptre shone again. The serpent and the raptor faded, lifeless. The three sappers inert on that barren grey ground. Silence, uncertainty. The breeze blew and the bodies came apart, turned into dry leaves that scattered through the wood.

The crossing opened once more at the great boar’s command.

― Goodbye, Fátima. Goodbye, Nadia -each of the youngsters said farewell to the girls and vanished through the door of light.

― I regret that it has all come to pass in this way, guardian. Your task was to give warning. Nothing more. Grateful we are to you -said the owl.

― Now it is time for us to depart, to return to our home. Now, while we still have somewhere to exist. Soon men will lay waste our lands and we shall be a race doomed to extinction -said the stag.

― Valiora was right. His words are those of a death foretold. With our going, part of the essence of this planet will be lost. But it will not be a loss that stands alone. Without animals or woods, without waters or skies, the human race will pass sentence on its own end -said the boar.

The three shone as they faded, becoming a single light. The voice of the three Corons became one.

― Thank you, guardian, you have fulfilled your task, but think no more of the walnut tree and its bell: there will be no next migration. We shall face our fate, joining our brothers in the infinite. Then we shall all return to dust, the dust from which we came. That distant moment will one day touch the now, and all life will come to its end. Do not forget these words, humans.

The halo disappeared. The ground knitted itself back together and it seemed that nothing had happened there. Everything silent. The family stood motionless, lost in thought.

― I don’t understand this ending…

― There’s nothing to understand, Nadia…

― Mum, Dad. Let’s go inside. You know… it all began the day I hung the little bell on the walnut tree…

Montserrat Fernández

The Fisherman of the Tides

The sea grew angrier by the minute, the boats rowed hard to reach the shore, pitch-dark night under storm clouds, the men of the village waited with lanterns, showing the fishermen where the coast lay.

Hassan waited for his friend to come in, standing in the water up to his knees.

― Hurry! -they shouted from the land.

Raindrops spattered their faces as the boats touched solid ground. They unloaded the catch in haste, mooring the boats on the sand. The boy threw himself on his friend.

― Ahmed! I’m so glad to see you. Mother said the ifrit of the skies must be angry with the demon of the sea and that there would be a great storm tonight. I was frightened when you were so late.

― We’re all right, Hassan -they chatted eagerly as they dragged the nets to the shed-. Father said there was nothing to worry about, that he had lived through many storms like this one, and that in worse conditions they always managed to get home. But truly, I was very afraid in spite of my father’s encouragement. I had never seen the sky and the sea rage like that. I’m glad to be on land. We had a good catch today. We’ll supply the village and be able to sell what’s left over.

― Well, you can’t imagine what we found in the quarry today! Golden snakes set in quartz!! They’re very beautiful. Father says we’ll be able to buy wool and cheese from the Qashqai. Mustafa told me the legend, he says the ifrit of the volcano hurled burning lava at the ifrit of time, and the fire was left trapped in the crystal, keeping its glow.

The two boys went into the house. There they were awaited, to change their wet clothes for dry ones. The fire warmed the labourers at the supper hour.

The men worked from early boyhood and learned trades according to their skills. Hassan cut and polished the sandstone with which the houses of the village were built. A son of quarrymen, he carried on the trade of his father, grandfather and great-grandfather. Ahmed was a fisherman, like his brother, like his father. They lived tied to the sea, bound to the waves by a force that could not be explained. Even on the days when the weather would not let them fish, he stayed leaning at the window, listening to the murmur of the waters.

The two belonged to different lineages joined in one great clan that struggled to survive. All were brothers, all sons and all fathers, but it was not custom that made these two little ones understand each other in so special a way. There were many more children, younger, older, but by some strange coincidence, no other of their own age.

As their mothers tell it, laughing, in that long year of famine all the men, except their husbands, suffered the so-called “pollen drought”. That year was hard, one of those times that stays caught in the memory. And there they were, to remind the villagers that even when nature turns her back on men and withholds her gifts, woman gives life to new males who prove the worth of a race that rules the skies, the seas and the earth above any other creature of Allah.

Of that night nothing more was known, and so many other days went by. The boys did their work and the little time left over they spent playing like any other children. Fishing for octopus among the rocks, catching insects, collecting stones and shells, hunting for treasure in the sand, playing hide-and-seek through the streets, annoying the hens in the yard, climbing to the tops of the palm trees. Landing from those beached ships and storming the coast with wooden swords that clashed against the sharp pincers of the crabs.

Those were days of plenty. After the storm, the catches were abundant, and abundant the gold taken from the mountain.

No sooner had the morning been embraced than a group of men and women set out for Dimeck to sell the riches of the village. They rode horses and camels loaded with bulging saddlebags, and in return they got cloves, tea, cinnamon, metal utensils and other everyday goods.

On those mornings the boats stayed moored and the hammers did not ring. The journey was made only once a year, and many were those who went, drawn by the call of the capital. The men who stayed behind busied themselves looking over the village and mending small things, the women went about their daily tasks a little more at ease. In those days of so many absences, the village lived at rest.

Under that carefree calm, Hassan and Ahmed slipped furtively to the stable. Among the goats and hens there remained a young sorrel who, for his spirit, would let no one ride him. The horse got up when he saw them and moved away from the boys.

― We’re not going to load you with bundles, or order you where to go, we know you’re untameable as the wind -said Hassan.

― We’ve come to propose an escapade. We climb on your back on condition that we’re home by nightfall. What do you say, Azzur?

The horse circled them, studying them closely, walked to the gate of the pen, pushed the latch with his muzzle and opened it. The boys looked at him in disappointment; they had not expected that invitation to leave the stable. They went out looking at the horse, waiting for him to shut the gate behind them. With the boys’ heavy gaze on the back of his neck, he gave a deep sigh and looked at the horizon, slowly left the stable, bowed, and with a turn of his head invited the boys to mount. The little ones, jubilant, fastened their tunics and packed water for a day’s journey. There was no one in sight and the horse flew towards the desert. They rode along the track of rocks that marked the way to the capital road. After several hours on the dunes, Hassan said.

― Perhaps we should go back. If we turn round now, it will already be night when we arrive.

― But we haven’t done anything, only gallop. We haven’t even seen highwaymen, or soldiers, or lions… The grown-ups are quite wrong to scold us over making this journey, there’s nothing interesting about this trip.

― If we go as far as the capital and leave the village for ten days, our fathers will beat us when they see us in the city, and our mothers too when we get back. We’ll take so many beatings we’ll reach old age black and blue -Ahmed stopped, thoughtful, at his friend’s conclusion.

― You’re right. Let’s go back, Azzur.

The horse looked at the boys in disappointment. “You bring me out of the village for this?” could be read in his eyes. They had barely retraced a few steps when a sandstorm began to rise. They had to find shelter. The horse left the track, heading away from the storm. They climbed the dunes and came upon a small hill of rock full of hollows. The largest of them, dark and unsettling.

The animal went into the gallery along a long passage where the murmur of dripping water could be heard and the ground underfoot was soft and covered in mould. The entrance was far behind when the storm struck. Once the danger had passed, Azzur was walking to start back when the cry of something not human echoed in the cave. They stopped, frightened. Silence. The cry was heard again. The boys dismounted uneasily.

― It sounds like a bird talking. It must be far off.

― I think it’s a moan, perhaps it’s hurt at the end of the cave -the horse shook his head, tugging at them.

― There isn’t a single man in the village you’ll let ride you. This shouldn’t frighten you at all -Azzur gave Ahmed a stern look in return.

― I don’t think he’s frightened, perhaps it’s the trap of some ifrit -the sorrel’s eyes opened wide.

― Well, if it’s a trap that makes it even more exciting. I’m a seaman, I’m not afraid of the sea or its creatures, there can’t be anything worse in here -the cry sounded again. Ahmed went a few steps in. Hassan waited.

― Ahmed… are you sure we’ll be all right?

― Of course! I’ve got my machete. What is there in this desert bigger than a falcon or a snake?

Hassan nodded and, holding on to his friend’s belt, they walked until they were lost in the depths of the gallery. The horse whinnied furiously to catch their attention, but they had already disappeared down a narrow tunnel. Azzur sat down to await the boys’ return.

As they went deeper into the cave, guided by the moans, a faint blue glow grew stronger and stronger, the precious stones embedded in the rock shone softly. The cries could be heard clearly; the end of the tunnel, a great hall full of luminous plants that glowed like embers between those iridescent walls opened before them. In the middle, a great peacock of pale white. A boulder was crushing its right wing; the moans went on. The little ones went down in silence.

― Hello…?

― AAAAHH!! -they drew back in fright-. Don’t go. Help me. Children… what are your names?

― Ahmed.

― Hassan.

― Thank God you have come. With your help I shall be able to escape from this cave. Some days ago, flying over the dunes, a storm took me by surprise. The ifrit of the sands offered to help me by bringing me to this cavern. I trusted that wicked being, whose only intention was to tear out all my plumage.

We met in hard combat, but my magic is greater than his. He begged for mercy and, fool that I am, I granted it. Treacherously, he hurled this rock upon me that holds me prisoner; however I try, I cannot free myself of it. The days without water or food have been long. I remember with rage the words of that shaitan, saying he would come back for my feathers when I breathed my last.

Hassan, Ahmed, will you help me get out of here?

― Of course we will! -they answered.

They found a couple of poles to use as levers. The bird pulled hard and the boys pushed bravely. After a couple of attempts the rock gave way and freed the peacock.

― Thank you, children! You have saved my life with this kind deed that does you honour. Small in stature but great of heart -the little ones smiled.

The bird rose, unfolding its plumage. All the brightness that lit the cave flowed towards the peacock. The light gave colour to its plumage, as brilliant as the morning sun. Its bearing swelled until it was twice its size.

― You were brave to venture into the unknown to save my life. Your courage must be rewarded.

― There’s no need. Our payment is the adventure we’ve had, and that it ends well.

― Praised be your humility. For that I shall give you three feathers instead of two. The third, give to whomever you wish -the bird plucked three feathers from its tail, those with the loveliest eyes.

― You needn’t have done that, sir. Now your tail won’t look so fine.

― The feathers will grow back as soon as I fly free, do not trouble yourselves. Know that these feathers have the gift of invisibility, and that those who carry them will be clear as water. It is my gift in return for yours. Keep them well and they will be of help to you in your lives. If one day death comes looking for you before your time, fasten your tunic with the feather and you will elude her who ends all things.

― Thank you very much. We’ll keep them and put them to good use.

― Now it is time to leave -at a movement of its wings, the roof split in two and daylight flooded the hall-. Climb on my back -the bird rose over the desert with the boys on its back.

Azzur watched in astonishment, running across the sands in the bird’s wake. And in less than a breath, just before nightfall, they reached the village. The bird took leave of the little ones and the horse returned to the stable, just as several men appeared along the road.

― Hassan. Ahmed. We have been looking for you all day. Where have you been? You should not have run off with Azzur. It is dangerous to leave the village without a grown-up beside you. You do not know the roads and that sorrel is a madcap. You could have got lost and died in the desert. You could have been kidnapped and sold as slaves. You could have fallen into the hands of bandits and been turned into murderers. You could… who knows how many more things could have happened to you. You are very small. Do you understand what I am telling you? -both nodded.

I know you are sensible boys and that this warning will be enough for you not to do it again, but you will be punished. Now go to the village with the women and children while we think about your reprimand.

― Yes, sir -they hurried along until they came to where their mothers were waiting. They threw their arms around them.

― Where have you been? We were so worried about you. We all searched for you in every corner until we found that Azzur was gone. You have been away all day; for a child it is very dangerous to go into the desert, the worst could have happened -those catastrophic words were repeated so often that in the end they cowed the boys.

When you are men, there will be no territory you do not ride. But now you must be responsible, not only towards yourselves, but towards others. We are one great family and we look after one another. At the tea hour, the men in assembly will decide what to do with you until your fathers return. For now, the two of you will walk at our side as if you were our very shadow.

Tea gathered the men under the tent. The boys, set apart in a corner, listened to the deliberation.

― Hassan. Ahmed. Come here. After long reflection we have decided that you may not be together by day or by night. A decision your fathers will confirm on their return, if they do not increase the punishment. It will be eleven days apart that will prevent bad ideas which might lead you to a tragic end.

We hope these days of separation will serve you to reflect on what has happened and help you to mature, leading you to a wise and intelligent manhood.

― It’s an unfair punishment! -cried Ahmed.

― Do not answer back, little one, or we shall increase the punishment -the boy stepped forward to reply when Hassan held him back with a gentle gesture.

― Easy, friend, eleven days are short in our lives and this punishment will help us grow.

― Very good, Hassan. That is the attitude if one wishes to become a man. Now go, each of you to your own house. Tell your mothers of the decision.

On the way out, Hassan slyly showed the feathers peeping from under his sash, winked at his friend, and they parted, each towards his own house. Both mothers, after supper, left them sleeping in their beds, trusting.

It was close to midnight when two shadows slipped down to the shore. Sitting on the sand, their feet wet by the sea, they were side by side.

― I’ve had a wonderful time, Ahmed.

― So have I. I’d never even heard of an adventure as strange as the one that happened to us today. We should do outings like this more often.

― Be quiet, you fool. What do you want, to be punished all your life? With this gift we can see each other every night until they lift the ban.

― You’re right… these eleven days without being able to play together will be long. When your father and mine come back, I hope that seeing us very sorry, they won’t make the punishment longer.

[…]

― Hassan…

― Yes?

― I’m sleepy, I’m tired.

― True, so am I. Let’s go back, it’s been a very long day.

In those eleven days everyone was amazed at how exemplary the little ones were. Not a word did they exchange in all that time; one could only notice a sleepy look on their faces.

The caravan returned from the capital laden with provisions and utensils. Once the bundles were unloaded, everything went back to normal. The fathers, with their families, learned of the adventures of the pair of children. The two met to reflect; the boys waited impatiently.

After much deliberation they gave their verdict.

― It is known that you have borne your punishment like men. Eleven days has been enough. There will be no need to prolong it.

― Provided you have understood the magnitude of your actions and their consequences. Let us know if that is so.

― It won’t happen again. We promise -said both boys-. Thank you.

― No, thanks are given when a favour passes between two parties. Thank yourselves for having understood and accepted. That is the attitude to take towards life, and it is to that you should be grateful.

― Tell us what you saw in the capital, what goes on there.

― Tumblers, fire-eaters, fakirs, pilgrims, white camels, crocodiles from the Nile, great palaces, black soldiers, dancing girls… but what caught our attention most was the drummer cockerel. He is small, with plain plumage, and his greatest talent lies in his skill at beating the skin of a drum with his feet; every tune he hears, he repeats on the instrument with blows of his spur.

― Many are the things to be found there. Wealth and more poverty, amusement and slavery, trade and trickery. The city is a place to visit like a breath of wind. If we could get from the sea everything we need, we would not resort to Dimeck.

― Then why so much fuss whenever there’s a chance to travel there?

― The city is too exotic when one lives in the monotony of the village. But it lacks morals, discipline, humility. See how we all come back; no one is tied to the village, and no one leaves it.

Children, that is enough for today. Tomorrow everything will go back to normal; now it is late and we must rest.

― No… tell us something more.

― Something more…? -the boys held their gaze-. Very well… a short tale I heard from an old nomad woman on the way to Dimeck. Just as she spoke, I pass her words on to you without changing a single letter.

It tells that two Sufi monks were on their way back to their convent. They were returning from carrying gifts to the regent of the temple of the seven domes. But on the way they came upon a peasant couple who asked their blessing for their newborn child, their fields and their flocks.

Out of courtesy, they accepted the invitation to mid-afternoon tea. By the time they took to the road again, night was already falling and the pass of the half-moon lay before them.

One of the monks was blind; with the help of a staff and of his companion, the two made their way.

― Fear nothing, friend -said the guide-. I know that once the sun is down, this place is inhabited by spirits and demons. But I shall keep my eyes wide open and protect you from all danger. Hold on tight and let us walk quickly.

The two men were reaching the heart of the path. The smell of fear made itself felt and the shaitan of the hundred eyes and thousand mouths appeared, summoned by dread. His eyes shone like fire, in his jaws hell could be glimpsed, the tongues hung boiling and his claws threatened death.

The guide stopped, terrified. The blind monk tugged at his companion.

― What is it, brother? I no longer hear your voice and you are shaking with sheer fear.

― The shaitan of the half-moon is in front of us; he bars our way and is coming closer.

― You know I cannot see. I walk in this world of darkness guided by my heart, and I feel no evil presence at all. Trust me when I tell you it is nothing but a hallucination brought on by tiredness. I shall be the one to guide you now; hold on to me and walk without fear.

With a firm step, the blind monk dragged his companion towards the demon. The monster, bewildered by the behaviour of the humans, made a show of his most terrifying shapes, but the men kept on their way without fear.

The shaitan, defeated, vanished into the air, letting the pilgrims pass. The two young men returned to the convent, and many, many other times they walked that road.

Do you understand the lesson of this tale? -both boys nodded-. Then that will be enough for today. It is time to sleep.

Ω

That night they slept happily, and like that one, many more went by over the years. The boys grew up happy and reached adolescence. The point at which the story stops once more. By then little had changed in the village, except the now masculine voices of those boys. Some games were still the same, fishing, riding… others not, hide-and-seek, catching insects…

At night, while Hassan and Ahmed watched the heavens, lying on the shore, oblivious to everything, side by side, their parents pondered the boys’ future.

Sixteen being a marriageable age, and there being no suitable women for the boys, the fathers decided, with the blessing of the elders, to raise the red three-pointed flag at the edge of the sea. It was common to marry masters to their slaves, and that signal stood for the village’s wish to buy slaves.

Ahmed and Hassan watched the flag waving on the sands; the moon shone on that sea of silver and the horizon remained clear, with no sails in sight.

Sooner or later some merchant ship would come, called by the signal, and a new stage would begin in their lives.

― Did you ever think about the moment when you and I would have to wed a woman and give her children? I thought one came to be a man in time, not all of a sudden.

― No, Hassan. I didn’t think this day would come. I’m not a boy of great imagination, but if I have ever dreamed of love, it wasn’t like this, marrying a slave.

― I don’t understand what our fathers intend. A marriage without love, deprived of freedom, a false relationship. What children can be born of it?

― Strange it will be to lie with one who conceives against her will. A mother with her honour outraged and, therefore, unhappy children. That will not be our fate.

― Ahmed, we shall abide by the will of the assembly. Our tribe has walked the dunes for many years; its beliefs come before its wisdom. But I don’t believe God blesses those who betray their own essence for the sake of what society will think. I don’t intend to take part in this blasphemy.

One day we’ll go to the capital, and there we’ll find pretty girls who’ll pine for us…

― I don’t know what you’ve thought up or how we’re going to manage it. But I agree with you from start to finish. I hope it goes well and is brief.

A few days later, a ship alerted by the flag anchored off the shore. The merchant came in on a small boat pushed by two big black men. Reaching land, he made for the group of men that was waiting, among them the boys and their fathers. The trader put out to sea again, accompanied by the four interested parties.
