Once on the ship, they waited on deck while the overseer brought out all the girls of an age with the boys. There must have been about twenty. The merchant walked among them with a riding crop, correcting their posture by pushing up a chin, striking the hunched backs. He pushed back their hair to show their faces and stripped off their clothes to show what little flesh the girls had.

― Smile if you want to leave this ship! -he said, laughing-. They’re thin, but they’re strong. If they’re still alive on the little food I give them, they’ll survive the longest drought and bear you children as tough as palm trees.

They’re daughters of the soldiers of Abdu-l-lah Said. Seldom have I had a batch of women so beautiful, well brought up and gentle. I might say these are slaves of royal stock. Ha, ha, ha. Come on, lads, don’t think twice. Take two or three each. They’ll be your delight and you’ll be happy for ever.

They were dirty, underfed and ragged. Ahmed and Hassan could only be shocked at the baseness of slavery.

― Don’t worry about how they look -said one of the fathers-. Choose the one you like best, and at your side they will soon recover their bloom and their smile.

― But Father, if we buy slaves we’ll be taking part in this cruelty -Hassan objected.

― My son, however hard you blow against the wind, you will not change its direction. The assembly has made a decision. Choose and let us go.

― We’ll take two young ones -said Ahmed’s father-. Come on, choose your future wife now.

The boys chose. Their fathers came closer to check the women’s health; they seemed healthy.

― A good choice. In my opinion there are no better slaves than those two you’re taking.

― How much do we owe you, merchant?

― A thousand dinars each.

― Take your money. Thank you for answering the call and for favouring us with your slaves. Farewell.

― See you soon, friends. Call me if you need my services again, and don’t forget to feed those girls if you want them to last till tomorrow… ha, ha, ha.

The six walked across the sand while the ship made for the open sea. The girls trembled, weak. The boys’ mothers were waiting with clothes to cover them. The boys walked ahead. No one said a word. It had been made plain that the slaves were not to the young men’s liking, but for their fathers that was of no importance where their standing was concerned. In time the disagreement would give way to the wellbeing of their sons and wives, and then they would understand the value of such a gesture.

That morning was one of great commotion. All the busybodies came out to see the faces of the girls, which, covered by the cloths, showed only the eyes. By decision of the families, no one would know anything of them until the day they recovered their health and beauty; they would stay shut away until then. After that time, both would be presented to society and the village would give its approval. Ahmed and Hassan were forbidden to be in the girls’ company until the wedding day. The girls lived in a room apart from the rest, to which only the boys’ mothers had access.

A month went by, wrapped in routine and expectation. When Friday came, once everyone had returned from their work, they were presented. The old master did the honours.

― Neighbours, here come the maidens chosen by Ahmed and Hassan -the boys on either side of the old man and the girls arriving with the mothers-. We are no friends of slavery, but given the circumstances, we make an exception.

These girls are daughters of great soldiers fallen in war, refined women of high education. After a month of careful attention from their future mothers-in-law, these girls have recovered their beauty and bloom.

Today is the day on which the village gives its approval for them to become part of our family -the girls stood beside the ones who had chosen them. One dressed in cobalt blue, the other in emerald green-. Please, take off your tunics and let us all see you.

― Oooohh!! -cried the crowd.

Mesmerised eyes followed the curves of that firm flesh, of those slender waists and regal faces. They wore long braided hair of jet black and had defiant honey-coloured eyes. Hassan and Ahmed were astonished at the beauty of their future wives. Both introduced themselves in voices of silk and the audience listened, overwhelmed by the scent of those flowers.

Everyone was delighted, and approved their staying in the village. The wedding celebrations began with a lavish feast. Night came with the union of the four young people. The villagers ate and drank, enjoying the music and the chatter. Yet, in spite of their smiles, the boys’ thoughts were far away, planning the future after that evening.

Only the embers were left when everyone was resting at home. Hassan and Ahmed slept in their beds, accompanied for the first time by their wives. The feeling was one of uncertainty.

― Tauaddud, I’m sorry it has all gone this way. Orphaned, abducted, ill-treated and now waiting to be raped -the young woman listened-. I always thought I would find someone I would fall in love with, someone I would kiss, overflowing with joy, on my wedding night, and yet here we are, looking at the ceiling.

Let us be honest: to let time go by so that love may be born would, I’d wager, be a mistake. The war has wounded you and I sense that you must heal your wounds, and this is neither the place nor the way to do it.

Much as your virtues are praised, my heart does not stir. This cannot be the marriage my parents long for.

― Your reasons surprise me, Hassan, but I am with you to the last word. Yet, given the circumstances, there is nothing left for us but to live together and learn to love each other. Once I was free, now I am a slave, and I have nowhere to go either.

“The conversation continues in Ahmed’s bedchamber”.

― You mustn’t be upset about that, Seineb. The very day we learned we were going to be forced to marry, we understood that the time had not come.

Too young to settle under the customs of our fathers. We are free in spirit and the outside world is waiting for us. Neither Hassan nor I want this, even if it means going against our elders.

We’ll let some time pass; it will be short. One of the coming nights we’ll get the horses ready, we’ll give you as many dinars as we can gather, clothes and food for the road. And on that night of the full moon you will leave the village without anyone noticing you go.

If you agree, so it shall be done.

― I have no words to express my gratitude.

― It’s decided, then. During these days we’ll behave like a loving married couple and we’ll pretend every night. Nothing will stand in the way of your escape. The horses know the road to Dimeck. The full moon will help you not to lose heart. You won’t need to worry about lions or bandits; at the moment of your leaving we’ll show you the true meaning of the word freedom -the girl did not know what he meant, but she felt the sincerity of his intention and her new destiny gave heart to her. The girls slept that night with their souls at peace.

The following days passed with the slowness of one who fears doing something wrong and being found out. The couples were affectionate, with a touch of fondness that satisfied the watchful eyes of their parents. They went to the gatherings, and let themselves be seen hand in hand, walking through the streets. And when they were among men, they spoke of the firmness of their wives’ flesh and of their skill as wives. They, in turn, gathered with other women, praised the great virility of their husbands.

Many days had now gone by and, at the end of the day, the full moon peered between the clouds.

― Hassan… the day has come, and though my longing to leave is great, I must admit that in these weeks I have felt loved by the people of the village, and you have treated me like a sultana. Love between you and me has not been born, but I could grow used to waking beside you every morning.

Yet I feel that this is not my place. I wish to remake my life, to recover my freedom and be mistress of my fate.

― And so, Ahmed, I beg and beseech you to keep your word. Grant us that freedom you spoke of, which to this day has kept us wondering. Tauaddud and I will thank you for ever for the gift you are giving us.

― There is nothing to thank us for. Your happiness is our greatest joy. I hope you will be so happy that you do not even remember these days. Now is the time to leave the village.

Look at this feather -and stroking it, he became invisible. The girl was left open-mouthed-. Here is your freedom.

― It’s wonderful, a feather from a magic peacock. We shall be invisible and never be taken captive again. It is too much to give thanks for in a single lifetime -the boy smiled.

― Stroke the feather and don’t let go of it.

Both vanished from sight and stole away to the stables, where the two couples met. The boys saddled a sorrel and lifted their wives onto it.

― Hassan, Ahmed, thank you so much. We shall never forget you. May Allah bless you with a happy life until your last heartbeat. Take great care in your adventures and find those who will make you tremble with pure love.

― So be it -the boys answered, smiling.

― Farewell.

With a firm touch of the spur, mount and riders rode off invisible and were lost in the sands. The boys waited until the hoofprints crossed the ridge.

They went home and shut themselves in their rooms. No one saw anything; no one saw anyone.

At daybreak the men missed a horse, while at the same time Ahmed and Hassan were alerting their fathers that the young women had fled while they slept. “They must have drugged us with the evening tea,” both claimed, and their words were not doubted for a moment.

The whole neighbourhood, incensed, spoke of the wicked will of those girls, who from the very beginning had laid the plan of their escape, taking in all the neighbours with their beauty and breeding, deceiving them in so vile a way. The boys’ parents, besides being angry, were downcast and felt they had failed. Those girls, who had won their affection, were leaving their sons without wives. For days Ahmed and Hassan felt the sadness of being abandoned, and avoided any mention of the girls. Their new situation was a great relief to them, free for a time of the obligations proper to their age and their culture.

Both young men hoped to reach a riper age so as to be free of their fathers’ tutelage and begin a life of their own choosing.

― Ahmed, do you think the girls will be all right?

― Yes, they are strong, sharp and beautiful women. They’ll achieve whatever they set out to do.

― I hope things go well for them and they make up for the time stolen from them. You know… when I think of them, of the courage they showed in leaving without wavering, in spite of the comforts the village offered them, when I think of the people around us, subject to their customs, when I think of you and me, I feel we are at the halfway point, an awkward milestone.

What is stopping us from leaving, Ahmed?

― I don’t know. Our roots, our families, our work. You and I together. Time does not exist. Conventions stifle us and yet these reasons keep us tied, for as long as we can avoid the road that is being forced on us.

― It’s good to have you at moments like this, friend -Hassan let his head drop onto his companion’s shoulder.

― You and I again -Ahmed answered. They both fell silent, watching the sun go down.

Ω

Time passed imperceptibly for them both. But the birds were returning from the lands of the north for the third time. By then they were about nineteen and had become men of strong arms and spirited temper.

Ahmed stood out, peerless, for his skill with rod and harpoon, and his name was spoken among the fishermen of the region for his ability to hunt sharks.

Hassan left the quarry to become an ornamental carver, recognised for his elegance and detail in the art of geometry.

The veil of success made people forget that the boys were still unmarried. The pride the village felt in them ran from mouth to mouth, and one day among many, the emir of the sultan of Dimeck appeared in the village and sent for the two young men. Astonished and uneasy, they came accompanied by their fathers. The emir was waiting for them, in session with the council of elders.

― Good afternoon, Your Excellency. Pray tell us to what we owe the favour of your visit.

― I have come in search of you. The echo of your talent with stone and of your skill with the harpoon has reached the halls of the royal palace. The sultan requires your services at the palace.

He wishes to enlarge his domains and calls for you as decorator under the guidance of the first architect, and you he requests to enlist in his fleet in search of the strangest beasts that dwell in the sea. What is more, I come under obligation to take you with me, your acceptance being taken as given.

These two sacks of gold are the payment to your families for your departure. With this money your absence will be covered -the boys were dazed, they did not know what to say, and by the time they managed to utter a word, the council had spoken.

― Our decision agrees with the will of the sultan. Tomorrow you will leave with the emir for Dimeck. You are the pride of your families and of our village -they looked at their fathers, but these were smiling, jubilant-. Your talent is recognised in the highest degree. You will work at court, you will be rich and you will have women with whom to form the family that until today has been denied you.

The council has spoken. What do the young men’s fathers say?

― We accept.

― Then there is no more to be said.

The two left the tent, pleading tiredness, while those who remained celebrated the sultan’s decision with tea and food.

Neither could sleep that night; though their eyes were closed, their thoughts went back and forth. They were not upset at leaving the village, nor did it grieve them to be recognised and rewarded for their work. What burned in their breasts was to spend a long time apart.

At dawn the bundles were already loaded on the horses and the three riders were making their way back to the capital. The emir rode ahead with his guard. The boys kept their distance. In their memory remained their fathers’ farewell, their mothers’ tears and the jubilation of a village.

Five days’ journey later, they passed through the walls of the great city. Hassan and Ahmed gazed in fascination at the charms of those streets, crowded with the most varied people and the most unsuspected trades. Dimeck was just as it had been described.

The trumpets broke the spell; they were reaching the palace gates. The portico opened and the way continued on foot to the audience hall. The emir presented the young men, who knelt before the sultan.

― Welcome to the heart of my kingdom, to my house.

The rumours concerning your skills are such that I cannot contain my wish to see you at your work -he clapped twice and two men appeared-. Hassan, Ahmed, I present to you Mesrur, royal architect, and Abu-Suaid, general of my ships, both men I trust.

Tomorrow at dawn each of you will leave with his mentor. Mesrur and Hassan, you will go to the residential palace, which is being built thirty miles from Dimeck. Abu-Suaid and Ahmed, you will join the royal fishing fleet and hunt down the greatest beasts of the sea.

Before nightfall supper will be offered to you. That is all. You may withdraw to your rooms.

They left the chamber accompanied by the emir.

― Great was the honour the sultan did you in giving you so much of his time. You may well not see him again for a long while. Unless you catch an eighty-foot squid or the palace turns out more beautiful than the city of Cairo.

Although you are now part of the royal retinue, only the highest ranks may share their time with the sultan.

Supper will be served in your bedchambers and tomorrow at dawn they will come for you. Be ready by then.

There the boys remained. They put down their bundles and changed their clothes. A shared balcony joined the two rooms.

― They mean to separate us, Ahmed. Tomorrow we shall no longer be together as we have been every day until now.

― Don’t worry, it will be short. It won’t take me long to hunt down the creatures he wants for his collection, and then I’ll be free.

― I don’t doubt your word, but a residential palace can take years to build. It will take me longer than you to be free of this obligation.

― Hassan, we’ll find a way to meet again as soon as can be. These jobs will bring us earnings that will let us do whatever we want, live the adventures we’ve longed for since we were children…

Supper arrived, interrupting the conversation, and after the meal tiredness got the better of them and they fell asleep.

The next morning two servants woke them. The hour had come.

― Hassan, work hard so that this palace bears your name on its walls.

― Ahmed, take great care; promise me that neither the sea nor its creatures will drag you down to the depths.

― I promise.

― Ahmed, take the feather with you. The sea is more dangerous than an architect’s workshop. If death lies in wait, she will not be able to find you if you carry it.

― Thank you, friend. See you soon.

― Farewell.

They walked in opposite directions. Hassan crossed the desert towards the palace; by the end of the afternoon he had reached the grounds where the pillars of the new building were rising, and there he began to draw his new designs. Ahmed reached the port; that same day he was ploughing the high seas on the royal ships, the nets already cast and the boats out hunting, manned by the harpooners.

In those first days they missed each other very much, but by dint of routine and hard work the days went by quickly, turning into weeks, into months, and the months gave way to years.

Ahmed displayed his skills and his catches were many and successful, winning him great recognition. But after almost two years, the idea of leaving the royal ship began to take shape in the young man’s mind. He was tired; he wished to be a coastal fisherman again. That night he asked heaven to let him hunt the greatest creature ever seen, so as to win the sultan’s indulgence.

The next morning the shouting woke him: the lookout had sighted a gigantic shark stalking the pod of whales they were pursuing. All the boats put out. The vessels surrounded the beast and one after another the blades fell on the monster, which fought ferociously, capsizing the boats and devouring the crews.

Ahmed’s harpoon flew and sank into an eye; the shark bellowed in fury. Like an arrow it swam towards the young man and rose above the waves with its jaws open. Ahmed fell onto the boat; the beast swallowed the craft and took it beneath the surface. Stillness; everyone watched in horror. Bubbles. The shark floated belly up with the blade buried in its heart. Ahmed surfaced with barely the strength to keep from sinking.

His companions rescued him amid cheers. The prey was loaded onto the ship. Ahmed recovered from his wounds. The ship returned to port to show the catch to the sultan.

Days later the vessel touched land. That same morning the sultan came down to the port to see the great shark. The dock was packed with onlookers. Abu-Suaid and Ahmed waited beside the beast.

His astonishment was complete when he beheld the size of the fish.

― You are the finest harpooner in the history of my kingdom. No one before had accomplished such a feat and lived. This will be the centrepiece of my collection. I was not wrong to recruit you for my fleet; the rumours about your skills were paltry, now that the true measure of your talent has been shown.

I shall reward you with five sacks of gold and you will be promoted to captain -the young man’s face was sad in spite of the praise-. Tell me, my son, I do not see you smile; perhaps you prefer other gifts. Ask and they shall be granted; by Allah, I will do it.

― My lord, your happiness comforts me. I know my catches please you greatly. But my wish lies far from the sea; all I want is to return to land, to return to my home and go on being the fisherman I was before -the sultan’s face darkened.

― I cannot believe what you are saying! Are you jesting?

― No, Your Majesty, it is my will.

― You are a great seaman; think it over, your life is here.

― I thank you, but after much reflection, I long for it more than gold or fame.

― I understand. It displeases me greatly to hear such words, but I gave my promise and so I shall do. You are released from your duties as royal fisherman. You will receive ten sacks of gold and you may go home. Rest tonight, and tomorrow at dawn you will have two horses ready for your departure -the sultan embraced the young man-. May Allah guide you.

The sultan left with his retinue. The seamen cried out in surprise at the young man’s wish.

― I am glad to have shared the sea with you, Ahmed. I wish you a happy life -said Abu-Suaid.

― I am glad to have known you too. Take good care.

― Until we meet again, friend.

Ahmed rested that night. At daybreak the horses were waiting. The young man rode out on his mount, with the sorrel packhorse beside him, leaving the capital.

After long hours the road forked: on one side the village, his family; on the other, the residential palace where Hassan was still working. His mind made up, the reins urged the horse on, and eight hours later he reached the gates of the compound. He tied up the animals, hid the sacks under the sand, and, feather in hand, became invisible and passed through the great gate of the palace.

The afternoon was well advanced. The building seemed far along, full of halls and rooms, with a constant coming and going of workmen. Ahmed walked in search of his friend. He was making for the upper floor when he recognised that voice; he drew near in silence. He watched his companion from a few feet away; he was talking with the architect.

― I think this arcade needs to be rebuilt; the keystone does not look safe. I understand that its beauty lies in its shape, but that multifoil arch will come down as soon as weight is put on it.

― Hassan, that design is truly beautiful, it is the summit of your work; the portico of the main terrace will be admired by the sultan.

― I know, Mesrur. I know how much time, effort and ingenuity I have put into it, but I do not want anyone walking beneath my work when it collapses.

― The mathematics say it will hold.

― Mesrur, I shall not change my mind.

― Listen, the sultan wants the most beautiful palace. That arch will stay there. I shall have a free-standing one built and it will be put to the test; if it does not fall, construction will go ahead.

― Agreed.

― That is enough for today; I am going to my bedchamber.

Ahmed was waiting for his friend to be alone before approaching, when the architect turned without breaking his stride.

― Don’t stand there daydreaming, go to your bedchamber. Don’t keep her waiting.

Ahmed stood motionless, his blood run cold. Hassan was walking down the corridor when his friend got his breath back. He followed in his footsteps through the palace until they reached the living quarters. Hassan went into a spacious tent. He greeted someone; they talked cheerfully. Ahmed listened intently. Night was falling, everyone seemed to be asleep, but a faint panting could be heard inside the tent. The blood boiled in his veins. Without more ado, he retraced his steps, mounted his horse and returned to the village.

The wish to find his friend after two years, to embrace him and celebrate together their return to the place that had seen them grow up, vanished. Ahmed was furious; he did not know the reason for those feelings towards his friend when it was joy that had brought him to him.

Five days later he reached the village. No one expected his return, much less alone. That night everyone celebrated his homecoming with a great supper, at which they listened to the thrilling tales of the high seas, and how that great shark which had come so close to killing him had won him his freedom and the riches he carried. To each of his neighbours he gave twenty gold coins. The rest he gave to his father and mother.

There and then, when the gathering ended, he let everyone know that he would go to live in the shelter on the cliff. They all asked the reason, and the only answer was “I need to be alone”. No one could or would understand, but there were no more questions.

The following morning he made this request of his parents.

― I want to think for a while, I must pray, I need to speak with Allah and find answers. The shelter on the cliff has everything a house should have. I shall not go out fishing out of obligation, only when my spirit needs it. There are dinars and gold enough to live three lifetimes without working. Every five days I shall come home to collect food and water. That is all I ask of you.

His parents, puzzled, could only grant their son’s request. Some days later, Ahmed was fully settled in the dwelling.

His day began sitting on the rocks, drinking tea while he watched the sunrise. He walked along the coast, swam near the shore, rowed without direction. His melancholy face sunk in silence. Ahmed was not happy; he felt in his spirit the lack of the thing that should have brought peace to his life. Little by little the days became weeks. The weeks gave way to months, and so more than two years went by without his noticing much difference.

On one of those many days, someone arrived in the village. The neighbours were surprised to see that bearded, elegantly dressed man, but as soon as they heard him speak, they discovered Hassan.

On learning of his arrival, the man rode to the cliff to tell his son that his friend had returned. The father’s smile turned to dismay when he saw Ahmed’s indifference.

― Are you not glad? He is your friend, you should go and see him.

― I thank you for coming all this way, but I do not wish to go down to the village. Give him my greetings.

The man returned to the village, bewildered; he could make no sense of that behaviour. Hassan let the night go by. The celebration was ending. Everyone was asleep in their bedchambers when the young man walked with an oil lamp towards the cliff. He knocked at the door.

― If you are looking for Ahmed, you will not find him inside the house -the voice came from among a heap of tackle and nets on the ground. The visitor came closer.

― I don’t know why, but I don’t think I deserve this welcome after so long.

― …

― Perhaps it’s better if I go and try again tomorrow. I shall only be here a couple of days before I return to the palace. I’ve accepted another project. I hope the morning breeze wakes you in a better mood -silence. He turned and walked away.

― …I have missed you from the very moment we parted, so long ago now -Hassan stopped to listen-. I have counted the seconds, the minutes, the hours of every single day since that night when I walked beside you without your knowing. You were lying with a woman; I could do nothing but turn round and come back.

I have been the greatest fisherman in the fleet, showered with honours and with a future as captain. My only wish was to stay alive each day so as to catch the greatest prey quickly and win the safe-conduct that would let me be with you.

I suppose a wife is waiting for you in Dimeck, and who knows whether children too…

― You are wrong, Ahmed. I have no children, I have no wife. That woman was a gift from the sultan that I had to accept. We never consummated it; we only lay there and pretended, and paid for her silence. I had no wish to die on the gallows, accused of refusing what society holds proper.

I have heard of your feats as a seaman, and the only thing that has let me survive your absence has been working until I dropped. I accepted this commission knowing you had returned to the village, thinking that since you had not visited me, you were rejecting my friendship.

But I fear the mistake was ours both, in running ahead, in believing phantoms without asking first. It has been very hard, as hard as it may have been for you -Ahmed got up, looked at his friend, took a couple of steps and, facing him, held him tight.

― I have missed you too much -he said through his tears-. I always longed to live hundreds of adventures with my one great friend; I did not want to grow up. And today all time for vanity is past. Today I know that the great adventure of my life is you, the one with whom I wish to grow old.

Now that you are here, that deep emptiness in my heart is no longer felt -Hassan smiled and wiped away his tears with a caress. He rested his forehead against his face.

― I love you too -and he kissed his lips.

That night the two friends kept each other awake. They talked, making up for lost time, smiling like children. They lay on the nets, hands entwined. They fell asleep.

The next morning they woke in the midday light. Ahmed was nestled under Hassan’s arm.

― Do you mean to go back to the palace?

― No. After what I know now, nothing and no one will be able to take me from your side. Dimeck is no place for us, and in the village they will accept it warily. We shall stay here, where we are far enough away. We shall be friends in the eyes of the world.

― I understand and I accept.

That same day Hassan went down to the village and told his parents of his decision not to return to the city. His wish was to stay with his family and his friend Ahmed. Their look of disbelief gave way to a forced acceptance that did not care to see any further. So long as they were only friends living in the shelter, they would not compromise the family, and everyone would bear the circumstances with a certain normality. Hassan was grateful for the tolerance shown.

He left the house and made for the stables. From there he sent a carrier pigeon bearing his intention to give up his post. A few days later the neighbours knew of the two young men’s resolve. And although eyes glittered more than they should each time the two came down to the village to visit their families and fetch food, no one denied them the respect they deserved. Their condition did not allow them to lead a life fully within the community, but even so they were happy to feel part of the village.

Time began to flow for their new shared life. The first oddness gave way to normality. They left the shelter and built a proper house on the other side of the cliff. And in spite of their riches, it was common to see them working with the rest of the neighbours at their well-known trades.

They lived happily, together, enjoying each other. Walking the coast, reading, laughing. Every minute of the day was a pleasure to be shared.

So beautiful was the life of those two men that the summers came and went almost unnoticed.

On one of those many days when Hassan was carving in the quarry and Ahmed was at sea with the fishermen, a wretched thing happened.

The sea was rough, and on such days the men tied themselves to the boats so as not to fall overboard. But a heavy sea capsized the boat Ahmed was in. Everyone shouted to their fallen companions; the other boats drew near. After a few seconds in the water, they surfaced and were helped aboard the other vessels. But the young man did not appear; the sailors turned the boat over; the rope that had held him was broken.

The boats rowed about in search of him, calling his name. For hours they searched, but he did not appear. The sea was growing rougher and rougher; the boats had to return to land if they did not want to lose all their men.

An ill omen sent a shiver through Hassan; the rock he was carving split. He looked up, searching for the source of that feeling: the grey sky, the smell of the sea. He dropped his tools and ran towards the coast, his heart racing and his insides clenched.

When he reached the shore, the boats were coming in to land. Hassan searched for his friend’s face.

― And Ahmed, why is he not with you? -he cried.

― Something very grave has happened. The boat capsized and everyone came up but him.

― NO!!!

― I swear we searched for him for hours, but the sea is getting rougher all the time and had we stayed out there we would all have perished.

― No… Ahmed… No!!

Hassan fell to his knees on the sand. The fishermen ran the boats aground and gathered the catch. The young man wept, his eyes lost in the waves.

― Come, son, come with us to the village; do not stay here or the waters will swallow you too.

A couple of men lifted him and helped him walk. He could barely move his legs. The storm roared on the horizon. The waves seemed to murmur the fisherman’s name; Hassan stopped and turned his gaze to the sea, listening intently. His tear-filled eyes turned bloodshot with rage.

― Lad, where are you going?!

― Come back, the storm is coming! -the men shouted.

The young man ran to the stables, swung himself onto a horse and rode across the sands towards the mountains. Night was falling, darkening the road. The first drops of rain wet his face. He reached the rocky massifs.

He got down from the horse and walked towards the highest mountain. At its foot stood a small broken altar and some battered braziers.

― Great ifrit of the mountains, lord of the rocks and the earth. I, your humble servant, call upon you -said the young man, kneeling before the altar.

A shudder made the earth tremble and the braziers burst into flame, lighting the darkness.

― Who dares disturb my sleep? -spoke a deep, broken voice. A great ifrit carved itself out of sand and stone before the young man.

― I am Hassan, faithful of Allah, son of Moamir and An-Nodama, born in the kingdom of Dimeck. I come before you to beg a hearing, imploring your help and your blessing.

― Tell me, are you by chance the famous stonecutter who carves the rocks of my kingdom, as well for a palace as for a small village?

― I am, my lord.

― Nature and I myself envy your talent; you see that my power does not reach as far as my desires.

Speak, young Hassan: what is it that you wish, and what is it that you are willing to give in exchange for my favour?

― If you grant my wish, everything you ask shall be yours.

My friend Ahmed is shipwrecked at sea on this day of storm; all came back but him.

The ifrit closed his eyes and a murmur could be heard in his long meditation.

― Your friend Ahmed is not dead; life has not yet left him. He is strong and fights against the waves, but he fights in vain; he would not survive even if a hundred hawsers bound him to the land. The ifrit of the sea desires him. He has awaited his coming for a long time.

Today Ahmed’s love no longer dwells in the sea; now his love belongs to someone. That is the reason why anger and jealousy have unleashed the fury of the ifrit of the seas, who would rather see the one he longs for die among the waves than watch that heart, now given away, little by little forget the sea.

― Save my friend’s life.

― The price must be high, Hassan.

― Do it, please.

― The friendship I have known in other men does not reach as far as sacrifice. Are you the man to whom the shipwrecked one has given his heart?

― I am.

― Love knows nothing of men or women, of gods or humans, of ages or religions. A peculiar feeling, love. I would multiply your offering fivefold only to feel it once more.

Hassan, what you ask will not be simple; I must rob an ifrit more powerful than I of his treasure. Success is not certain and only the weight of your gift will tip the scales in my favour.

Your life in exchange for Ahmed’s -Hassan bit his lips for an instant.

― I accept.

― So let your wish be fulfilled -the ifrit closed his eyes and his arms stretched towards the heavens. A few minutes. The demon opened his eyes-. It is done. Ahmed rests upon his bed, in the house you share, asleep. He will remember nothing of what happened.

Now it is your turn: your life will become mine. The love you feel for him I shall be able to feel, and I alone shall be the master of your beauty. But pity echoes in my memory. I grant you one year from this moment; when that time has passed you will die and, turned to rock, you will be mine -Hassan nodded.

― Thank you for the concession. We shall meet again.

― So we shall -the ifrit replied, vanishing.

The young man galloped breathless until he reached his home. The storm was raging and the tall waves stretched over the shore as though seeking what belonged to them.

He opened the door; Ahmed was sleeping naked under the sheets, his exhausted look bearing witness to the ordeal he had been through. Hassan lay down beside him and cared for his beloved until, several days later, he woke. Ahmed did not remember what had happened; in his last image he was fishing on the wild waters.

― You fell onto the boat and hit your head. When they got back to land the other fishermen brought you home. You have slept for days. I am so glad you are awake.

― Well, I don’t remember anything, my head doesn’t even hurt. I only remember dreaming that the sea was calling me, dragging me down to the depths.

― It was a dream, nothing more. Go on resting. You have lost weight; I shall go down to the village for food.

Hassan left his lover in bed and went down to the village. The men were gathered in the tent. The whole village knew what had happened and they came up to offer their condolences.

― You should know that a few hours after you returned to the coast, Ahmed’s body appeared on the shore. Though he seemed not to be alive, he was still breathing; I took him home, without leaving him for a single instant, I gave him my care and prayed for him. Barely an hour ago he woke. I have come to let you know that he is alive and that he does not remember what happened.

Hassan explained to them the version that Ahmed believed to be true; they all agreed to veil the truth, even as they celebrated the young man’s reappearance. They resolved to leave it a few days, until Ahmed had recovered, before he came down to the village and visited his family.

Days later, now recovered, the young man was walking towards the shore; the waves were coming up to him.

― Nooo!! -Hassan shouted, seeing him so close to the sea-. Don’t go into the water.

― Easy, I’ve barely wet my feet. I have survived a shipwreck in wild waters; what could a wave of this calm sea do to me?

I am a fisherman; salt water runs in my veins. Risk and love of the great blue go beyond good sense. Were it not so, there would only be fishermen of river and pond.

― I know, I know your passion for the sea all too well, but the ocean has come close to killing you. Do not tempt fate again. I beg you. Though my wish may seem foolish to you, if you love me, do not go near the sea.

We have spent a long time apart, we have overcome obstacles that could not be overcome, and now that I have you I do not want to lose you. Swear it to me.

― I don’t know why you are so afraid of the sea touching me, but I accept. I swear not to go near the shore, in honour of the tremendous love I bear you, beside which my devotion to my trade is tiny.

― Thank you -he answered, embracing him.

That same afternoon they went down to the village. There everyone welcomed with joy the young man who appeared in such good health. And there, before the fishermen, he announced his decision to give up fishing and that from that moment on he would devote himself to herding and tilling. The decision was supported with relief by his parents.

The young men returned home, and from then on they did as they had agreed. Hassan, for his part, gave up his work at the quarry. The two began a relaxed, peaceful life. They spent their time looking after their animals and their crops; they cooked, went down to the village, went for walks.

They loved each other and gave all their time to one another. And so, lost in the feeling that filled them, the weeks became months, and that serenity made Hassan forget his pact altogether.

Hassan was cooking while his companion was outside with the animals. He was stirring the pot when he felt a sharp stab in his fingers. The little finger seemed stiff; it had no feeling and had lost its cinnamon tone; it was hard, as if it were rock. He looked at the almanac: a little under two months remained. The ifrit of the mountains seemed to be collecting his payment in advance. Hassan hid the fact, doing everything he could to keep his friend from noticing the change.

The days went by, and although the young man did all he could to keep his secret, soon the difficulty of his movements and the pallor of his skin would give away the ill that had befallen him. He explained his condition by the change of season and pretended to have a cold. But a bad limp and his clumsiness ended with him flat on his face on the floor, unable to get up. Ahmed came running when he heard the fall.

― Hassan, what is it?!! -he lifted him and helped him to the bed. Holding him firmly, he noticed the stiffness of his muscles and the cold touch of his skin-. This is too much, Hassan. I don’t know what kind of illness this is, but it has gone very far. You can’t hide it behind a cold; you are hard and cold as stone.

I don’t know why I let you persuade me it was nothing. We must go down to the village at once so the doctor can see you.

― No, Ahmed, there will be no need. What my body suffers has no remedy; there is no plant or poison that can cure my ill. Barely a week remains.

Do not go searching and do not go away; only stay with me.

― I don’t understand what you mean; a week remains until what, exactly?

― Ah… -he sighed, leaning back-. How can I tell you…? I ask only that you do not stop loving me once you know what I have hidden from you for so long.

― Never.

― Thank you -he said, stroking his lips-. Almost a year ago, on the day you were shipwrecked… you did not appear on the shore by good fortune. That day the ifrit of the sea made your boat capsize, and where your companions survived, you were dragged down to the depths of the ocean to keep company for ever with that demon, who envies my luck in having you at my side.

It grieved that ifrit that little by little you were drawing away from the sea, that you did not ride his waves every day, that you did not risk your life in every swell. To keep him from dragging you off with him, I offered my life to the ifrit of the mountains.

― Hassan… -he said, his face undone.

― Now you know what happened. Look at the almanac; barely a few days remain until the happiest year of my life is complete, and my time is over.

When I die, bury me in the garden; that way I shall still be at your side and be able to see you every morning when you wake.

― Be quiet!! I’ll find a way, I’ll bargain with the ifrit.

― NO!! Don’t. Those demons have luck on their side; to deal with them is to walk towards ruin. Only stay at my side until night falls. Please… -Ahmed nodded, stroking his beloved.

Hassan spent the time left to him lying down, motionless, breathing slowly, accompanied by the ever fainter beating of his heart. Ahmed stayed at his side. The hours dragged on without end, broken only by the young man’s unintelligible whisper asking for water.

His skin turned snow-white, his hair grey and his eyes amber. He seemed drowsy, absent. The thread that held his life was breaking. Ahmed wept in silence. His tears slid over Hassan’s skin, now lifeless. He held him in his lap, pressing him to his chest, unbelieving.

With the first light of dawn, swollen with rage, he rode towards the mountains like an arrow.

― Demon of the mountains!!! -he shouted before the altar; nothing and no one answered. Several times he repeated his call. Silence. Ahmed fell to thinking-. Ifrit, I come to offer you my life in exchange for your favours! -there was no reply.

Ahmed’s eyes flew wide open with a stifled gasp. Back at full speed. No ifrit can resist the offering of a soul, unless he is not at home. The demon had gone out in search of Hassan. “NOOO!!!”, the young man screamed, making himself heard across the desert sands. The house appeared in the distance, the reddish outline of the ifrit taking the young man into his great hands.

― NOOO!!! Let him go, you fiend! Leave Hassan!! You already have his life; leave me his body so that I may bury him!

― Ha, ha, ha -laughed the demon-. His body and his soul are mine now -the ground cracked open, hindering the rider’s advance.

― Enough!!! Leave him, I tell you! Do not defy me or I shall not rest until I see you dead!!

― HA, HA, HA. A human is no more than a worm beside an ifrit. But you have taken my fancy, young man. Here your beloved shall remain for ever, as is your wish. Yet you will be the one who cannot reach him, however hard you try.

Great rocks began to rise, forming a high wall on either side of the ifrit. Ahmed leapt from his horse and scaled the boulders.

The shaitan tore the house apart and raised the marble figure of the young man upon the stones. The cliff shook, turning into a fortress impregnable from the land. The rocks sharp and slippery. Ahmed unable to go on, the wall twisting and pushing him into the void. With every attempt they rose higher, sharper and more treacherous.

― Wretched ifrit!! May Allah condemn you to the most terrible of deaths for what you are doing!

― Ha, ha, ha… Do not go on blaspheming. See that it leads nowhere good. I can even imagine finer ways to enlarge your suffering. You would do better to be silent and accept your fate as it is. If I kill you, you will dishonour your beloved’s sacrifice.

Ahmed fell silent, sitting, tearful, powerless, unable to enter that palace of rock that kept him from his friend.

For several days he remained there, motionless, sleeping in the open, wasted, disheartened. The ifrit, satisfied, returned to the mountains.

Ahmed went down to the village, dragged a boat to the shore and put out to sea, rowing as far as the cliff. A winding path led in to what had been his home. He moored the boat and slipped along the path, crawling among the stones, stealthily. Halfway there. A violent tremor of the earth.

― Ha, ha, ha… You are tireless, lad -the echo of a voice beneath the earth-. You will never feel Hassan’s touch again. He is mine now; your mere presence is enough to make the rocks of this fortress come alive and drive you from my domain, understand that. Give up your effort, go away -the path tilted, but Ahmed ran on headlong-. Ha, ha, ha… useless, boy.

The walls were closing in as they rose; if he went on, the rocks would crush him. He got back to the boat. He watched; there was no way in at all. He stayed in the boat, rocked by the waves, pondering how to outwit the ifrit and reach the body of his beloved.

He lay in the boat for a couple of days, entranced by his purpose. He thought of returning to Dimeck and asking the sultan’s favour; he would need a warship and many men to destroy the fortress. He thought of the last feather of invisibility. Many were the attempts he turned over, but none of them ended in success.

He leaned over the sea, his face reflected in the surface; it seemed as if someone were watching him from the depths.

― Show yourself… -a moment later the face of the lord of the depths took shape in the water.

― Greetings, Ahmed.

― I have given you everything, a life devoted to you. I have been the bravest warrior on the waves. Several times I risked my life, and even so, unsatisfied, you tried to drown me and take me with you.

― You gave me everything except your love. I could bear to see you tied to another mortal, but your decision… you came into my domain less and less… no… I would rather have seen you dead.

― You saw that your wish was not fulfilled. A capricious will is not enough to fight against love.

― I know… and I am sorry. At times I forget my nature; I am a being between the divine and the mortal, barred from what is human, yet so close to it.

― Today I am alive because my beloved gave his life for me. Today his marble figure remains the slave of the ifrit of the mountains. I want to go in, to recover his body and give him holy burial.

― I am not willing, Ahmed. I made a mistake with you.

― You have wrecked my life. You have no right to decide.

― You know an ifrit works magic only in exchange for an offering.

― I am aware of it. I offer you my body. My heart belongs to Hassan; he sacrificed himself for me, and it will always be his.

― A demon does not play fair, Ahmed, not even for you.

― You have robbed me of everything, I have nothing left to lose. Do as you please.

― Ha, ha, ha… -laughed the ifrit-. I admire you. The next time I feel love for a human, your memory shall be what guides my deeds.

Do you know…? I wish to redeem my guilt. In exchange for your offering I shall grant you the greatest gift I can give you. I feel a terrible hatred for the ifrit of the mountains. Your sacrifice will be enough to drive him from these lands.

In the battle I shall rob him of Hassan’s soul, and it will be free. My waters will bring down the walls of the fortress. You will be mine, but on the nights when the tide rises, the waves will come in over the land until they brush the figure of your lover. In those hours you will be free, so that you may be with him night after night, for ever and ever.

― I accept the bargain.

― So let it be done.

The boat began to sink slowly, dragging Ahmed down to the depths. The sea began to rear and the storm broke loose; with the rolling of the thunder the waves rose headlong and crashed against the fortress.

The ifrit of the mountains rose up in fury on feeling Ahmed’s presence. Before him, the giant demon of the sea. In a failed attempt to defend himself, the ifrit used all his power, but to no avail. Hassan’s soul was wrested from him and his only salvation was flight.

The waves destroyed the rock and the cliff was turned into a shore of fine, warm sand. The figure, half buried, looked no different from any other rock, but its colour had changed: the greyish prison, now the colour of honey, breathed freedom.

Keeping his word, the ifrit of the sea granted the chance to come in over the sand when the tide rose. The waters caressed the figure, and so, beneath the heavens, the two met until the end of days.

The Verses of Chronos

Amid grey mists London dawned for all its inhabitants, regardless of age or class. For some, that would be the last day their eyes would see; for others, one of the many still to come.

The little newspaper sellers were announcing yet another of the many suicides among the MPs who formed the opposition to a government anchored in the defence of the great fortunes and, by extension, in the submission and impoverishment of the proletariat. Scotland Yard was at a loss before a situation in which the deaths gave cause for thought. On the back page of the paper, the revival of Bizet’s Carmen was announced alongside the great horse race at Ascot, which every year brought together the most important aristocracy, royalty and bourgeoisie of Europe.

Arthur sold newspapers in Holy Mary St., a street where the carriages of the wealthy mingled with backstreet prostitutes, luxury hat shops with fortune-tellers, music schools with butchers’ shops, and the police with plunder.

A bobby was often seen running after some rascal to the cry of “Stop, thief! Stop, thief!” while the whistle shrilled at full blast and the truncheon danced in the air. The chances of slipping away varied with the day, but the punishment was always the same: a good hiding and a few days in the cells on water and stale bread. Which, all things considered, was far better fare than the street offered to anyone without a penny.

For Arthur, selling papers in that street was something fascinating. He dreamed of becoming one of those gentlemen in top hat, tailcoat and cane; he dreamed of a loving mother dressed in green satin, with wide petticoats and a camellia brooch; he dreamed of being one of those clean, rosy children who ate toffee and chocolates.

Arthur was a little man of about thirteen, an orphan, his parents perhaps dead of the widespread tuberculosis or the Russian flu, or perhaps he had simply been abandoned. He lived in an attic, sharing his space with a prostitute, crammed into a small room let by an old moneylender who claimed a good part of the youngsters’ earnings.

In those days, in working-class London, it was said that there were no boys or girls, only workers, misery, soot and thirty-hour shifts. Staying alive was a daily challenge set against the luxury of the great capital of the British Empire.

Selling the daily gazette might seem a desirable job at first sight, but the sale of the print run was not enough to eat on, and most of the boys held down more than two jobs in order to scrape together a pound a day. Unloading heavy crates in a warehouse, shining shoes or sweeping chimneys was a good way of not reaching nightfall on an empty stomach.

That June morning was like any other: overcast, cold and damp, but without rain, a pleasant day for escaping that perennial London gloom. Arthur cried “Daily Gazette, another suicide in Parliament” over and over, approaching the gentlemen walking down the street, “that’ll be a shilling, sir. Thank you”.

It must have been eleven o’clock, with few copies left under his arm and his throat beginning to feel the strain, when his voice fell silent at the sight of that woman of heavenly beauty appearing round the corner of Richmond St. She wore ivory-white tulle, silk gloves, a bodice that gave a glimpse of breasts that would make any man stumble, a hat whose brim was heaped with roses and chrysanthemums; her perfume was an arpeggio of the most distinguished scents. She had never been seen in that part of town, but she would certainly be remembered.

She was passing haughtily in front of Arthur when something fell from her bag without her noticing: a silver pocket watch. The boy picked it up, spellbound by its gleam. On the hunter case was engraved a coat of arms encircled by a serpent, on which could be read “vulnerant omnes, ultima necat”. The boy thought of keeping it, but when he raised his eyes and beheld the warmth of that lady’s back, he could do nothing but shout “My lady!” and run after her.

― Begging your pardon, miss, but you dropped this as you went by -her face fell when she saw the watch in the boy’s hands. She stopped, took out a handkerchief and with a look of disgust recovered it from the black hands.

― Thank you, boy. How much is the paper?

― A shilling.

― How many have you left?

― Five, my lady.

The woman dropped a half-sovereign, which the boy picked up, offering her the paper; the lady took it and rolled it up. “THWACK!” Arthur staggered from the slap. The young lady threw the newspapers to the ground and said in a threatening voice.

― A rat cannot touch a thing like this. Next time, call the owner’s attention and let him come and pick it up himself. Good day.

― Good day, miss -answered the boy, a tear running down his cheek.

Although the scene had been witnessed by several passers-by and even by an officer of the law, nothing more was said of it. The boy went on crying the headlines until his work was done.

Mr Stevenson was waiting at the warehouse. The boy handed over the whole of his takings except the eight shillings that were his due, along with some newspapers from previous days that Arthur put to good use. Like the rest of the children, he ran off without wasting a minute to begin his next job.

Elisabeth would be waiting for him at Rockwood and Clyne St. Beside an unfrequented alley, Arthur would find a suitcase with a young master’s outfit she had left there beforehand, and change his dirty clothes for garments that were already beginning to pinch. Unrecognisable, he would step back into the street, where a few yards on he met her and walked hand in hand with her; from there the two made for the central station. Both dressed for the occasion, they entered the railway station unchallenged, where they pretended to be nanny and young master waiting for the next train. Elisabeth showed off her figure under the corset while making conversation with many of those solitary travellers who could not help responding to the young woman’s charms.

Arthur possessed a quality that made him special, a talent the girl had known how to appreciate, taking him under her care, which was far better than living in an orphanage or sharing a room with eight other children hired out by some philanthropic exploiter.

His youth did not prevent him from being one of the best pickpockets in the city, and the alliance with that seductive young woman made them a dangerous pair. Each day they varied the scene of their thieving: the railway station, the central market, Goldmile St.; there was no crowded place they did not work. The day lasted until they landed a good prize or until the afternoon ran out.

The day had not gone badly: two purses adding up to seven pounds and forty-seven pence, as well as a forgotten suitcase of men’s clothes, which they managed to pass on for a few coins at the den of John Grills, a pirate in whose shop clothes, food, kitchenware and a long etcetera were sold, and whose chief supplier was theft. Nevertheless, for all his insufferable temper, John did everything he could so that all his neighbours had something to put in their mouths each night, and rather than let an apple rot, he gave it away. Which had turned his small, dirty shop into a thriving business among the poorest class.

Back home, the two would take off those clothes they treated with such care. A small sitting room gave onto eight bedrooms, each let to one of the many prostitutes of the red-light district. One of those rooms belonged to the pair. Arthur slept inside the wardrobe, on a little cot improvised from blankets, while Elisabeth used the bed for the business proper to her profession. Better days, worse days, but rare was the night when the springs of that old bed did not creak.

Sleeping in those conditions may have been hard for Arthur, but tiredness and a meagre diet made sleep something unbreakable, interrupted only after midnight, when there were no clients and Elisabeth, opening the wardrobe, would drag the boy to the bed, where the two slept together, keeping each other warm, the only way not to end up with pneumonia on those cold, damp nights.

The following day went the same way, except that on reaching the corner of Rockwood and Clyne St., Arthur found himself alone; Elisabeth was not there as usual. The boy waited expectantly, thinking that perhaps she had been delayed buying a sponge cake with which to celebrate the previous day’s good haul. The minutes went by and turned into more than two hours of waiting. At the end of the street the girl appeared, prettier and more elegant than on any other day. She crouched beside the boy and left a couple of coins on the ground.

― I’ll see you in the room, don’t follow me.

Arthur took the money and, when his friend turned the corner, set off for home by other streets. Whenever they went out together, they did so dressed alike, as rich or as poor. He reached the room and found the everyday Elisabeth, with no make-up, no trinkets.

― Hello, Arthur -she said with a big smile-. I’m so sorry I was late, I got held up on the way. I bought you this, I hope you’ll forgive me -she said, offering him a small cake.

― Thank you, Elisabeth, but tell me, if I may know, where were you coming from looking so lovely? -she blushed.

― I’ve spent a good part of the money we had; what was left was only enough to buy this cake -the boy looked at her, puzzled-. Don’t be cross with me, it occurred to me this morning, and although I didn’t ask you, it seemed a great idea. I dressed up like that because I’ve been to the racecourse. I’ve bought two tickets for the great race.

― Since when do you like horses, Elisabeth?

― Ah… I don’t like the races, ha, ha, ha.

― I know, don’t worry, I think it’s a great idea to go to the racecourse on a day like that and come away with a good haul. What I don’t think is right is that you expect me to finish this little cake on my own -he took it in his hands, broke it in two and offered her half.

― Thank you.

― You’re welcome -they enjoyed the sweet sitting on the bed.

Those days of waiting passed quite normally, until the day of the event arrived. Both were dressed for the occasion; she looked truly beautiful, and Arthur’s suit had been altered and fitted to perfection.

They walked a good stretch, then took the tram, and made a last journey by horse-drawn cart to the edge of the grounds. They looked on absorbed, marvelling at the tremendous luxury of that festivity: hundreds of carriages and the odd motor vehicle. All the women were dressed like queens and the men’s shoes shone as brightly as the enormous jewels worn by all those wealthy people from half of Europe. Accents mingled amid laughter and glasses of champagne. The pair were overwhelmed; she seemed not to stand out among the escorts prowling there, and Arthur felt out of place.

People were beginning to enter the grounds. The excitement was tangible; the music of the band and the coloured pennants made it almost a place of fantasy. In the entrance passages people pushed to get in, and already a couple of wallets, watches and trinkets were falling. The betting hall was a real hive where hundred-pound notes came and went. Elisabeth’s flirting had begun with some of the bookmakers, and the purloined pounds already weighed in the bag.

The races began and everyone took their seats. As the horses neared the post, the stands rose, roaring for their winner. Robbing those fervent people was proving easier than they had expected. The haul surpassed all expectation and, before the interval came, Elisabeth decided to leave the grounds before the victims missed their belongings and sent for the police; and if the grounds were closed for a search, they would end up in gaol. Arthur gladly accepted the invitation and, while everyone watched the steeds’ advance in rapture, the pair made their way back along the seats to the aisle. They were climbing the steps to the central hall when one of those many faces caught the boy’s attention. He stopped and peered under the enormous ochre-coloured sun hat. That complexion, that expression: it was the woman who days before had called him “rat” after slapping him when he returned the watch she had lost. She was sitting in the last row of the stand, her bag set beside her.

― Arthur, we must go now.

― Go outside, I won’t be more than ten minutes.

― Be careful, I’ll wait for you at the exit gates.

The girl went on her way, and the boy, with natural stealth, slipped up to the last row. The din was deafening and people were bouncing on their seats cheering the race. It was not hard to reach the lady’s side and, without anyone noticing a thing, a furtive little hand advanced among buttons to seize a purse stuffed with banknotes; but something gleamed in the pocket of her little jacket. He left the purse and, with unequalled skill, drew out the chain and after it the device that had once been between his fingers. He dropped it into his trousers and, just as he had come, he left, appearing beside his friend. The pair of thieves left the grounds behind before a great commotion arose among all that bedecked aristocracy, with no one yet aware of what was missing.

Elisabeth paid the driver, leaving a small tip. They took the tram and a while later were already walking home, by which time a huge uproar was scandalising everyone present. A loss or a petty theft was usual, but never had they reached thirty complaints after the fourth race.

― I suspect that by now there’ll have been the odd complaint. If the other young thieves didn’t get out in time, I dare say they’ll already be under arrest for want of a good pedigree to clear them of all blame -she said.

― I know there are different social classes, though I don’t understand why; when all’s said and done they’re as human as we are. But what I understand least is why they get so angry when someone steals a watch or a pendant from them. They’ve money enough to buy half of London and they cry out to Astraea, when the most wretched Londoner is capable of sharing his only mouthful of the day so as not to see a fellow creature go hungry.

― Don’t dwell on that now, Arthur, there are circumstances it’s better to accept. For the moment, think of the money we’ve made and of how much we’re going to get selling all these things. By the way, what did you steal from that woman, so elegant and beautiful?

― Do you remember what happened to me in Holy Mary St., the woman who hit me with a newspaper? Well, it was her. I had the purse in my hands when I saw the watch, and here it is.

― Very good, my friend. Now that the balance between you has been restored, are you thinking of keeping anything? I love this pendant, it brings out my beauty.

― It’s very pretty, but you’re a very beautiful woman and nothing adorns you more than your smile.

― Ha, ha, ha… -she laughed-. That was very sweet, you’re quite the charmer, but I sense something more behind your words.

― I’m not going to keep anything. I’ll get rid of it all and put the money where nobody will find it. You should do the same if you don’t want someone to identify the jewel and you to end up in prison.

― On reflection, you’re right, let the black market take care of everything. And with the money I’ll buy my own silver ring and have my initials engraved on it.

They reached the room, changed their clothes and went out to John Grills’s shop, where gold and diamonds turned into banknotes, so many that he had to write them out a receipt for five hundred pounds, payable when all the goods had been placed. The shopkeeper was surprised by the sheer scale of the theft. He was used to petty dealings, but never in such quantity.

― You’ve pulled off a masterstroke. I don’t understand how you got out of there with all this. If you manage it well, you could go more than two years without a single hungry day. You shouldn’t keep so much money at home, anyone could get into your room and leave you penniless. If you draw the total in small amounts, I’ll look after it for just three pounds a month. It’s a good deal and your money stays in safe keeping. I’m much cheaper than a pawnshop -the youngsters nodded.

― Listen, John, it seems a good deal to me. I’ve always heard that, for all your temper, you’re a decent sort. I know you won’t run off, but if you did… -she said menacingly.

― Ha, ha, ha… Elisabeth, I’ve known you since the day you were born. When your mother settled here, my shop had already been going for years. This business, sad and dirty as it is, feeds me and my family every day, and will do so until my last breath. The money from those jewels will run out sooner rather than later. Don’t worry about me, but about finding something to keep you when this is gone.

― It’s a deal -the shopkeeper shook hands with them both-. You have my word.

― John.

― Yes, Arthur?

― There’s still something left. Elisabeth wants to hand over the pendant she’s hiding under her blouse.

― Arthur!!

― We agreed we wouldn’t keep anything -the girl undid the cord and left it on the table.

― Value this too, John, and I don’t care what you give me for it -the man set the pendant aside, noting down a figure, and turned with particular attention to the pocket watch; he gazed at it, fascinated.

― Good heavens, Arthur, where did you get this? I don’t know what this metal is, but the hunter case is set with gold and diamond, and the hands are filigree in quartz -he could not get over his fascination-. This is no ordinary watch. Inside it there’s something liquid that seems to circulate with every beat; there are several dials but none marks the hours of the day or the days of the month. I’ve never seen the like. I’m unable to work out the value of such a thing; I don’t even think I could place it. It’s the first time in a great many years that I haven’t been certain. Phew… -he said without lifting his eyes from the object.

There was once someone in Philippus St., his name was James Drake, and he owned one of the most peculiar businesses in London. Once I went into his shop out of curiosity and discovered there the greatest extravagances I had ever seen in watchmaking. The truth is it can’t have gone very well for him, since a couple of years later that shop became a printing works with second-hand books for sale; the first name changed, Robert, but the surname remained. Perhaps if you go there they’ll be able to tell you something about this marvel. Although if you prefer, I could give you fifty pounds for it; it would be a pretty piece for my collection. What do you say?

― I don’t know what to answer. The truth is I don’t care about the watch, but it has aroused my curiosity. I’ll go to Robert Drake’s shop for information; I’m sure you too will be delighted to learn any detail, however small. Failing that, I promise the watch will go to John Grills. What do you think?

― You’re a good negotiator, lad, you’ve convinced me. I can’t wait a minute longer to know what this contraption measures. Come back as soon as you know something.

― We will, Mr John. Now, with your permission, we’ll go to the printer’s; with luck it may still be open.

― See you.

― See you soon, youngsters.

The two walked at a brisk pace; it would be more than half an hour before they reached their destination.

― It seems you’ve got hold of a very valuable piece. I hope that bookseller can tell us where to sell it and at what price. You’ve a good eye, my friend.

― It was chance. The truth is I only did it to repay her thanks in kind. All the same, I should have stolen the purse; this watch is burning a hole in my pocket. If that bookseller can’t tell us anything, I’ll sell it to John on the way back.

― Let’s leave the watch aside. Have you thought about the amount of money we’ve managed to put together? Nearly eight hundred pounds between cash and jewels. With that money we can eat something more than bread and soup every day. We could even rent a little two-bedroom attic, I know of some that would be ideal. Your own room, Arthur, isn’t that wonderful?

― As far as I’m concerned, yes, I can’t wait. But think of those fat old relics of clients of yours: by the time they get to the fifth floor they’ll be so worn out they won’t even feel like kissing you.

― Mmm… that’s true. But I’m not prepared to have you go on sleeping in a wardrobe, putting up with the panting of those slobberers.

― Are you thinking of changing trade?

― No, Arthur, I don’t know how to do anything except sell my favours. I’m a bit old now to try to change, though I’m afraid of dying of some disease; but seamstresses die too because of the dyes, and miners because of the coal. It’s a turbulent time for the lower class and London is not lenient with us. We are what we are, with money and without it. But one thing I’m sure of: I’ll go on plying my trade, but I’ll go up in the world. I’m beautiful and young; I’ll pay the entrance to those gentlemen’s clubs and sell my body at a better price, leaving the street. You and I will live in that attic and they will pay to treat me to a hotel room.

― I’d like to hear that you have a great dream, however small, that begins with getting out of this marginal poverty. I sense you’re trying to be realistic, but if I’m to wish, I’d make you queen, the monarch of the poor, Elisabeth II, she who will govern for all her subjects.

― I like that wish -she said, kissing him on the cheek-. If instead of a watch we had a magic lamp, that would of course be the first wish to be granted.

― I’d ask that there shouldn’t be a single child in London without food, without a home, without a family, without medicine. I know it’s not possible, but if I could, I’d buy parents who loved me, and you’d be my sister. Though for now I’ll settle for leaving that den and moving to the attic.

― You’ll see how our luck changes before long. Mark my words, I’ve a nose like Nostradamus.

― Ha, ha, ha… -they laughed.

On turning into the street there appeared a battered sign on which one could barely read “Robert Drake, printer”. The shop was gloomy, its dirty panes hiding the interior. Inside, candlelight seemed to flicker. The notice said “open”. The boy looked at his friend, she nodded, and he pushed the door, which resisted letting them in. The wood of the floorboards creaked and the metal chime above the door rang out, breaking that sepulchral silence. The shop, dusty and heavy-aired, was lit by the grey light from the street. Someone was moving inside the office.

― Coming -said a deep, aged voice.

The youngsters stayed pressed against the door in fear. Noises within. A moment later, emerging between the shelves, came a gentleman with a moustache and white hair, a laconic gaze and weathered hands, dressed in an aged smock stained with inks.

― Tell me, how can I help you?

― Erm… you see, we’ve come looking for James Drake -she said. The old man’s gaze flashed, his stooped figure straightened and he drew near the boy.

― Who mentioned that name to you? -Elisabeth pressed the boy against her protectively and went on.

― You see, sir, I don’t think that name matters.

― Try me.

― John Grills.

― I don’t know him. What have you come for? -he replied curtly.

― We have a watch whose value we don’t know. We’re looking for James Drake in the hope that he can tell us something.

― James Drake died a long time ago. I am a printer and a bookseller; don’t let the surname mislead you, pure coincidence. Go away, I can’t help you -the man turned and began to walk off.

The youngsters stood motionless for a moment before opening the door and going out. The candle seemed to flicker in a strange way, casting a shadow that disturbed the old man. The door had not yet closed when he retraced his steps and called to them.

― James is my brother. Come back in, I may be able to help you. Hurry -the bookseller locked the door and turned the sign to “closed”-. Come on, come -he said breathlessly, leading the young pair to the office-. What are your names?

― Arthur.

― Elisabeth.

― Very well, my name is Robert, James’s younger brother. Show me that watch -the lad took the device from his pocket. The old man snatched it from his hands, wild-eyed-. Good heavens! Where did you get that?

― I stole it from a woman who was unkind to me.

― You don’t know what you’ve done. You’ve condemned your existence. This watch was invented by my brother; it is “The Heart of Chronos”, capable of stopping time for whoever possesses it, of keeping them young, invincible. A device which, together with my own invention, “The Word of God”, a book in whose pages everything that is written comes to pass, makes them a terrible weapon -the youngsters listened, stupefied-. The trouble with my book is that it pays no heed to time: a thing may happen today or a decade from now, so my brother added several hands to control the timing of the wishes written -he went on, agitated-. That watch and the book belong to Madeleine Giovanni, the Empress of Darkness. I don’t know how we’re not dead already -the candle flickered again-. We’re in grave danger -he said, searching for something in the drawer. He took out a small key, put it into a hole in the back of the watch and gave it several turns. He picked up a hammer and crushed the key to pieces. The hands spun senselessly. He wrote an address on a piece of paper, which he slipped into a book-. Now time is turning out of joint. Take the watch, and that facsimile on which everything she writes is reproduced, to the address I’ve noted down for you. You must be quick; “The Verses of Chronos” must be destroyed before that woman does more harm.

The metal chime sounded. The old man looked out: a sombre figure in suit and hat was waiting inside the shop.

― They’re here already, there’s no time for more -he opened a secret passage behind a cupboard-. Go quickly and keep away from every shadow.

― Will we see each other again?

― I doubt it. Run.

The youngsters were fleeing along the passage when the figure entered the office.

Ω

― James… James… James! -she exclaimed before a great obsidian raised in the centre of the hall.

― Madeleine…

― I’m furious, James. A little sewer rat stole my watch this morning. By the time my acolytes found it, the device was in your brother’s shop. A pity I didn’t lock him up with you when I had the chance instead of turning him into an old man. It seems there was a key he kept, capable of driving its hands mad. I fear Robert has made use of that key -she said, more and more incensed-. And not only that, he has destroyed it, leaving “The Heart of Chronos” useless.

― I’m very glad.

― AAAHHHH!!! Be quiet, James!!! I want you to mend it and you have no choice! I have Robert. Do as I say or I’ll put him to death.

― Madeleine… it’s true it would be as simple as readjusting it, but I made only one key aligned with the watch. Your only option is to destroy it and accept that you are mortal again. If you don’t find it and destroy it, there will be no time, and without time the book will be no use to you either.

― I insist, your brother’s life is at stake.

― For more than thirty years I have lived shut up in this gloom, and my brother has been an old man. Death will be more welcome than a single day more like this.

― Then I offer you freedom for you both in exchange for your building another heart. It’s a good deal, James.

― Madeleine, my dear, you forget that you came into our workshop and stole our inventions, that you destroyed all my notes and studies so as to be the sole mistress of time and history. I’ve been locked up so many years without touching a watch that I wouldn’t even know how to wind the simplest of them.

― YOU LIE!!!

― No, Madeleine. I’m no longer the inventor I once was; I don’t even know who I am at this stage, and I owe it all to you.

― JAMES!!! Help me or I swear that…!

― I’m sorry, Madeleine, the game is over. Enjoy what you already have and move your pieces well. Destroy the watch and make the most of the years left to you.

― I haven’t got the watch!

― What?!

― Your brother has helped those two wretches. My whole army of shadows is turning over the streets of London in search of them.

― Madeleine, look at your hands -she lowered her gaze; her eyes stared in astonishment.

― James, I’m ageing!!

― Not only that, your time is running unchecked. Find the watch and destroy it, or you’ll die.

― NOOOO! -she screamed in fury. The darkness plunged the inventor into the depths, making him vanish into the rock-. Bring me Robert! -she ordered her henchmen. The old man entered the room and fell flat on his face, breaking his nose as he hit the floor, pushed by the shadows-. Robert… Robert… It seems that after so many years apart, the two of you still share the same stupidity you flaunted when I met you. Nevertheless, I’ll free your brother and give you back your youth if in return you tell me where the children are.

― I’m afraid you’re ageing before your time, Madeleine. Wrinkles don’t become you.

― Robert, tell me where those children are or I’ll tear off your brother’s fingers one by one and then do the same to you!

― Torture us if it pleases you; it won’t be as painful as these last thirty years. What’s more, we’ll enjoy watching you come unhinged -the woman seized the old man by the neck, strangling him-. Tell me where they are!

― You’re lost. In a few days you’ll be nothing but dust.

― AAAHHH!!! If I die I’ll drag you down with me.

― We’ve been waiting a long time for this moment. To die will be our liberation; we ought even to thank you for being so considerate.
