― ENOUGH! Take him away -she screamed in a rage, thrashing about like a madwoman, smashing every object in the hall-. AAAAAAAHHHH!!!!

Ω

Night had fallen on the city and the army of darkness watched every corner. Elisabeth and Arthur, however, were groping their way along a narrow passage that led them in a single direction. Only the thud of their footsteps and their quickened breathing could be heard.

― I don’t know how long we’ve been walking, but I need to stop, I’m exhausted -they both halted-. I’m frightened, Arthur. I don’t know why we’re here, I can’t see anything, I feel ill, I feel sick, I can’t breathe and I want to get out. I don’t know what got into that old man, or why we climbed into this hole. We went into his shop to have the wretched watch valued and look where we are, in a labyrinth that’s supposed to take us I don’t know where. And what if we don’t find the way out, what if we’re trapped? That old man was mad, he talked nothing but nonsense. He may be in the shop laughing at our expense, waiting for us to die so he can stuff us. We’ve fallen into that psychopath’s trap, Arthur, I want to go home -she said, sobbing. He hugged her hard.

― Easy, Elisabeth, nothing’s going to happen. That old man is no lunatic. Don’t ask me why, but I’m sure everything he said is true, I feel it in my heart. Let’s keep walking and we’ll soon see the light. Everything’s going to be all right, you’ll see.

― Do you promise?

― I promise.

Hand in hand the two went on for a little over an hour. Until at last they came up against what seemed to be a door. A knob turned, the leaf slid slowly into the darkness of the alley. No one in sight; the two came out cautiously, closing it gently behind them.

― Oh! -he exclaimed, stifling the gasp-. The door’s gone, Elisabeth.

― All I care about is that we’re out of that tunnel; one more minute in there and I swear I’d have died. I’ve no idea what’s going on, all this is beyond my understanding. I’m only a common prostitute and thief who can barely read. I want to go home and forget this nightmare -the boy held her firmly.

― You and I are not second-rate. Whatever happens, we’ll come out of this just as we come out every day from the cruelty and misery that have fallen to our lot.

Look up there, “six four nine”, those are the numbers of the address. There’s a door drawn on the wall -he squeezed the girl’s hands hard-. Listen, we can go through that door and carry on, or leave the alley, going on with our lives and regretting that we hadn’t the guts to take on the adventure we’re being offered -the girl looked at him, frightened, and took a deep breath.

― I’m not sure, Arthur, but if I’m to do it, I wouldn’t want it to be with anyone but you. Go on, my friend. What’s next? -the boy smiled and, without letting go of her hand, went up to the wall.

― Now I have to knock three times, according to the note.

“Knock… knock… knock…” The two drew back, waiting for the moment. Stillness. The sound of a knob turning; the wall opened inwards, and a gentleman with orange whiskers, in a dressing gown, smoking an enormous pipe, looked out in puzzlement.

― Who are you two and how did you find this place?

― Robert Drake sent us. We came out here after leaving the passage -the man’s expression changed; he put his head outside and looked about carefully.

― Come in -they went in, and he stayed behind to close the door-. What are your names?

― Elisabeth.

― Arthur. We have a facsimile of “The Word of God”, given to us by Robert, and “The Heart of Chronos”, which I stole without knowing it from Madeleine Giovanni -the man was dumbfounded.

― How…? -Elisabeth repeated her friend’s words. The gentleman said no more.

They climbed a spiral staircase. A great door, locked. He opened it onto a comfortable drawing room with carpet and broad armchairs. He closed it behind them. In it two women sat leafing through a newspaper. The elder, ash blonde, black-eyed, with a caustic face, dressed in indigo; the other, younger, red-haired and amber-eyed, snow-pale and gentle of expression. They looked at the youngsters with curiosity.

― These are Elisabeth and Arthur, Robert sends them. They bring “The Verses of Chronos” -the women stifled an exclamation-. Sit down there, youngsters. This is my wife, Dorothy, and that is her younger sister, Scarlett; my name is Thomas. Time is running against us, so you’d do well to relate everything that has happened since that watch fell into your hands, young man.

Arthur told it all, from the first encounter with Lady Giovanni to their arrival at six four nine, Blind Alley. The three listened, more than surprised, and a long minute of silence followed the boy’s account.

― This is not good, Thomas -said Dorothy-. I’m sure Madeleine has abducted Robert and that her shadows are lifting even the smallest stone in London in search of the watch. We can’t let that object return to her hands, but she’s very powerful, I don’t know what we can do.

― This is the moment, sister. We have the weapon that made her immortal, we know what her wishes are and we can get ahead of her. The watch isn’t working properly; she’s ageing. Look at the facsimile, the letters are disappearing, carrying out its decrees in no order, and without that order her plans are a failure. It’s our chance to free James and Robert and to finish with Madeleine, putting all this behind us. We’ve waited years to go out into the street without fear, and these young people are handing us the ideal opportunity -Scarlett waited expectantly. The couple approved the proposal. Thomas spoke.

― Like it or not, you’re in this now and your only option is to carry on what’s been started. We can’t go out into the street; in these circumstances Madeleine would detect us at once. But you can, and for that you’ll have our help -the youngsters nodded nervously-. Time presses, though unfortunately we must delay at least an hour to prepare everything you need.

All that’s left to her is to destroy the watch so as to stop time before she ages into extinction. Besides, “The Word of God” carries things out capriciously, so in a short space of time many more must be added to those already murdered, the MPs among them. Soon it will come knocking at our door, as at the door of all those friends of James and Robert.

Your task is simple: to save those who are going to die and to run, flee, escape until the watch strikes its last chime and Madeleine becomes oblivion, paying thus for all the terrible evil she has done -Elisabeth interrupted in a trembling voice.

― I don’t wish to be a nuisance. I had never heard of anything like what’s happening to us today, and it certainly seems more serious than we thought at first. But I think I speak for us both when I say it would be somewhat easier if you explained what all this is about -Thomas looked at the clock.

― Dorothy, please see to getting the names of the possible victims noted in the book before the letters finally disappear. If the ink were to be used up we’d have no way of saving anyone. Find out the speed of the watch too.

― But I have to assemble the music box.

― I’ll see to that. Scarlett, please alter two outfits for these youngsters. Meanwhile I’ll assemble the music box and the camera.

The three hurried at their tasks under the youngsters’ watchful gaze. Dorothy turned page after page, compiling endless notes. Scarlett took measurements to make over a couple of half-finished garments, and Thomas assembled the hundreds of parts lying on the table.

― We’ll be done much sooner than you think. In fact, in answer to your request, Elisabeth, I’ll enliven this tense wait by acquainting you in broad strokes with what concerns this unwelcome business.

It all began centuries ago, some thousands of years back, long before Arabs, Greeks or Egyptians, before the great flood. Our stock is lost in the mists of time, where written history does not reach, unfortunately: an Atlantis that, with the coming of the Ram, begins to be persecuted. We were few, and stupidity in the company of a distorted faith saw the pre-initiatic races, and their immense efforts to help humanity advance, reduced to dust, almost extinct.

Long centuries of obscurantism completely obstructed our work, and not until the middle of the fourteenth century would we, the technocrats, walk again with some freedom. With the Renaissance, those who had remained hidden from the Holy Inquisition re-emerged, recovering the great knowledge bequeathed by ancient civilisations and giving free rein to a new generation of technocrats. Art, the sciences, thought expand, reaching new horizons, new worlds, and I don’t speak metaphorically. Humanity was already one before Erik or the Pinzóns. The fact is that by then our society, though set apart, was no secret, and men like Leonardo da Vinci enjoyed sharing their ideas with us.

Yet once again this inner resurgence, which freed minds and advanced man to the level of our cosmic neighbours, was crushed by those great ruling families that plan our future and manipulate reality, aided by those arcane reptiles -surprise unsettled the youngsters-. Let us add to the situation the following axiom: intelligence is inversely proportional to the faith one professes in a religion of power and interests. And, to be honest, God did not make us in his image and likeness, nor does he lavish the excessive stupidity that fears him.

Do not fear the God of the English; know that the universe is within you and never leaves you alone; its only will is that every man be happy. Well… forgive my temper, I get worked up whenever I speak of this.

The Renaissance was brief and little by little we technocrats disappeared; societies changed and, rather than inventors, we were considered heretics. Without many places to choose from, we ended up taking refuge in the London of Romanticism, a city that allowed us to grow in parallel in complete secrecy. Technocrats came from all over the world, though fewer than expected. We had a huge old house where each of us developed his talent, feeling sheltered by his equals.

Our inventions went far beyond what Verne’s imagination guessed at. And had we been allowed, we would already have set foot on the ninth planet and revealed that the dinosaurs are the eighth form of life to walk the Earth. Mind you, inside our sphere there is only magma.

In secret, the Tecnos Organisation Centre was founded, a body supervised by the wisest and most elevated technocrats, who took charge of keeping the balance among us, providing us with a suitable place and hiding our lineage from the outside world, using devices that allowed us to live wholly unknown.

And perhaps you wonder why it wasn’t done so from the beginning of time; but to be castaways against the current is a hard thing to bear, and sometimes it is preferable to forget who you are, to give yourself up to worldly pleasures, rather than remain shut in a cage.

When we were young, more than thirty years ago now, the organisation had not yet been dissolved and a group of young people stood out for their gifts.

Scarlett was a seamstress whose fabrics worked unimaginable wonders. A cloth able to blend into its surroundings, able to absorb the properties of the material it is in contact with, to be invisible; a fabric that everyone admires or that makes you unrecognisable to the crowd.

Dorothy and her delicious taste for music. She built music boxes which, depending on their score, were able to exert capricious influences on their listeners, feelings ranging from hatred to obedience, oblivion or adoration. This last being the reason, I believe, that we are together -he said, blowing her a kiss; she smiled.

I was a good photographer; I loved to capture everything that caught my lens’s interest. I created photographs with movement and sound, three-dimensional colour photography and soul portraits, able to capture a man in a snapshot that comes back to life when immersed in a special solution.

In this group there were also Robert and James Drake. Robert was the younger of the two, the bookseller. He invented books that narrated their own texts, books whose pages were written by thought, however far away one might be. Invisible inks that reacted to their owner’s touch and, as a crowning touch, he created “The Book of Deeds”, where what was written came true, though not chronologically, for which he needed his brother James.

He had the gift we all longed for, to master time. To go back a few seconds, make it advance swiftly, stop it, or attain immortality by escaping time itself. His masterpiece is that device, “The Time Regulometer”. Such was the stir among the technocrats that their inventions were renamed, and not only that, they were perfected in perfect symbiosis, giving rise to “The Verses of Chronos”.

Madeleine Giovanni was part of the group. Her mastery lay in the creation of reactors similar to light bulbs, able to generate every intensity of light, to burn, to cut, to create visual spectacles, to trap light. Her abilities did not end there; she also had necromancer blood, one mestiza among millions, in centuries. Her magic, that of shadows, was a dangerous game that ended by darkening her heart, for she mastered not only the physical ones but the spiritual, being able to draw out men’s fears and rages to give life to the darkness. But that we would not know until later.

Besides those you already know, there was among us Harry, whose keys opened any door, whose twin knobs opened one door here and another in China. Knobs able to open a door drawn in chalk on the wall, as you have seen for yourselves.

Ronald was a fanatic for speed; his autogyro vehicles reached more than twenty miles a minute on the ground, but his hovercraft were truly dizzying; he went round the world in a single day.

Lastly, Sophie, possibly the only technocrat whose inventions marked a before and an after. James’s games with time were dwarfed by Sophie’s capacity to capture it. She created only one device, an audiovisual transmitter able to travel to any point in time, to any place in the universe.

No mentalist, necromancer or technocrat had achieved such a thing. We saw the first, the second, the third, the fourth world war, the great cataclysm. Our blood would rise again, though not to be recognised but to be enslaved in the name of power and conquest: genocides, destruction, chaos. That was not the future we wanted for ourselves; we did not deserve it. Those visions plunged us into the deepest sadness.

The wise decreed the dissolution of our brotherhood, as well as the destruction of our inventions, besides the prohibition on building any device whatever. We were all resettled in society with a job, becoming conventional citizens, coming down, as you see, to the present day. But it did not all end here: the wise who governed the balance disappeared with the technocracy, and although we obeyed the decision in full, our group had one last wish that nobody knew of. We destroyed everything except our fetishes, kept as a memento on condition that they remained dismantled and unusable. But this extinction did not seem to please Madeleine much, not the disappearance of the TOC in itself, that did not matter; what she longed for were James’s and Robert’s inventions. She wished to be immortal and to impose her will. Keeping our inventions on the sly was our worst decision. Madeleine made us believe that, like us, she had given up inventing, and on the contrary she went on experimenting and increasing her power.

One day among so many, she asked the brothers to sell her their inventions; she had managed to amass a fortune through fraud and through influence obtained by threats. She wished to buy “The Verses of Chronos” for her own enjoyment, which meant presenting a psychopathic mestiza with a weapon of world destruction. The brothers refused, letting us know. We managed to dissuade Madeleine, but shortly afterwards her threats took shape and she turned on us, her friends. We tried to stand up to her, but that failed attempt ended in the deaths of Harry, Sophie and Ronald. Implacable, she threatened to kill the rest if the Drakes did not set their devices working. They did not, and her wrath was worse than death. The most terrible torture, until at last she got her wish. She burned our skin for days until she turned us into what you see, a mass of scars that would frighten Quasimodo himself. For James the punishment was to be locked in the shadows for the rest of his life, and Robert was turned into an old man by means of “The Clock of Ages”. Dreadful.

The three of us live apart, and whenever we go out into the street we do so in one of Scarlett’s suits, so that no one pays attention and we pass unnoticed. Not satisfied with this, when we least expect it her shadows cross our path, sidelong, reminding us that she watches us and that we live by her mercy -Arthur discreetly raised his hand-. Yes?

― Erm… I wonder whether destroying the watch and the book wouldn’t be an option. That way we’d save the lives and Madeleine would lose her great power.

― I’m afraid that is the last option, even though it is the most effective. For the letters to burn and the hands to stop would give breath to those to be sacrificed, but it would leave her alive, and Madeleine must not remain among us; letting time run against her until it sweeps her away is the only way of making sure she vanishes from our world. Those who have died are not few; what are a few more. You will save those who must be saved. It seems unjust and it is, but there is no other way -the youngsters listened to the man’s categorical, pitiless words.

― And what is it we have to do now? -asked Elisabeth. Thomas kept silent; he was finishing the assembly.

― Et voilà. Camera and music box assembled and working. Dorothy, have you the names? -the woman slid over a list of more than twenty people. The man ran his fingers over the unfortunates-. Aristocracy, bourgeoisie, politicians, ordinary people. Besides these names, do we know nothing else?

― Some will be easy to find, others are complete unknowns.

― I doubt they are people with influence or great capital; these names will be those of the parents and grandparents of people who will hinder her plans -said Scarlett-. We can’t choose at random.

― Send a teletype to Margareth -he said.

― You can’t do that. She’s not one of the technocrats, her power is not refined, and helping us will be her sentence. Losing her parents is more than she deserved. Go no further, Thomas -Dorothy replied firmly.

― Whether she likes it or not, she is a technocrat, and I’ll wager she’s dying to avenge her parents’ memory. These children are going to risk their lives too, or do they not worry you? -the woman returned an outraged look-. How much time do you reckon we have?

― Seventy-two hours, roughly.

― In these three days that remain, there is time for all of us to take risks in order to finish with Madeleine. Either we can or we can’t… -the couple held each other’s gaze for a moment.

― The outfits are ready -Scarlett interrupted, coming over to the children-. I understand both your points of view and I share them. At worst, Margareth is still one of us; her death is not deserved, but it is as valid as that of any of us or of those on the list. However, regardless of our decision, here are two people foreign to our world who have granted us the chance to believe in a different future. They have already put their lives in danger to reach this point; perhaps they have something to say -the couple’s expression changed. The three looked at the youngsters. Elisabeth shrank and, in an unintelligible stammer, said.

― I… I don’t know who you are… I don’t understand wh… what all this is about… but I felt my life was in danger, that… that I might die if I didn’t run… If it’s true that, even if we wish to put all this aside -she said, firmly clasping Arthur’s hand-, we’re obliged to take part in this race, I’d rather keep running for three days than have our lives ended without our being able to do anything to prevent it -the boy answered with a wink and said.

― Send that teletype to Margareth. What’s at stake is not only lives; we’re risking it to be free. She’ll understand the reasons. We’re wasting time -Thomas nodded.

Sitting down at the key, he connected the wires and sent the message. Margareth accepted the task. Thomas appended the names. Everyone waited. “Negative… negative… positive…” Only three of those names proved to be of great importance in the fabric of history. The addresses of the three subjects were typed out. Margareth thanked them for letting her take part. The technocrats smiled, satisfied.

― The names of those you will save belong to the forebears of men of the future: Grace Valentine, Jonathan Rockwood and Nikola Tesla. We don’t know what their fruit for humanity will be, but if we can do something for them, I believe it is our duty to act -he said. The youngsters were putting on the garments of concealment-. I’m sorry to burden you with so much gear, but you must carry the watch, the camera and the music box with you. The cases that hold them are made of the same cloth; no one will tell a trunk from a handbag. Put those inhibitors in your ears; they’ll keep the music from having any effect on you.

The facsimile will stay with us; knowing her plans, these verses are of no further relevance. Now it’s time for you to go and do your part of the work. Don’t come back here. As soon as we leave this house, Madeleine will know we have news of the watch and will come looking for us; with luck, that will buy you some time. From now on chance will be a companion to reckon with. Remember that you both possess a gift which, though dormant, descends from mentalists. Make use of it if it comes to that.

Thomas saw the youngsters to the door, took a key from the cupboard, where there were dozens of them, put it in the lock, and the door opened onto Livingston Avenue.

― This is the nearest we can leave you. Now go, my friends; we’ll meet at the end of this adventure.

Arthur and Elisabeth went down the steps of that luxurious house. On the other side the technocrats said goodbye, disappearing behind the great door. The young pair reached the last step. They looked at each other in fear.

― It seemed easier and less risky when we were in there -Arthur watched the crowd going to and fro, lit by the light of the street lamps that blurred their shapes.

― I won’t deny it, I’m frightened too… very frightened. The risk that we might both lose our lives wasn’t in my plans when I stole this damned watch. But I assure you it thrills me to be caught up in something I’d otherwise never have experienced. We have the chance to do something great, to know a world we never even imagined existed.

― Thrilling, yes. Though I’d rather have read of these escapades in a weekly than be the heroine. Now let’s go before I change my mind.

She pronounced, resolute, gripping her friend’s hand and plunging into the throng, where they passed unnoticed at a brisk walk. Carriages went in every direction, people crossed to and fro on their way home, and some gentlemen were draining their glasses of whisky in good company in the red-lit pubs that enlivened the night. Amid that vibrant crowd, Arthur and Elisabeth advanced uneasily, glancing nervously about, peering in search of Madeleine Giovanni’s acolytes so as to avoid them.

“THUMP”, the case containing the camera fell to the ground when they bumped into a gentleman they nearly knocked over.

― Well…! What a knock, youngsters. You should mind your step; I’m a stout enough man to be seen even at night.

― We’re so very sorry, sir. It was quite unintentional; we hope we haven’t hurt you or caused you any trouble.

― Don’t worry, young lady, it was nothing, but go more carefully or you’ll end up running into someone unwelcome.

― Yes, sir.

Arthur picked up the case and made sure everything was in its place. The two resumed their pace, ever more hurried.

― Elisabeth.

― Yes?

― Don’t you think what that man said is more than sensible? We’re attracting too much attention; we’re the only ones practically flying among all the passers-by -the girl slowed at once.

― You’re right, we’d better walk like everyone else and stop turning our heads looking for shadows; from outside it must look as if we’re running from the terrible bubonic plague. I’m so terrified I doubt I can hide it -the boy smiled.

They left the avenue for narrower, less busy streets. They had been walking for more than two hours now.

― Elisabeth. Are you sure you know where we’re going? We’re already a long way from anywhere and your sense of direction doesn’t seem to be leading us right.

― Excuse me, Arthur, but I know London like the back of my hand. My services have reached the remotest house, and though it’s true I don’t know how far we have to go to the street in question, I assure you we’re in the right area and will find the right address shortly -she was just finishing the sentence when they turned the corner and came upon a policeman chatting animatedly with a young woman. Arthur tugged at the girl.

― Come on, he’s bound to know the street and can tell us which way to go -a few yards from the couple Elisabeth threw her hands up to her face, covering it, stopping the boy short.

― What’s the matter, what are you doing?

― That girl… I know her, she’s a prostitute, if I go near she’ll recognise me.

― She won’t, we’re wearing the outfits, and even if she did recognise you, what could she do to us beyond saying hello? It will be an ideal chance to test how well these garments work.

Good evening, constable.

― Good evening, youngsters.

― Forgive us for disturbing you and interrupting your conversation.

― You’re not disturbing me, I was merely attending to this young woman’s queries; like you, she stopped to consult me. Tell me.

― I’ll be waiting upstairs to settle my queries, Mr Policeman. Don’t keep him too long -she said with a wink, and the woman went into the house. The policeman blushed, embarrassed.

― Sir, we’re looking for this street -the girl went on-. I know it’s around here, but it’s so long since I came that in the dark of the night all the streets looked the same to me and I lost my way -the officer grew thoughtful.

― Mmm…, the truth is I couldn’t say exactly, allow me a moment -he opened his pocket and took out a map-. Help me hold it. Let’s see, we’re here and the street you’re looking for is… here. You go straight on to the end of this road, then left, and right at the second turning.

― Thank you very much, officer. You’ve been a great help, I don’t know what we’d have done if we hadn’t met you -as she spoke, she let her gloves fall and bent to pick them up; the policeman bent too, coming face to face with the young woman’s low neckline. A moment Arthur used to reach into the trouser pocket where the officer had put the map, without his noticing a thing.

― Thank you again, you are a gentleman truly courteous to ladies. We wouldn’t wish to steal a second more from you; a very good night to you.

― Good night.

The youngsters were now walking only a few yards from their goal. The streets grew poor and narrow.

― Did you see that, Arthur? That’s the kind of officer who does the Crown proud. Men of great ethical and civic integrity in the service of the people, so long as the aggressor is a poor starveling and the victim a rich swine. Paradoxical that, on that premise, they end the day losing themselves in the slums seeking the favours of the cheapest whore. From the most mediocre to the Commissioner of Scotland Yard. Scum!

― Don’t get worked up. In the police, as in politics, banking or the Church, there must be all sorts, from people who hang up their uniform to become one more citizen, to corrupt men of scant morality, filthy up to the eyeballs.

― True, but what does it matter being an honest banker or policeman if you work for a body that serves interests imposed by Lord Money? They make love to us and then treat us as an undesirable social class… ARGGG! Did you see how he looked at my breasts? Arthur, don’t grow up.

― Look!! We’re here, 21 Little Young St. In that window, on the fourth floor, there’s a light. They’re awake, so we won’t have to get them out of bed -the front door was shut.

― Can you open it or would you rather I rang the bell?

― Give me a moment and one of those hairpins that hold up your hair -the girl handed him the metal and Arthur turned it a couple of times before the lock gave.

― You should carry your own; a good thief always goes equipped. I’m going to save the world and my hair’s a mess because of you -the boy pushed the door and with a bow made way for his friend.

― Pray, do come in, Lady Messy-Hair -he said in a plummy voice.

― Oh, too kind, my dear fellow! -she said in the same tone, pinching his shoulder.

― Owww! -she took back the hairpin to do her hair.

They crept up the narrow wooden stairs that led to the fourth floor.

― It’s here -said the lad, knocking on the door. The girl arrived a couple of steps behind.

― Why did you knock without waiting for me first?

― You only had four steps to go, it wasn’t so hasty -someone was looking through the peephole.

― Who is it?

― You see, we haven’t prepared anything…

― Oh dear…

― My name is Elisabeth and my companion is Arthur. We’ve come looking for Grace -the voice shouted.

― Grace, a certain Elisabeth and a certain Arthur are asking for you! Do you know them? Do you really think this is a time for people to come bothering us at home?

― I don’t know anyone by those names and I’m not expecting any visit -the woman came back behind the door.

― Go away, no Grace lives here. We don’t wish to be disturbed, it’s late.

― Wait…! It’s important. Madam, we’re photographers from the Daily Gazette, we’ve come to take your daughter’s portrait -a long moment in which the woman considered what to do. The youngsters bit their lips expectantly. The door opened; a lady watched from the doorway, scrutinising them from head to toe. She did not seem convinced, but their elegant appearance aroused her curiosity.

― Photographers from the Daily Gazette, eh…? And where’s the camera?

― Here, in the case -he answered, taking the device out of its cover. The woman seemed to accept it. She turned on her heel and went into the house, leaving the door open.

― Come on, don’t stand there, come in and shut the door, we haven’t got all night -and so they did. A hallway with a long corridor-. Go on to the end.

― And now what? -she murmured.

― I’m not that inventive. Take out the music box -they reached the entrance to the room. The lady was waiting beside a young woman rocking a little girl.

― These are the young people asking for you.

― Hello -she said kindly-. To what do we owe the visit?

― Erm… the reason… -Arthur did not know what to say and nudged his companion with his elbow.

― The reason is that we’d like to take your portrait while you listen to this melody.

― What?! -the woman exclaimed. With no time for more, her soured expression turned cheerful, sunk in the innocent notes.

― Arthur, set up the tripod. Is there anyone else in the family?

― Yes, my husband and my elder grandson. They’re in the next room.

― Call them, please, so they can be photographed as well.

― George, come here this minute and bring your grandson with you -despite her happy expression, she kept her severity. The man appeared grumbling, with the little boy hanging from his hand.

― What do you want, Mildred, who are these? -and all five were entranced.

― A little closer together, please -said Arthur, looking through the lens-. Two, one, “POOF” -the flash blinded the room and by the time sight returned the sitters were gone; a colour print emerged from the camera with the photograph of the five members. They smiled, blinked and seemed to breathe, holding the pose-. WOOOH!!! Did you see that? -exclaimed the boy.

― …Yes… Incredible…

― Let’s go! -he said in excitement, tapping the hands of the girl, who was still turning the handle. They packed up and left the house in all haste.

They were in the street. They had to take a cab and make for the railway station, but first they had to get back to the avenue. Arthur studied the map, looking for the quickest way to reach the nearest cab stand.

― We have to go down to the end of the street, turn right and then left. It’s fifteen blocks and the train to our next destination leaves in fifty minutes; if we don’t make it we’ll have to wait until tomorrow for the next one.

The two walked at a quick pace. The road was silent, most of the windows dark. After a few absent minutes, Elisabeth stopped.

― One moment, Arthur -she looked about-. Do you hear it?

― I don’t hear anything.

― Too much calm, don’t you think?

― I suppose it’s normal at this hour; we’re probably oversensitive, reading too much into every detail.

― I don’t know, Arthur. I feel a strange cold… Let’s go on -after they had covered some ten yards, the hooves of a trotting horse broke into the roadway; a cab was approaching-. Let’s take it, it’ll save us time and get us out of these streets that already have my hair standing on end.

― Of course -he answered his friend’s stricken face. He stepped off the pavement and hailed it. The cab stopped-. We’re in luck, it’s free -they climbed in-. Take us to the railway station, please. We’ll pay you more if you make haste.

The driver, rigid, assented with a slight movement of the head. He was dressed entirely in black: gloves, hat and a raincoat whose lapels covered his face. The boy watched for a moment, but the lunge of the horse as it started threw him back onto his seat. The girl’s words drew his attention away.

― Thank heaven we came across this carriage. I was going out of my mind feeling that at every step hundreds of shadows were following close behind us.

― Don’t fret over that; those henchmen don’t mean to follow us, they mean to find us and capture us. Have no doubt that as soon as they come upon us we’ll have no breath left. These are nothing but the unfounded suspicions of a pair of novices -he said with a smile. Hugging his friend, who was breathing more easily.

Stillness settled in the cab; both waited in silence, looking out of the window. A while later the driver came back to the boy’s mind; he put his head out of the window and watched him discreetly, along with the streets. He did not know that area, but something told him they were moving away from their goal. Slowly he drew back in and took Elisabeth’s hand; she was shivering.

― Don’t say anything -he whispered-, but I think…

― I know, I can feel that cold even more than when we were in the street.

― What do we do now? -he asked, huddling against her.

“BOOOOMMM!!!” A heavy blow against the cab lifted it onto its two side wheels. The youngsters fell out of their seats. The speed increased thunderously. “BANG, BANG!!” Again and again they crashed against something trying to stop them, or to overturn them. Another carriage at full speed was trying to draw near. Both drivers whipped their horses hard, swerving sharply at every corner to try to win the race.

― Arthur! -the girl shouted, trying to hold on to the seat as she was thrown about the cab.

― Arthur!! Elisabeth!! -cried a deep voice. The driver of the other vehicle was Thomas-. You have to get out of there! I can’t stop him; I haven’t hands enough to drive the horse and play the music box. I’ll pull alongside and you’ll jump across to this cab.

― You’re mad, we’ll be killed, we’re going too fast! -she shouted.

― You’ll be dead before dawn if you don’t get out of there!

Thomas whipped the nag, which regained the race, and closed in, colliding with the carriage. The shadow fought to throw the man from his seat with heavy blows, while he tried to keep control. “BANG, BANG!!” went the wheels. Arthur opened the door and stretched to grasp the handles of the other cab.

― Come on, youngsters, I can’t hold on any longer! “THUMP”.

― AAAAHH!! -screamed the boy, clinging to one cab by his hands and held to the other by his feet by Elisabeth.

― Arthur, hold on!! -cried Thomas, snapping the reins. The boy managed to jump across-. Come on, Elisabeth! -the girl, held back by fear, checked her jump at the last instant, but Arthur, alert, hauled on her with a great heave and flung her into the cab-. Arthur, I have the box in my pocket, take it and play it!

The boy climbed up to the man, who was struggling to shake off the acolyte. He took several blows on the back that all but hurled him to the ground. The boy put his hand into the heavy jacket and brought out the device; holding on to the rail with his feet, he played the music. “Ding, dong, dang, ding, ding, dong”. The shadow let go of the technocrat, who regained control of the carriage.

― Keep playing it. It won’t stop him, but it will slow his movements so long as he’s within range of the waves. Unfortunately I don’t think it reaches a hundred feet -Arthur sat down beside Thomas; Elisabeth caught her breath, watching the other cab fall behind-. I’ll take you to the station and you’ll catch the train there… But whatever possessed you to get into a cab driven by a shadow?!

― I’m sorry… I don’t know how it happened…

― Less than six hours in the street and they almost have you! I can’t believe you’re two thieves capable of stealing hundreds of pounds at the races and getting away scot-free, and you can’t go unnoticed for three days! -the boy fell mute, glassy-eyed, frightened by the man’s rage.

― Don’t talk to Arthur like that, we don’t deserve to be treated so!! -Elisabeth protested through the speaking tube-. Nobody’s to blame for this, we’re doing it as well as we can. If you’re absolutely certain you can do better, set us down and carry out our task yourself -the man fell silent and went on driving.

― I’m sorry, Arthur -he said, laying his hand on the boy’s shoulder-. So long shut away under these scars that I’ve become a monster. I hope you can forgive me -the boy nodded with a timid smile-. Elisabeth, can you hear me?

― Yes.

― I apologise to you too. I got very nervous, not only at the thought of failure, but at knowing you were in grave danger.

― …Mmm… Apology accepted.

― Thank you. Now listen: inside the cab you’ll already have noticed a large case.

― Yes, I see it.

― Open it. Inside there are two inflatables, a replica of each of you. Pull the ring.

― WOW!!! -two exact copies of the youngsters, with a small seam that gave them away, swelled until they were sitting beside the girl-. This is incredible, Thomas!

― Thank Scarlett, she’s lost none of her talent.

― …Thomas…

― Yes, Arthur?

― Look behind you -not only were several carriages crammed with shadows pursuing them; as many more were running over the rooftops.

― Bloody hell, what timing! Arthur, pass Elisabeth the box, it’s for you two -the girl took it through the window without allowing a second of silence-. Get into the cab. Throw those two capes over yourselves; just as we reach the station you’ll get out and pull the straps that hang over the shoulders. Don’t move until a couple of minutes before the train leaves. That cloth will turn you into a pair of trunks on wheels; I’ll pay to have you taken to the platform. But don’t speak and don’t move. Elisabeth, wind up those puppets by squeezing the air pump hanging from the sleeve -the youngsters obeyed; all was ready for when the moment came.

― Whoaaa…!! -the horse stopped-. Now, my young friends, make it -Thomas got down from the carriage, opened the door and brought the two young people out. On the other side, two large trunks resting on the ground-. Hey, you, lad! Here, ten pounds if you take those two big cases to platform number five.

― Of course, sir, as you say.

Thomas played the music box, followed by Arthur and Elisabeth; they walked down the street followed by the shadows, which passed unnoticed among the few travellers. The cab was searched, but there was nothing there. They all advanced behind the technocrat and his friends, ready to pounce on them at the slightest slip. A quarter of an hour later Thomas was surrounded on every side, so that sooner or later, with no possible escape, the acolytes would catch him and destroy the box. The ruse had been believed and the time gained was more than enough.

At the station the guard was giving his last call to the passengers when those two trunks boarded the train. A boy and a young woman sat down, breathing deeply, exhausted, in a carriage with few passengers. Opposite them a pair of men: one thin, of singular good looks, smoking a pipe, with an absent air; the other shorter and plumper, with a moustache, muttering as he leafed through the paper.

The guard came into the carriage asking for tickets. Everyone showed theirs.

― Ticket, please.

― Ahm… you see, sir, we boarded the train in a great hurry, we very nearly missed it, and that’s why my brother and I have no tickets.

― You see, miss, without a ticket you cannot travel. You’ll have to get off at the next stop.

― You can’t do that to us, we’re not getting off until the third station.

― Miss -he said sternly-, you must pay a fine and leave the train -the man with the pipe was watching.

― What if I pay you the price of the two tickets and we forget this mishap? -Elisabeth drew a pound from her cleavage, gently took the old man’s hands and, with a caress, left the note between his fingers-. This will be enough to settle the misunderstanding -the guard nodded without a word, issued two tickets and went on his way. Arthur watched open-mouthed.

― You’re very good. I didn’t know you could do that.

― Nor did I, it was improvised.

― I need to close my eyes, Arthur, wake me when we get there.

― It’s a deal.

Elisabeth leaned back in the seat and dropped off at once from weariness. There were two stops to go, but they took a little over an hour; they had time to rest. Arthur played with his fingers, turning an imaginary handle while he hummed the melody, his head resting against the glass, watching the landscape go by. His eyes were closing little by little. The two youngsters had fallen peacefully asleep when someone gently shook their knees.

― Mmmm…

― Youngsters, it’s your stop, we’re nearly there. Wake up quickly or you won’t reach your destination.

― Eh?!! -they exclaimed, dazed with sleep-. Where are we?

― On the train, you fell asleep. And if I’m not mistaken, from what I heard you say, this is your stop -the pair sat up quickly and walked towards the door.

― Thank you very much, sir, that was very kind of you, we owe you one -the gentleman winked at them by way of reply.

The train stopped and the two young people got off and went into the station hut. They had reached their next destination, a part of London unknown to them, barely sketched on the map and with not many people to ask in the small hours.

On the benches, a couple of sleeping passengers and the night watchman, a deaf old man, unable to remember whether they should turn right or left on reaching the crossroads.

― It’s all very dark. The houses must be about ten minutes away, but I don’t know if I dare walk without seeing where I’m treading -she said.

― It’s earth, stones and long grass, all wet. The worst is over: at most a twisted ankle and a couple of teeth knocked out. It’s not a bad idea to try -she listened stubbornly.

― I’m wearing heels, Arthur.

― It’s not my fault you’re not wearing footwear suited to the wilder parts of London. But you’ll agree with me that we can’t stay here.

― Be quiet and let me think -the boy opened his eyes wide, watching her with a look of surprise-. What are you doing, idiot?

― Elisabeth, I didn’t know you could do that -she gave him a sidelong look-. I don’t know if you’re thinking of something useful, but when you do your head lights up. Keep at it and we’ll get to the end of the road without tripping. Ha, ha, ha… -he laughed, doubling up.

― You’re a fool, I don’t know why you say that. I don’t laugh at you, knowing that the only great exertion you make is the farts you let off in your sleep -ashamed, he fell silent.

― That was a low blow.

― Tit for tat… Look, a lantern’s coming along the road -they both got up, waiting for the light. A couple of minutes later a well-padded lady, red-haired and kindly-faced, arrived in a cart drawn by a donkey-. Good evening, miss, forgive us for troubling you. We’re looking for this address; we’ve never been in this part before and the watchman couldn’t tell us which way.

― Good evening, children… John? He’s as deaf as a post; he knows the train’s arrived ten minutes after it’s gone. The address you’re going to is near, a good half-hour on foot, only with this darkness and the stones you could end up in hospital sooner than at the end of the road. I’m Rose, the local innkeeper. I come some nights looking for travellers to take to the inn; for three pounds I’ll take you as far as the inn and for seven you spend the night there, hot water included.

― Thank you very much for the information. For now, if it’s all right with you, we’ll accept the ride; we’re in a great hurry to find a certain person.

― Up on the cart, the three pounds in advance -the lad gave her the money and the animal set off-. And who are you looking for in that place? It’s nothing but a great store of maize and wheat; there’s nobody there but farmhands and the odd foreman.

― We’re looking for Jonathan. Have you heard of him?

― …Doesn’t ring a bell. But at this hour they’re all asleep, and I assure you that if you wake those clods at a time like this, the only thing you’ll get is a sound thrashing. More than thirty families are crammed into that building; men and women alike work separating and treating the grain. Many of them do it with a child on their back. I pity them. I don’t care for my job, but it makes me feel like a princess when I think of the inhuman things the day labourers suffer. Anyway…

As I was saying, I don’t think it’s a good idea to burst into the big house and wake them all, however important your business. Two young gentlefolk like you, I can’t imagine what you might want with people like that… -she waited for an answer. Elisabeth studied her; she did not seem likely to accept silence.

― He’s a distant relative we don’t know in person. A great-uncle of ours has died and it was his wish that a few possessions be passed on to them -the lady looked somewhat incredulous.

― There are better hours of the day for bringing such news.

― We missed the train and that was the reason for our delay.

― Where was I?… Ah, yes… well, I think you’d better stay and sleep at the inn. The streets aren’t properly lit and it’s a long way to the warehouses. You’ll surely end up lost; it’s cold and it rains every little while, it’s no place to fall asleep without a roof -the youngsters looked at each other; the arguments seemed sound. For all their haste, the conditions prevailed.

― All right, we’ll spend the night at your inn.

― It’s a good decision -the woman laughed slyly, satisfied-. You may think I’m fonder of money than a cat is of cream, but I’ll be hanged if what I’ve told you isn’t true. Had you arrived at an earlier hour there’d be no problem. But the world turns the same for everyone, rich and poor, and you’ll have to wait for dawn.

The youngsters bore the monologue stoically, longing to reach the room as soon as possible and leave behind the cold, the night and the talkative old woman. Money in hand in exchange for the keys to an old, moth-eaten room with a noisy floor and peeling wallpaper, but at least warm and dry. More comfortable than the place where they slept every day.

They were tired. A bed each, clean sheets, blankets. Arthur took off his clothes quickly and got into bed naked with the camera, with the watch and the music box under the pillow.

― It’s comfortable -she said, slipping in in her underwear.

― Yes -he answered, smiling. Elisabeth pulled the sheets right up, unbuttoned her blouse and let it fall to one side. She sighed, relaxed.

― It’s the first time we’ve slept in separate beds, and this peacefully in spite of everything. Now I know why people like money so much: it allows luxuries like this.

― Elisabeth, thank you for embarking on this journey with me. If you weren’t at my side I’d already have failed. I promise you that if we come out of this, you’ll never lack a good mattress to sleep on each night.

― Thank you, Arthur. Good night, sleep well.

― Good night, you sleep well too.

The first rays of light were just brushing in when the innkeeper entered the room and found the beds empty. The pair were already walking towards the warehouse. People were stirring; they came out of the doorways, here and there, preparing for the long day that lay ahead as it did every day. It was not hard to find the address; one of the many workers offered to walk them to the place.

They found themselves before a huge gate, ajar; inside, a great space full of hay and alfalfa. In a small room a gentleman was doing sums on a delivery note.

― Good morning, sir, forgive us for troubling you.

― I’m busy -he answered without looking up. Elisabeth stepped forward.

― Jonathan Rockwood, please.

― In the next shed. Go through that door at the back and it’s the first on the right.

― Thank you -the man carried on, unperturbed.

The youngsters reached the place; there were a great many workers gathering the bales and separating the grain.

― Excuse me, sir, Jonathan Rockwood, please.

― That one over there -they went up to him. The air was thick with flour dust.

― Good morning. I’m not mistaken if I say you are Jonathan.

― Yes, that’s me -he answered without stopping for a second.

― We’re photographers from the Daily Gazette; we’ve come to take a portrait of you and your family.

― You’ll have to forgive me, but I can’t leave my work. I don’t know why you’d want to photograph me, but you’ll have to wait until the day is over. In fact, I beg you to go; if they see me talking they might sack me, and that’s the last thing I want. Look for me at the gate when we finish -the youngsters looked at each other, hesitating.

― HEY!! You there, what are you doing, who gave you leave to come in?! -shouted one of the foremen ill-temperedly, coming up fast-. Who are these, Jonathan, do you know them?

― No, sir.

― Then why are you talking to them?! I ought to sack you!

― And you, what are you still doing here? Do I have to kick you out?

― Excuse me, sir, but Jonathan doesn’t know us, we’re from the Daily Gazette.

― I don’t give a fig where you’re from or whether you know each other or not! I said clear off!! And you, come to me at the break so I can remind you of the rules.

― Yes, sir.

― Look here! Don’t be so cruel, I’m telling you he doesn’t know us -the foreman raised his hand to strike, when Elisabeth quickly brought out the music box and played it. The brute went limp, as did Jonathan-. Apologise to this man.

― My apologies.

― Now repeat, “I’m a damned bastard” -she said.

― I am.

― I know you are, but I want you to say it.

― I am.

― Oh well… Go away.

― As you command, madam.

― Jonathan, have you a family?

― Yes, madam, my wife works here too. She’s expecting our first child.

― Very good, take us to her -the man nodded.

The three walked to the place where the woman did her work.

― Lily, these people have come to take our photograph.

― But Jonathan, what are you doing away from your…? -the music dazed her.

― Jonathan, please take the four of us somewhere we can be alone.

They walked towards a small room. Whoever crossed their path turned in surprise, and at once the melody made them forget whatever they had seen, and they went on with their work. They closed the door behind them; they were alone, and a small window lit the cubicle.

― Please stand there so that Arthur can take the photograph -she said without ceasing to turn the handle.

“FLASH!!”, the light lit up everything; a second later the slide appeared with the two sitters smiling. Everything went back into the case. They left the warehouse without attracting attention. Down the street, retracing their way, passing the inn; the woman watched them go with half a smile.

The way back to the station seemed short. They could make out the muddy, stony path along which they had travelled the night before. They were happy: they had succeeded in photographing the second name and the morning seemed to be starting well.

― I’m glad the innkeeper turned up yesterday. If we’d done it on foot and in the dark, we’d still be in the ditch calling for help -said the boy.

― The truth is that, for all her chatter, it was good to meet her. If she hadn’t warned us, we’d have gone to the warehouse, and I don’t want to think of that foreman’s reaction at being woken in the small hours.

― Yes, I’d give anything for the next photograph to be got as calmly as this one. We’ve the whole day ahead of us and I can’t imagine where to hide to escape those shadows.

― At least now, in the sunlight, their powers are diminished -she replied.

They reached the platform. The train would arrive in a little over an hour, so they waited sitting on a bench, calmly, breathing that passing peace of the quiet outskirts.

The whistles were accompanied by a column of smoke. The train slowed as it drew near. Some got off and others got on. This time they carried tickets to show the guard. There were not many passengers in the carriage. They were sitting in a bay of four seats, two of them empty. Over the seat in front showed a bowler hat and a cap.

― They’re the same gentlemen we travelled with yesterday -whispered Arthur.

― I’m glad; that way, when we get to London, they’ll wake us if we fall asleep.

― London is the last stop -grumbled the boy-. Besides, I don’t intend to sleep.

― Well, with your permission, I want to have a nap. I don’t know when we’ll be able to sleep again, so I’m seizing the chance.

― Do as you please, I’ll let you know if we have to run for it.

The girl leaned back. The quiet in the carriage, accompanied by the deafening clatter, was disturbed by the stifled exclamation of the man in the bowler. He was reading the newspaper.

― For God’s sake, don’t tell me you need to play your violin now! -the fellow lowered his voice. Arthur roused himself from his reverie and paid attention-. This goes beyond anything imaginable; I can’t believe there’s any meditation that could account for eight crimes with confessed culprits and one inexplicable disappearance.

― My dear friend, I tell you there’s something fishy here. They’re all people of public interest: politicians, businessmen, journalists, aristocrats. Someone close to them kills them and remembers nothing of how. I sense there’s a single criminal behind all this, and it’s more than possible that he uses techniques such as hypnosis to carry out his plans.

― If it weren’t that I trust fully in that talent of yours that solves everything, I’d call you naive.

― In part, yes. The disappearance of that family… I can find nothing to link it with the murders -the men fell silent.

A while had passed when Elisabeth’s head dropped onto the boy’s shoulder. Arthur stroked her hair, murmuring a melody. The man in the cap turned slowly and discreetly on making out the notes; out of the corner of his eye he recognised the young pair. Absorbed in his thoughts, he had not noticed them come in. He smiled, remembering how the girl had used her charms to get round the guard.

He returned again to his meditations. The monotony of the grey landscape. It was not long now to London. The guard came in and approached the pair, murmuring.

― Sir, a telegram has arrived for you -the man stood up, drawing deeply on his pipe. He nodded. The guard left them.

― What does it say, my friend? You have me on tenterhooks.

― The Commissioner informs us that there have been two more murders -he said, stressing the last words. Arthur listened nervously.

― And who are they?

― Edward Redwine and Dana Highlands.

The boy sighed with relief on learning that his third name was still alive. At that instant the man in the cap turned, catching the boy’s telltale expression. Arthur shut his eyes, pretending to sleep. The man said no more, sat down and continued the journey lost in thought.

The locomotive ceased its racket on arriving in London. It was mid-morning and countless people were walking to and fro laden with trunks, suitcases and bags. The guard passed through the carriage announcing the arrival. The pair of men left the compartment promptly. Elisabeth woke; they picked up the cases and at an unhurried pace walked towards their next encounter.

― He’s still alive. I managed to hear those two men talking about the murders already committed because of the book. While you were asleep they received a telegram announcing a couple more dead, and it’s also known that a family has disappeared. That gentleman was guessing, and he didn’t seem far off the mark, given that for society the technocrats don’t exist.

― That’s reason to be glad, Arthur. With luck we’ll make it three out of three and keep the watch for long enough. It’ll take us a little over two hours to reach the street where the King Charles Hotel is. We’ll have to go down to the Thames and walk along its bank until we come to the place. It shouldn’t be difficult.

We could get there sooner by taking a cab, but after yesterday’s experience, it being daytime and with so many people in the streets, it seems a better idea to walk and enjoy the stroll. What do you say, comrade?

― I think it’s a splendid plan, and while we’re about it, since we haven’t eaten yet, what do you say we buy some fish and chips and eat them on the way? As you rightly said, who knows when we’ll next be able to eat. Besides, I need no excuse, I’m starving.

― Living every second at breakneck speed under threat makes me think of those greasy chips as a delicacy. I accept the proposal.

The two walked the streets of London until they reached the riverbank, following its course towards the sea. In the place they were bound for there was a small tourist harbour where dozens of visitors from overseas and from Europe put in, arriving in the capital with a good wad of notes to lodge in the numerous hotels of the district. Yet on arriving at its door they realised that, amid so much ostentation, there was room for small, modest hotels, where guests came and went gallantly dressed. They went in to reception.

― Good morning. My name is Arthur and this is my colleague Elisabeth; we’re photographers from the Daily Gazette -he said, tapping the case that held the camera-. We have an appointment with Mr Nikola Tesla; if you’d be so kind as to let him know, you’d be doing us a great favour. Thank you.

― Good morning. I’m afraid the gentleman is out of London, giving one of the lectures for which he has come from America. I don’t know what arrangement you had, but he won’t be back at the hotel until eleven tonight, since the train leaves the station at ten minutes past ten.

― Oh…! -he exclaimed, wavering-. We must have misunderstood the time of our meeting. All the same, I wonder whether you’d mind if we sat in the lounge until he arrives.

― I warn you that there are a great many hours until the said return.

― True, but our only job is to do that interview and take his portrait. That’s what we’re paid for and we can’t go back to the office without our work, whatever the hour -Elisabeth replied.

― As you wish. Lunch, tea and dinner are available in the café.

― Thank you very much. We’ll make use of it.

― Thank you; let me know if you need anything -the receptionist concluded, returning to his duties.

― Blast! -he exclaimed, dropping onto the sofa-. Why did this have to happen? Until eleven, no less. It’s one in the afternoon; that’s too many hours to be stuck in here, it’s maddening. It’s time enough for him to be murdered.

― Arthur, we’ll wait here quietly; we’re safe. We’ll read the papers, drink tea, and pray that the shadows don’t find us and that Nikola stays alive.

― … -the boy accepted with a deep sigh-. What do you think his lectures are about?

― I don’t know, but they must be something important if his name’s on our list.

― …I suppose…

The hours passed tediously in that lounge, where the only entertainment was reading, accompanied by refreshments and the constant worry of being surprised by the acolytes.

The wall clock struck seven in the evening. The maître d’ came over to the youngsters.

― Sir, madam, I regret to inform you that the train that was to bring Mr Tesla has been cancelled owing to an accident on the line.

― AN ACCIDENT!! -Arthur leapt up, crying out-. Have people been killed, what else do you know?!

― Allow me to explain, young sir. Owing to the rain, a small stream has flooded the line. The railway authorities believe that by dawn tomorrow the water will have gone down and will let the train through. It was Mr Tesla himself who let us know of his absence and the reasons for it. In consequence of which, I invite you to give up your wait and come back tomorrow -the boy sat down, relieved. Elisabeth remained thoughtful.

― Is there a room we could take for tonight? -the man nodded.

― Together or separate?

― Same room, separate beds. Thank you.

― I must ask you to step over to reception and complete the booking, if you’d be so kind. Thank you.

― Thank you.

The youngsters signed with a false name. They took a room for that night for twenty pounds. They shut themselves in.

― Are you tired?

― … -she smiled-. Less than yesterday, but my nerves are such that my head is seething and my heart is pounding; it’s an exhausting feeling.

― I feel the same. But I’m glad to be able to spend the night sleeping peacefully rather than running. Whether we get the photograph or not, we’re successfully carrying out one part of the task: keeping the watch away from Madeleine.

― So far we’re doing well… I wonder what’s become of Thomas; I hope he managed to escape the henchmen.

― She won’t do them more harm than she’s already done. I’ll wager that if she’s caught them she’s keeping them alive, in view of events. She needs them.

― God grant it’s so, Arthur. Good night, sleep well.

― Good night, you too.

The night passed quiet, warm, silent. They slept peacefully. So much so that the morning had long since broken and Big Ben was striking nine. Elisabeth woke, dressed without a sound and went out into the corridor. She went down to reception.

― Good morning.

― Good morning, miss, how can I help you?

― I’d like to know whether Mr Tesla is back yet.

― Yes, miss. He finished his breakfast a few moments ago and went to his room. Would you like me to let him know you’re here?

― I think it would be better if I announced myself, if you’d be so kind as to tell me the number of his room.

― Mr Tesla is on the third floor, room fourteen.

― Thank you.

The young woman went up the stairs and into the room. She drew the curtains, waking her companion.

― …mmm…

― He’s here, we’ve work to do -the boy leapt out of bed.

― When did he get back? What time is it?

― I don’t suppose he’s been back long. It’s nearly ten. We’ve slept more than ever.

― These beds are terribly comfortable; I think I could sleep on them for a whole week -he said, finishing dressing.

With all the devices on their backs they went down to the floor below and came to door fourteen. “Knock, knock, knock”…, there was no answer. “Knock, knock, knock”.

― Is this the room?

― Yes.

― Sure?

― “Three fourteen” was what the receptionist said. Mr Tesla, are you in your room… Mr Tesla?! -Elisabeth was about to knock again when Arthur stopped her. There was no one in the corridor.

― Give me a hairpin.

The boy turned the knob and the door stood open. They looked inside; it seemed empty.

― Perhaps he’s gone out…

The two went in, closing the door behind them. Elisabeth walked slowly towards the window, looking about uneasily.

― I’ve a strange feeling too, Elisabeth. We ought to get out of here -the girl ignored the boy.

― Did you hear that?

― No.

― Inside the wardrobe.

She drew near, listening hard. A tiny stifled cry. The girl passed her hand over the handle and with a sharp movement opened the wardrobe.

― AAAAAAHHH!!! Run, Arthur!!

― ELISABETH!!

The shadows fell upon the youngsters. The man tumbled out, held by the darkness that gagged him fast despite his efforts to break free.

― The music box!!

― I’m trying!

Shouted the girl, struggling to bring her hands together to play the device; an acolyte held her arm, preventing it. Arthur fought, rolling on the floor, while a couple of shadows tried to take the watch from him.

― Elisabeth!

― Arthur!

― The music!

“CRAAAAASH!!!” the door burst open with a blow that tore it off its hinges. The man in the cap came in, followed by his companion, after kicking the door; he stared in astonishment and, with no time to draw breath, the girl shouted.

― Play the music!

And she threw the box, which he caught in mid-air in a leap that dodged a shadow’s hand; at once the notes sounded and the henchmen slowed until they were almost motionless, though their strength was undiminished.

― Keep turning, please! -cried the girl.

― The camera, the camera!

Shouted Arthur, seeing one of the cutthroats drawing near it. The man in the bowler threw himself to the floor and took it in his hands.

― Take his photograph, hurry, take that gentleman’s portrait!

A portal of darkness was beginning to open. He focused the lens, the flash lit the room, and Nikola disappeared under the men’s astonished gaze. The printed image, with its sitter smiling. The shadow reached the device and crushed it to pieces.

― Take the photographs and guard them with your life! Don’t lose them! -shouted the young woman.

The portal sucked in the darkness, which dragged the youngsters with it, and vanished. The two men were left in the room, astounded.

― I still don’t know what happened.

― Keep those photographs safe, my friend. It seems to me that, after what we’ve seen, the hypnosis conjecture loses ground and gives way to a new hypothesis. I sense that if our friends come out of this alive, we shall meet again to return to them those portraits that seem to breathe.

― How did you know they might know something?

― The boy’s expression gave him away, but I never for a moment imagined anything like this. I still haven’t got my breath back…

Ω

― It seemed a good plan. After all, you had nothing to lose, and to achieve your end you didn’t hesitate in the slightest to gamble the lives of two innocents. When all’s said and done, they’re nothing but the dregs of this post-Cro-Magnon society in which we unfortunately live and which has so scorned the technocrats. Am I wrong? Were it not for those two brats, we wouldn’t be gathered here today -she spoke seated on a throne, in an empty hall of black marble, lit by oculi in the ceiling. Before her the four technocrats knelt on the floor, held by tentacles of gloom. The figure of a man standing could be glimpsed behind the wall of obsidian-. Those children, helped by your devices, have very nearly cost me my life. The life of the great Empress of Shadows, Madeleine Giovanni, hanging on the skill of a common pickpocket capable of holding at bay a horde of darkness that until recently I believed efficient.

I’ve preferred not to look in the mirror, but my parchment hands, my voice, this weariness, are symptoms that the hours are slipping from me; brief, I sense, those that remain. Insufficient, in any case. Unlike you, Margareth has a whole life ahead of her, and however great the hatred she feels for me for putting her parents to death, she is a weak soul. It was enough to flood her heart with the bleakest darkness for her to give me the names of the three fortunates you decided to save so selectively from my elegant devastation. You may thank this girl, for she is the reason my acolytes have finally found your little friends.

I sense they’re about to arrive -a circle of blackness formed in the centre of the hall. The figures appeared, carrying the youngsters.

― Let go of me, I tell you!! -shouted the boy. Madeleine went up to them.

― Do you remember me?

― No!

― Am I so old that you don’t know who I am? -Arthur looked at her.

― It’s you!

― Yes, the young woman from whom you unjustly stole the watch.

― You’re too late. It’s what you deserve -murmured Elisabeth. The woman tore the device from the boy’s pocket, returning a threatening look.

― James, readjust it and give me breath again. If you do, I’ll pardon your brother, your friends and the children. If not, I’ll end the lives of all of you, I’ll break the watch, and in the little that’s left to me I’ll find the way to be eternal, young.

― The watch is beyond mending, I’ve told you already. Without notes, without memory, I’m not who I was. Break it now and live the time that’s left to you like any other human. I offer you “The Clock of Ages”: you’ll look young, though you won’t be everlasting.

― ENOUGH!! James, if that’s your last word, what do you say we begin by killing Robert?

― Madeleine, don’t do it.

― Don’t worry, James, we’ll meet in the next life -said his brother-. We haven’t failed; even if she breaks the watch, she can have few years left. We’ve managed to finish the mistress of shadows. Time will take its revenge in the end -the woman looked at the device between her fingers.

― James, I know you can mend it.

― I can’t, Madeleine. Destroy it before it steals your last breath.

The woman closed it between her hands, squeezing; rays of light and of darkness danced, blazing, disintegrating the device, which crumbled to dust. Her breathing quickened, her eyes bulged, she put her hand to her chest and fell to her knees.

― WHAT’S HAPPENING TO ME, JAMES?!!

― You’ve lived so long bound to that invention that its destruction is altering the temporal fabric of your person. You’ve been young for so long that age is striking hard at your body, now mortal once more. You’ll come out of it alive and you’ll get used to it.

― JAAAAAAAAAMMMEEEEES!!!!! THIS ISN’T RIGHT… TIME HASN’T STOPPED… I’M STILL AGEING!!

― It’s not possible, the watch has been destroyed!

― JAMES, DO SOMETHING, I’M DYING!!

― Ha, ha, ha! -laughed Robert-. You’re forgetting something, Madeleine: “The Verses of Chronos” is made up of two pieces, the watch and the book. Perhaps by destroying “The Word of God” time will stop running for you.

The woman dragged herself to the throne. Arthur ran to the book and seized it.

― NOOO, BOY!! -shouted James.

― AAAARTHURRRR!! -cried the girl.

A halo of darkness blazed, destroying the book and charring the left arm of the boy, who fell to the floor in a faint. Elisabeth gathered him into her lap.

― Bravo, old friend! Do you feel life returning to you?

― ROBERT DRAKE, I’LL KILL YOU AND HOUND YOU THROUGH HELL!! -she screamed, writhing in pain.

― Robert, make it stop, let her live!! That’s enough.

― No, James! It’s not enough. She must atone for all her crimes. The universe demands balance. To deal death, she had to write on the pages in ink of blood, and now all the lives she stole are claiming their time. I can do nothing against the cosmic laws.

― JAMES… DON’T LET ME DIE!!

― Madeleine, set me free!! -the obsidian cracked, freeing the man, who ran to her and held her in his arms-. Madeleine, I’m with you, be calm.

― I don’t know why you shed your tears for her!

― Enough, Thomas!

― Look at what she’s done to us!

― BE QUIET!! -he cried in fury-. Madeleine… -he whispered-, I’m sorry.

A loud hum deafened the hall; all watched, transfixed; the image warped. An interdimensional opening gaped in the hall. The noise came from the other side. A silhouette could be glimpsed; its skin, in the half-light, looked scaly. Its distorted voice was barely comprehensible.

― Mestiza, I appear in the hour of your release to offer you eternal life in exchange for your power!

― Out of here, go back to your world!!! -shouted the technocrats. At a gesture of the creature’s hand they were hurled against the wall.

― Madeleine, don’t accept, please. I’ll go with you, we’ll die together, but don’t do it, don’t become one of them, don’t end your life that way. Do it for that woman I once loved with all my soul and for whom I keep that love even today. Pay no heed to that monster; its offer is nothing but hell. Die with dignity -the woman looked at him, letting a tear fall.

― It’s lovely to know -she murmured, dying- that someone still feels something for the woman I once was. But the shadows have consumed me and I’ve scarcely any humanity left. I can’t make worse what’s done and I don’t wish to die. I’m sorry.

Arcane! I accept the bargain, my life in exchange for my power.

― So be it!

The old woman’s body convulsed, rising into the air, turning into a mass of darkness and flesh that little by little evaporated. The female figure re-formed in ether in the creature’s arms.

― Now you are pure essence. You will acquire new powers with which you will serve me to the end of mastering life, making me emperor of the universe and lord of the arcana!

The hole closed, taking the creature and the woman with it. The shadows faded. Silence. The palace was free of the spell.

― James, let’s go -Robert raised his brother, who remained staring into space.

― They’ve gone… she’s gone…

Thomas carried Arthur in his arms; the wound was not bleeding; the boy remained dazed.

The following afternoon, rested after sleep, they all gathered in the drawing room. Elisabeth, James, Robert, Margareth, Thomas, Scarlett and Dorothy. Along the corridor a new figure appeared.

― How is he, William? -asked Thomas.

― All has gone well. The mechanical arm has adapted, and his mental ability lets him master the device without training -the boy appeared beside the man.

― Arthur!! -they all exclaimed on seeing him on his feet, with his metal arm and with a smile.

― How are you, my friend? -asked Elisabeth, hugging him, weeping for joy.

― Very well. I feel odd, but well. Thank you.

― I’m very glad you’re in perfect health, Arthur -said Robert. James stepped forward.

― Many things are going to change for us from now on; too many the important decisions. But first I’d like to make use of “The Clock of Ages” and mend, illusion though it be, part of the harm done. Come closer, my friends.

The five technocrats surrounded the clock; the hands spun swiftly backwards and skin grew taut, bowed backs straightened, scars and wrinkles disappeared. James stopped time; they looked twenty-nine, the age at which they had ceased to be inventors.

― Now is the moment -James went on-, given the circumstances, to recover our lineage; the world needs the technocrats to face the evil that is coming. Moreover, I believe it is the right occasion to join the technocrats in brotherhood with the other lineages: the mentalists, among whom we already count two great friends, and soon we must establish ties with the necromancers.

If all those present agree, I declare today the beginning of an era in which we shall be masters of our origins and gifts. Today is the day to refound the TOC, to which we shall soon add two new initials -they all nodded-. If so, my friends, there’s no time for rest; we must gather as many as we can and train all those of dormant talent to prepare ourselves for the new threat.

Elisabeth, Arthur, a thousand thanks, infinite thanks; we are tremendously grateful to you, there are no words for your deed. You’ve done too much, and it’s true that we need your talent, but you may leave if that is your wish. You’re under no obligation; live a new life without want, it is our gift.

― We’re staying -they answered in unison, with a smile.

― Ha, ha, ha. Bravo! I’m very glad. If it suits you, take a few days off while we prepare this beginning, and while you’re at it, visit that detective and his friend so that they can return the photographs to you. Tempus fugit, my friends.

The Traveller of the Stars

Time does not pass for me…

…it simply passes me by.

It is one of those grey days…

…the mind races and then everything seems to go more slowly,

…one of those on which sleeping is a sound solution and the bed proves itself the best ally against the autumn cold.

The air thickens…

…one acquires the gift of glimpsing,

…the knowledge that the inevitable must happen.

The situation we were living through seemed to have a bad ending, yet it still clung to a hope that did not exist.

That night must have been the wildest moment of my life. When I read his letter I could do nothing but brandish my guts and scream like a woman possessed, beating her head against the wall with the sole purpose of stopping time.

Guilt floods every corner of my being. The anxiety “in crescendo” at the proven inability to slam the door on that brazen feeling of loneliness, of affliction, of helplessness.

The eyes open wide, the heart beats out of control, the mind races and the noise deafens. Only at that moment am I left at the mercy of suspended time. The tears try to come out, unable, encysted.

Sleep does not elude suffering; everything that frustrates us, that scourges us, takes shape again and again so as to bring home to us our boundless need to resolve every conflict that besieges the soul.

At that very instant the collapse carries me to catharsis; I want only to be switched off, to travel to the past and wake in a happy moment, or simply not to wake.

An overdose of sleeping pills leaves me like a vegetable.

Waking the next day. Waking and remembering. And as the only idea that occurs to me, cleaning the whole house with the vacuum cleaner, dazed by the machine’s noisy motor. And then, an hour later, light-headed, mop at the ready, with the bucket full of soap and water, scrubbing with such ardour that the tiles seem to crack while sweat mists my forehead.

After switching on the hi-fi, I put on the compilation cassette with the saddest songs I had managed to find; track number three, “Standby” by Extremoduro, can be heard all down the corridor. I begin to sing. I should like to be the taciturn poet of those lines.

Slumped against the wall, eyes brimming, strengthless, mortally wounded, while Robe goes on talking about me. The music keeps playing, but no longer listened to.

Wretched. The senses cancelled. I do not feel cold; the awkward position does not hurt. The sensations are so many that this is the only, the true moment, a brief but longed-for instant in which everything is reduced to zero.

I do not know whether I am alive or dead. The gaze blurs, and to evaporate is accepted in exchange for keeping that state in which one has no name, no past and no future. One is only energy detached from one’s own body.

The parenthesis of peace is brief. A tremulous shiver runs down my spine and I wake from the trance. With resolute calm, I repent and cry out for forgiveness, I beg and plead for a second chance to do things properly.

Little by little, memory starts up, saturating itself, and I appear again. Loathing boils, harrying the stupid woman who did not know how to love, who forgot to give thanks every day. Remembering to pray when all is lost.

The certainty of my life. I believe myself wonderful, kind, brilliant, forgetting the inversely proportional quantity of filth that corresponds to vain insolence.

I feel alone, and when the wall has come down after so much beating against it, I get up disoriented; I do not know who I am, why I am here. And swift as lightning, the light of knowledge pierces me. Suddenly, before the mirror, I am not that woman.

I never wanted to hurt my family, and least of all the man I love.
