I get up knowing I have found myself, if only in part, to discover that I am even more lost than before…

At that precise moment I am cleaning the bathroom and do not know how I got there. Pulling the thread, I conclude that one wipe of the cloth over the basin brings back those adolescent years when I was judged strong, tall and old enough to lend a hand at home. I look towards the corridor, empty; no one lives in the house, and at that instant I miss, if nothing else, my mother’s maddening shouts.

Everything is tidied away; I am sitting in the armchair and the telephone hangs from my hand.

― Mum.

― Yes?

― How are you?

― Blah, blah, blah…

For the first few minutes she reproaches me for going so long without ringing her. The reproach gives way to heartfelt concern. The only undying worry, the one you feel as your own when the man you have shared ten years of your life with has become a stranger, when friends are conspicuous by their absence.

I stay on the telephone while she tells me everything she has done from getting up to going to bed, the family gossip, the neighbours’ gossip, everything… Nothing escapes that feverish mind that needs to make itself heard. I listen without interest to what she says, but gratified to know she is there, to have her close.

More than an hour later the end of the conversation arrives without my noticing, and I realise it when the famous question comes.

― Well, that’s that… and you, what are you having for lunch today?

― Ah… vegetables and cuttlefish.

― Very good, I like you to eat healthily. Look after yourself and ring me more often.

― Of course. Thanks, Mum. I love you. A kiss.

― And one for you. I love you too.

Lying back on the sofa, I am a little calmer. Curled up on myself, with the echo of the maternal voice as my only shelter, I feel colder than ever.

I am exhausted; I cannot think any more. I look at the clock; it is time to leave for the office.

I look at the marks on the wall and wonder how my skull withstood such an onslaught. I imagine I wiped out all the neurons that were meant to be of use to me from sixty onwards.

Nor do I know what stopped me; perhaps the unconscious certainty that one more blow would end up doing me harm. I let myself fall, strengthless, dazed, stumbling against the corner of the bed and marking myself with a scar of about three centimetres on the knee, the only tangible reminder of that night.

I was alone, and nobody knew anything of what had happened, except the neighbours. The next day I pass some of them, but only one dares to ask.

― I don’t want to pry, but yesterday someone was screaming; it came from your flat. We were worried, I very nearly called the police.

― I’m so sorry, it was me. It hadn’t happened since I was a child; I had an epileptic fit and it didn’t stop until I passed out. Luckily no harm was done.

― Goodness…! It sounded as though you were really suffering. If it happens again, and you’d like, leave us a set of keys so we can help you.

― Thank you very much, but it won’t happen again. After this, I’ll go back to taking my tablets. I’m grateful for your concern; I feel easier knowing you’re there.

― Well, as you see fit, girl. Look after yourself. We’re here for whatever you need. See you later.

― See you.

The answer was not prepared, but I am greatly surprised by the inventiveness I displayed. It may not be wholly believable, but it is easier to lie than to explain the truth.

I leave home with my forehead grazed and my eyes swollen. My thoughts are so many that their noise prevents me from hearing a single one of them. And the mental maelstrom lets my heart make itself felt down to the last capillary.

My will. I am so unhappy that I would not mind turning into stardust at a gust of wind. That feeling floods me; it neither grows nor shrinks; it has cancelled me out.

By the time I realise, I am already climbing the stairs to the office.

I work as a copywriter and proofreader in a law firm, and this afternoon, given the circumstances, I do not care whether the texts are in German or in Arabic.

Although I read more than fifty pages, I do not take in a single letter of them.

― Are you all right, Elina? -he asks, shaking me-. That’s the fourth time I’ve said your name.

― Oh… sorry, I was intent on my reading and didn’t notice.

― You don’t look well, girl.

― I haven’t slept well, that’s all.

― You’ve worked for me for three years; we know each other well enough for me to tell you’re trying to hide something. Those bumps on your forehead, your skin, your eyes. I hope nobody did that to you.

― No…! Don’t think that, it was me. I’m more than enough on my own for certain things.

― All right, I don’t wish to know the reason, but please, don’t let it happen again. Take the afternoon off and don’t come in for a couple of days. I need you focused.

Until then, look after yourself.

― Thank you, Eugene. You’re a good boss.

― And you a great woman. Whatever it is, cheer up; nothing and no one is worth a punishment like that.

I do not know what to do. Going home is not the immediate option. Being in the office is a good choice for letting the hours run by, but it carries risks. All I can do is leave work and walk the streets aimlessly.

I make for the city centre, where at least the crowds and the deafening noise end up giving me the usual headache that goes with my unconscious agoraphobia.

After a macramé exhibition and a commemorative one on Japanese comics, full of freaks in costume, after visiting every clothes shop and trying on a great many shoes, after leafing through books in several bookshops and going over the coffered ceiling of the cathedral, I end up circling Independence Square a couple of times, to finish sitting in a café drinking five cups of long black coffee. I do not know whether the caffeine has anything to do with it, but the cold sweats and the burning sensation make my body something foreign.

The afternoon is ending and all that is left to me is to go home, taking the longest way there. I cross Concord Avenue on foot, and two hours later the door of my flat opens to receive me in silence and darkness, solitary and narrow. I shut it. I go straight to the medicine cabinet and take a couple of sleeping pills. Without even taking off my clothes, I let myself fall on the bed.

Face down, exhausted, wanting to finish at this point, I have no will to hurt myself, to cry out. A deep sensation. After the fit of rage, calm refloats old guilts, behaviours that ought never to occur. Fingers pointing straight at me as solely responsible for what has happened. Forgetting the effect of such pills, in an imperious will to cancel the conscious mind, I throw the doors wide open to the most powerful self. Unable to wake, subject to the true order that governs life, the subconscious, the one that seems to know with certainty everything about what we believe we do not know.

And for no apparent reason I begin to think that no one has been responsible, that it has come about by force of a push from both sides. I notice the clear-sighted certainty that lights me for the first time, blinding, letting me know that I have been a complete idiot, not to use a stronger insult. Kneeling before the omniscient alter ego, I take pity on myself and long for divine liquidation as I fall asleep on account of the narcotics.

I walk in solitude. The new moon is absent from a completely black sky. The faint orange light of the street lamps lights the streets of the outskirts that I had walked so many times before, hanging on his arm and in the company of my dog, on those evenings of happiness. All of it known, yet transfigured. The silence is accompanied by a soft breeze. Once more I travel the route I used to take every evening, by now a healthy habit: walking to spread out the thoughts crammed beneath the walls of home. I recognise pavements, the roadway, the abandoned warehouses, the groves of ash and holm oak.

I go forward calmly. Thought cancelled. I had not noticed, but I have managed to leave aside the terrible “hum” of vital apathy; I believe I am happy. I confine myself to enjoying the walk, to breathing that peace, when the building catches my attention. I do not remember having seen it before, though in that very spot I would have sworn I had seen an industrial unit. Looking at it closely, I make out its lines in the dark, the forms of an old, ruinous and abandoned railway station drawing themselves in iron, marble and glass.

The strangeness lasts barely an instant. I go on walking to the end of the street. Left or right? I do not like to plan the route, and every corner invites a different course. However, I opt for the right, drawn rather by the clatter of horses’ hooves arriving from the distance. Not that it strikes me as unheard of; the outskirts are close to the countryside, and at times I had seen riders and mounts flirting with the asphalt. What is truly strange is the thick black fog that hides the street. I stand in the middle of the road watching the nothingness, listening to the hard beating of the hooves, nearer and nearer. Suddenly, a carriage drawn by four jet-black horses bursts out at full speed. I leap aside, falling to the ground. It has no driver, and as it passes me I can see that someone inside meets my gaze.

The coach stops and the door opens. A figure hidden under a black cloak steps down and comes towards me. I can see only the gleam of its eyes. I want to get up and put distance between us, but I stay paralysed, motionless. It reaches me and stops as it brushes the tips of my feet. Its eyes stare at me; fear turns to curiosity, as if I knew of that stranger.

Its body stretches towards my face, and when its eyes are so close to mine that its breath brushes my skin, I see within them the greatness of the universe. I feel myself floating among the stars; it is a magnanimous sensation of calm.

“…Elina…”, the echo of my name. With the next blink the face appears before me; I am left hanging, held by a couple of fingers that push up my chin. It stares at me. Its complexion redraws itself constantly, vaporous, so that I cannot say whether it is man or woman, child or old.

― You have looked upon the place from which we all come. You may return to it, knowing yourself responsible for your extinction, or go back to your cathedral and take on your destiny -I say nothing, but the fear of death becomes tangible-. You have chosen. But remember that the next time you wish to give up, there will be no chance of return.

The figure fades and I fall to the ground, stunned.

When I open my eyes again all is dark. I watch, motionless. Lying down, I feel the touch of the sheets; I switch on the light. My room. I need a moment to digest the memory of what I have lived through. I recall it all in detail. I hear footsteps in the corridor; my mother comes into the bedroom.

― Elina, what are you doing awake, little one? It was only a dream; tomorrow when you wake you won’t remember a thing and you’ll get up fresh and happy -she kisses my forehead-. Rest now, good night.

She turns off the light and I fall asleep that very instant.

Ω

The morning light comes into the room; it is an orange, warm light; I have slept too long. I take the watch from the bedside table: it is a quarter past five in the afternoon. The bed is soaked with sweat.

I get up to go to the bathroom and wash my face. I stare at myself, move closer to the mirror, scrutinising myself; I remember the dream and a shiver runs through my body. That figure in black, the horses, the railway station… my mother… I do not know why I was remembering her, but, uneasy, I pick up the telephone and ring her.

― Mum.

― Hello, Elina, how are you, love?

― All fine, Mum. And you?

― Me?… well, the same as ever, at home. Work this morning, making lunch, and now watching my soap. And by the way, next week I’ve a couple of days off, I could spend them with you two.

― I’ve a lot of work, Mum. I’m a bit stressed. If you come we’ll have little time together. Let a few days go by and I’ll let you know.

― All right, but don’t take too long to tell me, I don’t want to be left without a train ticket.

Her voice comforts me, and as I hang up I remember her presence while I slept. A peculiar thing, the figure of a mother, capable of driving Buddha himself to distraction and at the same time the last name one utters before succumbing to the worst of judgements.

I sit on the sofa looking at the sky through the window. Remembering every fraction of what I lived. To contemplate the immensity of the universe and feel the place we come from leads me to forget the fear of everything except going back without having walked the road; now I sense with certainty what we are sent for.

The figure did not represent a nineteenth-century death; it is not the reaper who ends everything and carries you off to the underworlds. That figure was benevolent and tyrannical at once. It showed me that, should I give up, I would be welcomed home, and at the same time made me understand that my wish was to go on. The encounter makes me think again: in spite of the great chaos that disorders my whole being, in spite of the ruins that my ego and my past life have become, in the deepest place there beats the pure, joyful and hopeful heart of the little girl I still am. I understood that in the greatest darkness the light goes on existing within, and that everything that grows from it is unalterable and authentic.

The tears well up without ceasing. The blow has been so hard that I feel my ego breaking. The crack runs from side to side and threatens to shatter the shell that until then had been me. I am very sad, lost. At last I understand who I have been and which of my acts have led me here. I take in my guilt not to scourge myself but to learn from it, since my greatest punishment consists in finding myself in the present moment. Alone, abandoned.

The last tear runs through the beginnings of a smile. I feel strangely well.

My separation, my parents’ separation, my grandmother’s death, the absence of my dog, the frustration of a job without prospects. All of that in less than a year was carrying me towards an inner cataclysm that ends in a severe depression. But what I take for an insurmountable obstacle is no more than the beginning. I have been the victim of a revelation.

The new experience opens before me, and the feeling of strange cheerfulness is due to knowing that I could begin everything again with the certainty of doing it right. The mistakes are seen in the third person, and at that moment I know. I want to go back to the past and make amends, but that wish is an impossibility. Nevertheless I am glad that from this instant on all will be well.

The afternoon goes by peacefully. My breathing is slow and my befuddled mind does not want to move from the sofa. Night comes and tiredness wins. I sleep curled up in the sitting room, quiet until the next morning.

The alarm goes off, I wake, I feel well enough to try to do my job. I get on the bicycle and pedal to the firm. Countless folders stuffed with sheets of paper await on my desk. Before I sit down Eugene calls me into his office.

― You didn’t answer my call yesterday. Are you all right?

― Yes, I’m fine. I was asleep all day and I took the phone off the hook. Thank you for troubling. I think I’ll be able to concentrate on the texts.

― You have me rather worried. We’ve a lot of work; if you’re not up to it, I can call Svetlana to stand in for you for a few days. I owe you some days’ holiday.

― Much obliged, boss. I’ll try to meet today’s target; otherwise I may ask you for the rest of the week.

― As you think best, but if you decide to stay, I need you at two hundred per cent. Agreed?

I go back to my seat and try to lose myself completely in the texts.

At five in the afternoon the working day ends. I have corrected enough articles not to have remembered my present reality even once. But the pace at which I am working is slower than usual.

― Eugene, a good many of the folders are done, but I couldn’t manage it all. Perhaps it’s better that Svetlana takes care of the rest on Thursday and Friday, if you’ve no objection.

― No problem, go home and get better. We’ll see each other on Monday.

― I wanted to know whether you’d let me take some work home; it helps me switch off to keep my mind busy.

― Take whatever you like from the ninety-eight filing cabinet. It needs a thorough going-over.

― Thanks, see you on Monday.

― Look after yourself -I answer with a smile.

That afternoon the bicycle does its job. Riding round the city aimlessly, wearing out my muscles until, by chance, I arrive home.

Mr Silence awaits me on one side and Mrs Solitude on the other. I switch on every light in the flat, open windows, and the breeze runs through, airing the tangible heaviness. The music plays at full volume in the headphones, so that between screams and concerts the neighbours do not end up breaking the door down. The music invades the humours, and my spirits rise impetuously. I dance to and fro, jump and pant until I drop, exhausted. I realise that it is only the small details that tell one day from another.

Night approaches, and after doing therapy by watching Notting Hill and Bridget Jones’s Diary, which I remember as very funny but which, in spite of the laughs, leave me with an unwelcome bitter taste whose only explanation is the longing for my love life to resume and conclude in the happy ending the protagonists enjoy. At that instant a gastric noise lasting several seconds reminds me that for three days I have been living on water and fructose. I have a couple of slices of toast with cheese while I look at the photographs that appear in the encyclopaedia beside the word universe: the Andromeda nebula, the Eagle, Alpha Centauri, and the circumspect gaze of the Eye of God. I feel that between those images and what I saw in the dream there is something more than science.

I clear everything away and go to bed. I have decided not to take any drug to sleep. Only music, a little Django, Miles, Emmet, Coleman…

Instead, my emotional imbalance sees to keeping me awake for a long while. I toss and turn, over and over.

When I open my eyes, a distant melody is still playing. I feel at peace, serene. It is midnight, the light violet; a few stars can be seen beside the waxing moon.

I am sitting on a bench among those groves of ash and holm oak that I knew so well. I hear no noise and no voice; that silence is a reflection of my inner emptiness. The breeze brings with it a dandelion seed that settles on my lap; it stays long enough to make me remember what they say: blow and make a wish. I take heart to wish myself happiness, and when I am about to catch it, it slips between my fingers and takes flight. Pushed by joy, I run after it. It sways here and there and I cannot capture it. A childish feeling that wants to go on with that game, which I enjoy in fits of laughter at my ineptitude for something apparently so simple. The seed rolls over the asphalt until the garden ends and I look up. I do not know how far I have trotted or in which direction, but I find myself in that street, before that building, just like the last time, only that in the evening light it looks different.

The railway station, though old, still keeps its splendour of former days. Its appearance is very different from the previous visit.

A voice resounds over the loudspeakers, “service to Saint Petersburg, platform five, last call”. The building stands among the gardens of the outskirts; there is no track at all, no trains arriving or leaving. No one passes through it.

I remain in front of the building, and an inexplicable curiosity pushes me to climb the steps. I turn the knob several times; it is stuck. I push, but it seems to be barred. I step back and consider how to get in. At that instant I remember what my grandmother used to say, “always ask for things with a please”. I must be humble and rethink my manners. I go up, knock on the door and say “I should like to come into the station, please”.

The knob turns; the door glides open softly. I go in; it is dark, the door closes, the dusk light barely enters; it is gloomy, dusty, the air heavy, an old atmosphere. On the wall there is a fuse box; I lift all the switches, and the current, trembling, lights with difficulty, one after another, the enormous chandeliers that hang from the glass roof. Empty carriages, rusted locomotives, forgotten suitcases; the tracks disappear into the darkness of the tunnels. The walls are decorated with great Art Nouveau mosaics. I take a couple of steps and stop; my footprints are left marked in the dust. I feel that I am not alone.

― I sense that, finding yourself here, you have chosen the path that is, to my mind, the right one.

― You made me understand, and I choose to go on.

― Good…

― You may show yourself, I’m not afraid -beside me appears a figure, black and blue from feet to neck, with the pale, neutral face of a harlequin-. Hello.

― My greetings, Elina. Allow me to take this form; I prefer it, given the circumstances.

― Thank you. It’s more comfortable to look at than the last one.

― I’m glad. Do you know what this place is?

― A railway station.

― True, but it is also something more. A metaphor…

― …It’s me…

― Exactly. This is more or less the state you are in. An abandonment almost in ruins, but recoverable with a great deal of effort.

― I accept the conditions; it is what I want.

― I see fear, uncertainty, doubt, but also a great will to grow, to walk further and have done with the past.

Always be honest with yourself, for the universe listens and attends to true feelings. Your journey has begun, Elina -at a snap of his fingers the locomotive starts up-. That train is to take you to the labyrinth of your memory; there you will face the rawness of self-knowledge. Go through all the rooms, and at the end you will pass through the great door; once you are beyond it the road will become very hard, but you will have all the tools to walk it.

The decision is yours. Are you getting on? -I nod-. Then we shall meet at the end of this journey. Enjoy it.

I board the carriage. The engine moves off. I wave goodbye. He inclines his head with half a smile. The darkness of the tunnel gives way to a blinding light. The pain in my eyes is such that, when I open them again, it is the setting of my bedroom that surrounds me.

Ω

I turn over and lie face down. I remember the dream in detail, even the smell of the gardens and the station. I do not want to get up; I would rather stay in bed, musing. The memories rush one after another through my mind, but I look at them from a different point of view. I feel freed, new, and at the same time fragile and weak. I have the chance to begin from the beginning, and I am ready to take on any challenge, to suffer the greatest sorrow and to feel the pain, so long as I learn to be myself.

But all this sounds very lyrical in the moments after the dream, a moment in which the conscious mind is still running at half throttle. Before long everything starts up with waking: the anxiety, the fear, the loneliness, the non-existent self-esteem, all those mistaken thoughts, the hostile feelings; they have great strength, they resist leaving in spite of the new knowledge. And it is true that the ego wants them to stay, to scourge the spirit of the ungodly.

By the time I get out of bed, I am once again a sack of broken feelings, among which every ten or fifteen minutes, from sheer exhaustion, a distant voice sounds, shouting “ENOUGH!”.

“I know you did it badly; stop blaming yourself, stop justifying everyone else, leave the past behind. Face your now with joy, with wisdom, with diligence, with serenity”.

The effect of these words lasts a few seconds, which give way to an infernal loop I am hopeless at controlling. How many times can I repeat the same thing in an hour, how many fictitious conversations can I imagine, reconstruct and try to mend? The truth is that I am finding it unbearable to get through the day. Bearing in mind that I have taken it off.

The first thing I do is drink a tea while I take up for the “twenty-umpty-thirteenth” time a French travel phrase book. I have always wanted to learn new languages; I hoard no end of guides and dictionaries on the shelf. Nevertheless, the drink finished, I close the little book. My mind is incapable of absorbing information in its present state. The folder of reports is on the table, but I prefer to leave it for later.

I place myself in front of the television, look at the Nintendo and for an instant believe that playing would cancel certain brain functions, but at that very moment the said muscle is wide awake, reminding me that video games were only fun when I shared them, laughing, with him. The other option is the screen; three box sets light up on the shelf, Ally McBeal, Northern Exposure and The Golden Girls. I am resolved: the hours that remain of these three days devoted to recovering are going to be filled with one episode after another.

Eugene’s gesture in giving me days off is to be thanked, but they are not enough to rehabilitate me.

When the afternoon is over I break off the session; I am a little hungry, though only enough for an apple and a couple of biscuits. So much television is having its effect; I am calm and take the opportunity to close my eyes. I breathe deeply and a shiver runs through me from top to bottom. I see myself climbing a very long staircase, steep, missing some of its steps, but I reach the end, and at its zenith there is peace. I can glimpse a future moment and I feel comforted. The stinking plague that seems to have unlimited rope has an end, though what is not clear is when.

I go back to the sofa. I carry on watching one episode after another, while several glasses of camomile, lime blossom and lemon balm go down. A while later the telephone rings: a message on the answering machine.

― Don’t forget I’m waiting to buy the tickets. That’s all. A kiss -to which I reply with another message.

― I’ve a lot of work, but on Saturday I’ll come over and pay you a visit. What do you think?

― How silly, you coming for a little while when I can spend the weekend with you. Do as you like, but be careful at the wheel, and bring me some of those biscuits I like so much. A kiss, love.

― Noted. A kiss, Mum.

I switch off the television. I sit down to go over the texts and so some two hours pass without remembering, turned into an automaton.

By the time I notice, it is nearly one in the morning. I am not sleepy, so I put the music on, to be befuddled by it until I fall asleep. I do not think the musical selection very apt.

With my eyes full of tears, the next thing is to drop off and be sitting once more on a bench, surrounded by holm oaks and gardens in flower. Only this time the seat is on a railway halt. The name of the stop on the sign is blurred; I go up to it, and though some letters have disappeared, the marks suggest “Sadalmelik”. I stand thinking, trying to work out what that might mean. And while I wander without success, a goldfinch alights on the sign. Its song sounds cheerful and it catches; I begin to whistle. It takes flight and settles in an ash a few yards from me. It goes on singing. I go down the steps of the halt and approach the bird, which flies further on. Understanding that it is guiding me, I follow it expectantly.

The path comes to a wall whose design reminds me of Le Corbusier. In front of me, a waterfall so thin that the garden continuing on the other side can be seen through it. The goldfinch is beside me. I look at the water with little will to cross. The bird flies off and places itself inside. It warbles. I draw near the waterfall; more than reluctance, what I feel is a certain fear. I put out my hand; it is warm and I feel a pleasant sensation; I put my arm in, weightless. Slowly I cross the curtain, feeling well-being in every pore.

Without more ado I find myself on the other side. I am comforted and completely dry. The little bird, perched on a bush, sings merrily. I walk towards it, elated, when I feel a shiver. I turn round: behind the curtain of water there is a shadow, as though someone had been left trapped. The seconds seem minutes of absence. The goldfinch sings, calling for my attention.

The path continues under an arch that opens onto a chamber. I walk on, but this time the bird does not come with me. I enter the room; it looks like an open-air museum, benches in the centre, people contemplating what is on show, and long, broad display cases along the walls, full of an infinity of objects. Two signs: on one, “start of visit”; on the other, “exit”. I begin at the beginning, trying to hear the visitors’ comments; although they speak, I cannot manage to understand what they say.

The first case is lit in white. There are nappies, bibs, a pillow with drawings on it, photographs of a baby. These are things for a newborn. An enormous sense of purity, of innocence before the beginning. As they advance, the lights take on colour, the backgrounds are patterned and the objects vary. That little girl must be about five: dolls, photographs, clothes, a comb, cologne… one moment… that photo… the girl… it’s me. Those were my toys. I retrace my steps to the beginning: that pillow is the one I would not let out of my sight as a baby. The music box. This is not someone else’s exhibition. These are the memories that make up my life. I go on, but far more slowly, remembering every detail, contemplating each of those memories. Photographs of my mother, my father, my grandparents, the nursery. A happy childhood, a first five years pleasant to recall.

As I advance in time, I do not find only lovely memories. That recorder: I must have used it when I was about eight. That day I was punished in class; I do not remember exactly what I did, some harmless bit of mischief, but that bastard, crippled for the teaching of children, must have thought himself inspired when he had my mother sent for. I remember standing beside her watching her cry, while that inconsiderate man reeled off a string of crimes for which her daughter ought to be executed. My mother looked at me feeling a failure, helpless, hopeless. She was learning to bring up her daughter day by day; her trust in teachers was absolute, and those words carried great weight from then on. I was certainly irreverent, but what happened there marked me so much that all I could do was bite the recorder until not a space was left without the mark of my teeth.

It is all much more than I can remember. Even the most insignificant object produces an emotion. But it is on reaching the day of my twelfth birthday that I feel the hilarity of a great moment. There were about nine people, classmates, boys and girls. We celebrated with sandwiches, fizzy drinks and games, to and fro in the sitting room of my house. Everyone was smiling, my mother waits on us, and I had lovely presents, a folder I still keep today and an ABBA cassette. I remember there were two boys I liked, and having them at home was a triumph for me.

A few hours later, when some had already left, my father appeared after his day’s work. He was carrying something in his hands. My surprise was enormous when he gave it to me: a little house with a hamster inside. It was my first pet, the first living being in my charge. I fell so in love with that white rodent with a patch over one eye that my dolls were pushed aside for a long time.

The peculiar thing about the visit is the feeling the progression produces. Innocence is deformed under education and social relations. It strikes me as gruesome by the time it reaches adolescence, and yet I remember those secondary-school years as the best of my student life.

I suppose that in those days I took myself for a woman capable of surviving independently. However, my university years were paid for by my grandparents. I lived with them because a shared flat was out of the question. I suppose this turned me once again into a child to be raised. Into a granddaughter who sees in them the future of her parents, and living through the decline of an irreconcilable couple is not at all pleasant. It affects you far more than it seems, and not only collaterally: unconsciously you make certain behaviours your own and internalise ways of living that are not at all suited to being happy.

Add to that a degree debased by swindlers, a precarious teaching staff and a student body that, rather than championing it, seemed to want to sink it in poison. I would be lying if I said there were no moments of fun, but on the whole I must admit that the bad memories weigh more.

Amid all that, the great good fortune of finding him. Together since we were eighteen. We had a body to discover, a personality to forge; we wanted to help and support each other in working at our vocation so as to mature together.

After finishing my degree I got a job near my parents, once again that little girl of the house. He stayed in the capital and we kept up the relationship at a distance. But the bad habits, rather than easing, seemed to grow.

The next case is smashed to pieces; it is a chaos of objects, of thoughts, of emotions and acts. The bulb flickers, goes out and bursts. I feel a strong tightness in my chest, breathlessness and weakness. I leave the line and settle on a bench to calm down, though all I can do is burst into tears. I lie down full length; it does not matter that there are people. I need to close my eyes and let the last tear fall.

Looking sensibly at the retrospective, there are moments that no longer hurt, thanks to forgetting. It is as simple as leaving them behind to avoid suffering, and as easy as remembering to feel the onslaught of all the pain not overcome. And yet the immediate past and the present grieve more, in spite of being time already lived.

I feel a caress on my head and open my eyes. An old woman is sitting beside me, looking at me sweetly. I sit up and there is no one; everyone but her has disappeared.

Where is everything? The objects in the cases, my memories; nothing is left, absolutely nothing, emptiness; I cannot help being astonished. I get up and take a few steps; I look at the old woman in disbelief. I want to speak, but emotion will not let me; I am short of air. The lady, with a gesture of her hand, invites me to sit. She takes a sweet from her bag and offers it to me insistently; I accept it. She takes out another for herself. Honey and lemon; its sweet taste calms me.

― Smile, young lady. You needn’t worry, they haven’t stolen your past; you carry it inside you. Time and space join together capriciously in a fabric that we create but are not aware of, and in that enormous weave in which you feel so alone and out of place there is a spot you will reach sooner or later where you will have absolute knowledge of the thread of your life, and when that moment comes you will understand that wisdom implies power.

You may not understand it now, but you will if you let your being flow.

― …Thank you.

I cannot quite make it out, but I feel relieved. She is so sure of what she says that she conveys that certainty to me. She gets up and begins to walk towards the exit. I follow her with my eyes; she turns and says.

― By the way, remember my face well; we shall meet again -she goes on her way until she disappears.

More than pensive, I am left empty of thought. The goldfinch sings again. It flies over my head.

― Where are we going now? -I ask.

And its answer is a few notes that turn into a voice. The clock radio murmurs, pulling me out of my dream.

Ω

Light comes into the room; once again I have a whole day ahead of me with the familiar risks involved in leaving aside the fullness of the subconscious to enter the lean and stifling space of wakefulness. To remember my dreams in the smallest detail, to know the state I am in while I sleep and to be unable to feel myself while awake, is something that exasperates me. And although I choose to meditate and repeat some phrases I had read in the Tamil text “All Is One”, I can do no more than lose concentration at irregular intervals. Ramblings that crop up between the words of the prayer and even manage to be funny.

I go out for a ride along the river basin. Pedalling, sweating, the wind in my face, the music, and a couple of shouts going down long slopes all have their effect, breathing heart into me.

It is sunny, so I choose to have a tea and sit in the square to toast myself. I watch the people go by, trying to remember whether any of those faces belongs to the people who inhabit my dreams. A ceaseless coming and going of people crowds the streets while as many again, sitting in the cafés, enjoy the moment. But as it happens I am sitting alone on the terrace of this imitation Indian tea house where a song is playing.

The waiter comes over. He is handsome, Mediterranean, and his voice is lovely. So I accept that conversation should arise in exchange for escaping the words of the song.

― Is the tea to your liking?

― Very good, yes.

― I’m glad. I thought the absent clientele was down to the herbs being tasteless.

― No, not at all. This Genmaicha is very tasty. But coffee and fizzy drinks have you beaten.

― That the rest of the world is wrong doesn’t mean my boss is -he smiles.

― It’s a matter of fashion. Perhaps in ten years the tea house will be a gold mine.

― I don’t think I’ll be here by then… I hope you don’t mind being the terrace’s only customer.

― No, in fact I sat here because I don’t want to hear other people’s conversations. I’m after peace and quiet.

― Oh, sorry to interrupt.

― It’s all right, so long as it’s interesting. What’s your name?

― Alberto.

― And what are you doing so far from home?

― You’ll think it’s mad. I’m a classical dancer. I came to Russia meaning to join the Russian Imperial Ballet, my dream. But they picked me for their third-rate companies, in padding roles. I understood this wasn’t for me.

Now I work as a waiter. I’ll save a bit and go back before the winter sets in hard. At least in my own country I was better thought of.

― I’m sorry.

― And you, what do you do, if that’s not indiscreet?

― I studied journalism. Tired of illiterates and fraudulent “journalisme”, I decided to leave the trade. I work as a proofreader and copywriter in a firm of lawyers and consultants. Just like you, I’ve fulfilled my dream.

― At least you’re at home. I miss my people a lot, the language, the weather… a bit of everything -a pause opens as we attend to the stir of a large group of tourists.

― Do you know the Gaia theory?

― It’s not unknown to me, but I’d rather not put my foot in it -I smile at his answer.

― In short, it says that everything in the universe is part of one macro-organism, the cosmos. Each of us is a small cell with a task. Perhaps we’re neurons lost in the muscle tissue of a foot, and that’s why we don’t see clearly.

― Ha, ha, ha… possibly no more than platelets. I’ve sometimes thought about what our real meaning is, what our mark is worth. Chimeras of a species, chimeras of time.

I don’t make my life depend on it. I dance and make sacrifices thinking of my own satisfaction, expecting nothing in return.

Imagine: ipso facto a meteor falls on the Earth and it is wiped out. My next thought would be, all that dancing for nothing, and yet we don’t stop the time trial of success to reflect on ourselves. Where is our satisfaction? It’s ambiguous. It doesn’t only take knowing you’ve reached the elite; you also want other people’s recognition, and then you want to pass it on… phew…

Being rejected here has been an exercise in humility. I’ll go on dancing in any ballet at all, and I’ll do it finding my happiness in that. There’ll be nothing more.

― I truly hope you manage it. Perhaps one day I’ll found a newspaper where good sense, craftsmanship, sincerity and respect come first, but I hope that day arrives before that comet of yours falls.

And by the way, I disagree with you: sooner than an enormous rock, it will be the human being itself, like a cancerous cell, that ends up killing the macro-organism we live in -he grows thoughtful.

― You know… next time you’ll try a green tea with vanilla. It will be nice to find out what conversation a sweet aroma gives rise to.

― I’ll hold you to that. It’s been a pleasure to meet you and share this while with you. Tell me what I owe you.

― Nothing. It’s on me. But only on condition that you come back another day.

― Of course, I’ll be back -I am leaving when he calls out.

― You’re going without telling me your name!

― I meant to tell you over my next tea. My name is Elina -I answer, laughing. He stays watching me go.

After that chance encounter, or not, the rest of the day passes somewhat more bearably than the last. And between one brooding and another, his face appears and makes me smile.

I go home. I do not remember exactly what I ate. I sit down to go over the texts and an hour later I lie back on the sofa to watch television. The thoughts are still there, colliding fast and at the same time passing by.

I know it is not a reasonable way of trying to get better, but I perceive that something is relaxing in me, in the deepest part. In spite of the peaks and abysses of my emotions, the experiences of my sleeping ego are having positive effects.

When night comes, already in bed, in the dark and with half a smile, I wait, intrigued.

The moon shines high in the sky. I do not know how I got here; I am perched in a tree. I can see clearly the crowns of the ashes and holm oaks, the walls of the labyrinth I had entered, and in the centre, rising impetuously, there is a bronze sculpture of a woman.

I climb down. I regain the path, heading for the centre. Behind the vegetation I make out her figure against the light, and a few steps later I stop before her. She is regal, commanding; she gazes into infinity, her arms stretched out in front and her hands turned downwards. Contemplating her, the spectator feels governed, subdued and small. This is the figure of the great matriarch who rules and saves her clan, which remains eternally grateful to her.

A mother whose mask is so large that the character transcends her reality and comes to exert her fictitious influence over us. A great woman, a model daughter, mother, grandmother…

Born into a poor home, of working-class stock, longed for. The apple of her father’s eye and the envy of her mother, whose green eyes, fair hair and talc-white complexion were pushed aside in the matter of attention now that she had arrived. Who was going to speak of Nordic beauty when the offspring had inherited the best of her parents?

That jealousy, though hidden, was already flooding the seas between mother and daughter. Witness the brief five years in which she was raised by her grandmother, of which she keeps a better memory than of the fifty years with her mother.

The mask that covers the face, solemn and impetuous in expression, seems to crack and at the same time to mould signs of sadness as I scrutinise it.

The girl was growing up, soon to become a woman and to rear a brother who needed looking after. The family and the responsibilities increased. A father who, in spite of loving her madly and living for her, preferred to spend more than sixteen hours in a factory rather than spend one more than necessary with that lady of indomitable and insufferable character, with whom every hint of conversation began and ended in a quarrel.

That attitude, though practical for the family, heightened the sense of an absent father, an acclaimed father. The girl needed that figure in order to grow, to free herself from her mother’s implacable yoke. The young woman added years in an obstructed family, and the prohibitions increased along with her needs. Adolescence, friends, fun. The development of character at a delicate age. The storm of new emotions collided head-on with the archaism towards the female figure. The obligations, the refusals, the disenchantment, the tedium. Her younger brother enjoyed lax treatment and freedoms. Her father remained absent and her mother dictated her life. Exhausted. What she had believed to be kindness, tenderness, calm was now drawn in a single word: fear.

The first train bound for anywhere was the one she took. She did not know him, did not know who he was or where he came from, but he was enough to flee her nucleus. Leaving behind all those years that seemed not to pass. She did not even know his name when she said “I do”, and shortly afterwards she had a daughter.

She had not noticed at the time, but the man beside her kept a past life. A past that would mark their destiny as a couple.

Both worked hard to survive. She did not want to admit it, but he spent so many hours out that once again she felt abandoned, and that was the reason for having me. A mother who wants a daughter to fill her time, and a father who did not seem much concerned to take a position on the next step of the life that had fallen to him.

Before long the couple’s problems began, and they went on until much later. One quarrel after another that did no more than hide the reality of the insurmountable incompatibility of those two fugitives. He raised his hand and she forgave. He humiliated and she answered with love.

My mother went on being a little girl who looked after her brother, now her daughter; she lived submissive like her father, and she faced her husband, formerly her mother.

That little girl, from one day to the next, resolved to become the supermother she had always dreamed of having. She needed no one else to face her life, society, to raise her daughter. And perhaps that was her greatest mistake. Everything revolved around her little one and she forgot herself, so much so that once the girl left home my mother did not know who she was or what she was for. She was a woman without purpose tied to a man she did not love and whom she felt to be a complete stranger.

Perhaps the best thing about my leaving is that she finally made up her mind to divorce. Breaking with part of her past. The death of my grandmother helped in that too, capable as she was of tormenting her from hours away by telephone. After her passing, my mother rested in peace.

The mask falls to pieces and reveals the face of a little girl. I accept that I must not be like her; I must change the patterns of my life if I do not want to end up waiting for my father’s death to feel relief, if I do not want my mother to go leaving me the debt towards that great maternal sacrifice, imposed by her own decision, which while overprotecting me turns me into a slave. I do not want to become either of them and I do not intend to repeat what happened. I must understand my parents’ behaviour, exonerate them of all blame; they too were victims, but I must break ties to find myself, so as not to inherit. The mask falls away completely. The expressionless face gives way to joy, innocence, humanity. My mother is all that behind the crust that hides her true self. The great deity dwindles until she becomes the image of the commonest of mortals.

Ω

I open my eyes. The cassette ends up jamming; the screech wakes me abruptly. It is 3 in the morning. Side B plays and I fall asleep halfway through the song.

I am standing under an entrance arch. Before me, another part of the labyrinth to discover. It is daytime and the sky is clear. Only a fountain in the centre. I go up to it; it is full of fish, but not just any fish. These are the fish that were in the aquarium at home, the ones I used to go and buy with Dad. The same ones he would contemplate for hours, oblivious to everything, lost in the depths of ancient Atlantis.

For the first time, before the enormous pool, I understood what my father was doing in front of the aquarium. He was simply forgetting. He was setting aside the harmful problems of a threatening job, one of those you go to in fear and come out of limping. Enslaved to feeling the dread of dismissal every single day for more than thirty years. Hard work, from dawn to dusk, the kind that leaves the sweat stored in his daughter’s memory every time he kissed her on his return. Without doubt, my father was a harried man.

A man trapped in his twenties, in his bachelor years. My father, though he loved his wife and his daughter on his real plane, in the depths of his heart, there where the guts speak, was still a young man whose road had gone astray.

He is one of those people who are forced to leave the bosom of the family violently in their youth. Obliged to work out of necessity to escape a punishing father who scorns him, humiliates him, and a mother already punished. A childhood full of beatings, an adolescence of rebellion, a maturity of objection. A libertine, and one woman among so many to marry and to swear eternal love to. A daughter who is born and grows fast, a daughter he hardly sees, a daughter to bring up when he had never been taught how. A new life to face when he had not even broken with the last. A jumble of indigestible feelings that dragged on through time, making his life a loop he did not know how to escape. A repetition he did not want to leave either; he had grown comfortable, had made himself strong, a man of character in the face of circumstances.

The rampart could withstand cannon fire and Greek fire, but the wall kept him from advancing, from leaving his limits and knowing new territories. He could not get out, and that burned him though he did not know it, for that rage came from far off, from his hands to his heart. A survivor perhaps, though it would be more apt to say he was a reptile, a creature conceived to be alone. Paradoxical, being the son of an abuser.

A father who sometimes confuses affection with presents, at other times with gestures of sacrifice, at others with hugs, but all in one and the same bag of values. Loves that he uses as a weapon when anger comes, recalling all the suffering on account of his family. He forgets that love knows nothing of reproaches, only of self-denial. But let us not be cruel; he has not arrived here by chance. Although his behaviour has deep repercussions among those close to him, he does not mean to hurt anyone. He simply does not know how to be a father, how to be a husband, how to be a friend. An emotional cripple. Someone who cannot express his feelings towards those around him, and, more serious still, cannot express them towards himself.

My father needs to forgive, to forgive himself, to see himself free of guilt, to be acknowledged by his parents, to have friends, to socialise, to be humble, to love himself, to accept his new life. And, once cleared of charges, to decide what kind of relationship he wants with the world. My father, after pulling down the rampart, has to be honest with himself, settle on a route and walk it. It will be good for him, and it also lightens, if it does not dissolve altogether, the weight of the burden his figure places on his ties.

Each of the fish swimming in the pool lets me glimpse with precise certainty features of the paternal profile I would scarcely have suspected. Among those crystalline fluctuations I understand many things, not only about him but about me, marking out the harmful inheritance that must be extinguished.

As I accept, the fish fade, turned into long sighs swollen with calm. I put my hand in the water and stroke the last of them. It only remains for me to understand that love for my father has weight and measure, that I need do no more. I need not strive to help him, I need not blame him; I need only love him in the way in which the relationship between us makes me happy. And that is a relationship of distant love.

It may be that bringing up a child is unnecessary; we imitate our parents to perfection. My gaze stays fixed on the depths.

The ripples of the water glint, and the darkness turns to light behind my eyelids. The single-note beeps, beep…, beep…, beep…, the alarm clock shrills.

Ω

It is daytime. A new day lies ahead to fill and a new dream experience to absorb. I recall it, rapt, with a great feeling of freedom, of wide horizons. I am at peace with my parents.

Many details and great moments of my life take on meaning. I accept them, embrace them and let them go.

For a few hours I stay lying in bed, trying to reorganise the repertoire of my brand-new perspective. I understand the relationship between my parents and their relationship with me. And most important of all, I understand many things about what my own relationship has been. An account that is no excuse, but one which, without our knowing, made us a faithful copy of our parents. Exactly the opposite of what one wants to be when one grows up.

Though it hurts terribly to have lost him, in this solitude in which I cannot take a step without feeling the touch of his skin, the warmth of his gaze. I am fully convinced that every decision, good and bad, was made by both of us. I am half guilty and half innocent.

I am alone, more alone than ever, but happy, as happy as one can be in a situation like this.

I do not know how, but it pleases me that that someone has come to show me what lies beyond. It is the only way to know my nature. Contrary to my first decision to give up, every night cardinal points are revealed that surprise me pleasantly and grandiloquently.

After midday, with no wish to eat, my only appetite is for correcting text. My mind craves inconsistencies of spelling and grammar.

The text sets out the defence of a worker, an electrical technician who was going to be dismissed. A company whose intention was to get rid of its staff in order to rebuild its workforce with subcontractors. Self-employed workers at bargain prices, paying for their own materials, their vehicle, their sick leave, their social security.

With an enormous volume of work and profits in the millions, dismissing its workers counted as unfair dismissal. That being so, the compensation rose considerably. So the best move, with the law’s assistance, was to send the workers to the other end of the country. The man, with a wife and children, with a home and no prospects of work, unable to pay for a rented flat, the travel and his family’s keep, plus his own, on a miserable wage, ended up begging to resign voluntarily. Eighty-five per cent less expense for the company, and a practice to be exploited.

Nevertheless, this worker, after a long fight and after uniting all the labour unions of the province, won the favour of a judge and set a precedent. The company’s manoeuvre was recognised as a malicious ruse. Full payment of the dismissal plus damages was ordered. A pity it was not retroactive.

After the lesson in law and with everything corrected, I take to the street. While I eat an apple my motor instinct switches on; I need to clear my mind, to see faces, streets, noises. I go without direction until I turn up, without meaning to, in the same square where yesterday I was drinking tea.

I go closer; there are people both inside and on the terrace. I search among those faces.

― Were you looking for me? -he asks, tapping my shoulder with a finger.

― Hey, hello! -I turn; it is him.

― I’ve finished. I was going to catch the bus, but I couldn’t pay because I’d forgotten my wallet.

― Oh…

― It wasn’t chance. Passing by, or were you thinking of sitting down?

― I was thinking of sitting down on condition I was alone and had you as my waiter, but apparently today’s not the day.

― Well… Allow me a proposal: I go in, fetch my wallet, make two vanilla teas to take away and we go for a stroll while we chat. What do you think?

― If you’re not too tired…

― Not at all, and even if I were, I’ll take a taxi. There’s no more to be said -I answer with a smile-. Give me a couple of minutes.

We both walk, seasoned with the warm aroma of the infusion. It is Saturday and the bustle of the street gives the conversation one turn after another. Without knowing how, we end up asking each other, laughing, who our favourite childhood character is.

― Bert and Ernie -he answers-, very funny; they made an unusual couple for being nothing more than friends.

― I prefer Count von Count and his long monologues from one to five.

Sharing this while is very pleasant. The best moment is when he climbs onto a balustrade on the Birch Walk and, to the waltz of a street orchestra, delights me with a few ballet steps. In one of his movements he plucks some leaves and offers me the bouquet in a final bow.

― Thank you very much, handsome gallant.

― Many you’re-welcomes -he looks at his pocket watch.

― Is it late?

― I can’t say half past ten on a Saturday night is late, but I’m up early tomorrow. I train first thing and in the afternoon I’m back at work.

The truth is I’m tired. I wouldn’t want you to think I’m bored, quite the opposite; I’m delighted to have run into you and to have spent this wonderful while with you.

― Thank you. I can say the same. Go home and rest -we walk a little further to the taxi rank.

― Drop by the café whenever you like. I’ll be delighted to see you.

― Of course, see you soon. Good night.

And here we say goodbye. I watch the car move off. From here it is more than an hour’s walk home. So I decide to enjoy a ride back as well, chauffeur included.

I watch the lights through the window; in spite of being part of the city, everything goes by apart from me. I know absolutely no one; one has to go deep into the neighbourhood to come across the odd neighbour, or at least to see the usual faces serving in the shops.

My new situation is leading me to know the deepest part of myself, but the truth is that outside my own door nothing is familiar to me. I have no friends.

Home again, I do not feel like television, or radio, or reading, only the wish to listen to a Mina record, while, lying on the floor, I look at the firmament, losing my thoughts, trying to find my guide among the stars that little by little light up my inner universe.

When my buttocks begin to hurt too much I discover two great truths: that drawing a centaur with four points of light requires a great deal of imagination, and that a mattress is the best place to enjoy the horizontal position.

I go to bed; I am tired and eager to know another section of myself.

Sitting on a wooden chair, before a table with a game of chess almost finished, surrounded by a tangible emptiness, so much so that it seems to weigh on my shoulders.

There are no doors in this enclosure, which I already recognise from earlier visits, but I sense strangeness, distance. There is no other chair; in fact the board leaves me to one side; the players must have been to my left and to my right.

All the pieces are off the board except the black king and the white queen; only one square separates them. I do not know what that position is called, but the game is a draw. Two great personalities with a desire to be the only one, obliged to share a victory not attained, a duality which, if it were not ended, would repeat itself again and again down a line that sooner or later would end by shouting “ENOUGH”, with a generation unable to sow again, punished or perhaps blessed by a nature we long ago preferred not to hear.

On the board there are not two ivory carvings; on the board there are two people, and it is perhaps more accurate to see in them two imperative profiles. That of a repressor and that of one resigned. Roles without gender.

In the first instance, it is a matter of a brief exercise in introspection. I had become a tyrant; that side of my father I so detest is one of the many inheritances of my dynasty. And the man who had been my partner had been sodomised relentlessly. We had both accepted our roles and played them to perfection every day.

My attitude was not comparable to that of my predecessors. Detesting their ways had led me to avoid them, but I had not for that reason failed to find new ways of flogging the one who shared his life with me. My ways were more intellectual, less aggressive at first sight, but just as harmful in their result.

I never understood how my father could have inherited such patterns and put them into practice, and now that lack of understanding fell on me. Neither he nor I was comparable to that swine my grandfather, but once the dead leaves were cleared away, the branch was the same and reached down to the roots.

I place the king and the queen as the first pieces of the family tree. My great-grandparents, my grandparents, my parents. One white, active; another black, passive.

Although I did not know my great-grandparents, I had heard my grandparents speak of their parents. I lay out the pieces with certainty, and after studying a brief genealogy I notice the axial reflection of the chart. What is black on one side is white on the other. Not one piece fails. At that moment I understand that it is not only physical features that are inherited; behaviours, memories, conflicts and greatnesses are inherited too, and they arrive not in a straight line but zigzagging all along our lineage. Formats we believe our own, other people’s conduct, past mixtures with present practice.

Having come to this point, I understand that I must break with the defects of my own self in order to progress as a person, but also that I must discover and take on the concepts inherited from a lineage whose way of facing life strikes me as hardly practical for being happy and coexisting in harmony.

My great-grandparents. Two of them had been shot in the war: my paternal grandfather’s father, a fascist; my maternal grandmother’s father, a liberal. The extreme character of those individuals shapes their descendants.

A soldier shot by firing squad, a woman who died giving birth to her third child, a boy brought up by his aunt as someone else’s burden and educated in a military school. An orphaned child whose only memory of his mother is that he killed her. It is no wonder that such a life gives rise to behaviour like that of my paternal grandfather. All those unresolved emotions are passed on to my father, with his own father’s ill-treatment added.

Next, the other great-grandfather, on my mother’s side, a hardened drunkard who was never at home, abandoned his family and squandered his savings and his wife’s. An early death from a stroke, and a woman who has to become a great matriarch to bring up her children, working from dawn to dusk, carrying her little ones on her back even when she scrubs and peels potatoes as a kitchen hand.

One of those children, my grandfather, a weak gentleman unable to bear his wife, leaves home as his father had done before him, causing his children to feel the same lack he felt towards his own father.

The shades blend from one extreme to the other, and although it is true that the differences are abysmal, in no case do they find a midpoint, a balance between the black and the white of those figures that face each other in hard and endless battle, communing in a grey that points in the same direction.

I lean back in the chair. I look at the vast sky and, with deliberate disdain, put my feet up on the table, knocking the board and the pieces to the floor. I feel a long shiver run the length of my body, a tingling that rises from the depths of my heart and disperses through my skin.

Many sensations that are not mine leave me, making up a something I shall miss. I begin to feel authentically myself from one end to the other, and the feeling promises to go on until the last drop of rust of my dynasty has been distilled.

Ω

The dampness of my eyes becomes palpable; I am still lying down, but now on the bed; I am awake and I confess myself truly strange. The sensation has crossed over from the dream to become physical. Of all the earlier experiences, this is without doubt the most disconcerting of all. I understand the influence of my parents, my own history, but I could never have come to imagine the enormous compendium of unknown people that made up my life.

From that moment I am ready to be myself, and these tears are the last notes of the mourning for the absent. Edith Piaf gets me out of bed with satisfaction, “Non, je ne regrette rien”.

The hypnagogic experiences disperse as the conscious mind takes full possession. Once more the everyday Elina. The load seems the same, but it feels lighter; even the morning migraine I used to suffer on waking has gone.

After a Sunday breakfast, with the house gleaming, having watched nearly all the series I own, the only thing that occurs to me is to go out and take as long a walk as possible, so as not to spend more hours than necessary at home.

I walk past the halt of the local train; as it happens, in ten minutes the train passes for the neighbouring town, which stands beside an enormous lake. I do not hesitate to buy the ticket. The journey takes about fifty minutes, but I need to disconnect from the great city.

With my gaze lost in the distance, after emerging from the capital’s underground, most of the journey runs through forest, skirting the great lake, so immense that the far shore is lost on the horizon beneath the mountains. In the carriage only three people, each of them absorbed in their thoughts, oblivious to the rest of the passengers. The deafening rattle of the rails. And during that brief hour I perceive only a few minutes passing, under the hypnotic effect of a landscape in motion. At the shore the water is icy and crystal clear; the fish swim in the calm surface; I take off my shoes and begin to walk, letting the water bathe my feet. I notice that the waves carry off the very last of my ills. I feel languor, calm; the people I pass seem to be enjoying the same sensation as I am. It is too long since I stopped to converse with nature, since I left my humanity behind to become a drop of the sea.

I do not know how long the walk lasts, but by the time I want to turn back I have long since left the town behind. I have been walking for nearly two hours and they seem like minutes. The return is even shorter, which gives me to understand that the clock measures time, but life is lived in moments, moments that know nothing of days or nights. It is one’s state of mind that dictates the length of an era, and I am sure that this one is going to mark a before and an after. All the same, I am grateful to find moments like this, a silence in which I can say for certain that I feel well.

The train back is just as relaxed. If I did not have to return to work the next day, it would be a therapy to consider daily.

Home again, it must be four in the afternoon. I make lunch, set out supper and, while I am at it, cook for several days of the week.

It is not that I am much happier than a couple of days ago, but I need to avoid thinking about what ends by dragging me under. I put on an ABBA compilation at full volume. I sing my voice away to “Chiquitita”, and side B of the cassette carries on once more into its side A.

Everything I prepared ends up in the freezer. I prefer to eat two bars of dark chocolate, accompanied by some brown rice cakes for balance. Between one square and the next goes a swig of pineapple batida with ice. It is not the best menu for a proper diet, but on a Sunday night one cannot ask for more. I am not used to drink; the alcohol is only there to be offered to guests. Dizzy and with the chocolate churning my insides, lying back on the sofa with the whole day’s happiness as a backdrop, his smiling image, his caresses, his kisses dull my senses. And although the stereo is off, I can hear Patsy Cline singing “Crazy”.

I suppose it is not the ideal way to end the day, but at least there is no more to think about and my head falls silent.

I open my eyes. I appear in a meadow full of flowers and trees. I find myself facing a small hill that hides what is happening on the other side. I can hear children shouting and laughing; it is pleasant to hear that hubbub. The smell of lavender, of rosemary. I breathe deeply and the goldfinch appears, alighting on my shoulder. I am not inside the labyrinth, but I have not left it.

I walk uphill. I smile, infected by those children playing on the other side. But on reaching the top I am surprised by a great absence: an empty meadow, silent, only a pair of swings, one still swaying, the other inert. I go closer. It goes on rocking as though someone were pushing it. I take a seat on the second, when the glee of a little boy scampering a few yards away catches my attention. I want to get up, but someone takes my hand.

― You’re not ready yet to go near him.

Beside me, swinging, an adolescent boy smiles. His face is strangely familiar to me although I have never seen him.

― Hello.

― … Hello, Elina… -I look at him, trying to guess-. Perhaps you’ll recognise me like this -and passing his hand over his face, he changes, transforming it into the harlequin’s.

― Oh, goodness! It’s you… hello.

― Hello again. May I?

― Yes, of course -and he becomes a boy again.

― Thank you for bringing me here.

― You’ve nothing to thank me for; you got here on your own. I only showed you another option.

― Thank you for that, then. I’ve learnt many things about myself through catharsis. I’ve destroyed my whole ego, including who I believed I was. Crossing the waterfall I left my erroneous zones behind, and before my history, I accepted. Knowing myself through my parents, through my family, I’m beginning to build who I really am.

― Now all that remains is to smooth the rough edges and walk forwards. The hardest task: to evolve, to be in balance and harmony, to be diligent, temperate, wise and serene. Some take many lives to attain peace, but having come this far, I sense that part of the road is already behind you.

Similar to the Shiguseigan. The premises are respect for oneself, since that is the respect we show outwards, towards our equals; respect for life, the earth, the cosmos.

Balance, the point on the sphere that takes us away from the extremes and allows us breadth of vision, that harmonises the energies of one’s own being so that one walks the light.

Flow: setting aside prejudices, learnt concepts; flowing as pure instinct, enjoying each step without judging it, breaking with the obstacles of our own that hinder our advance. Letting oneself be carried without fighting against the current allows us to understand our surroundings.

And lastly, keeping these three laws, however hard they may seem. An arduous but satisfying obligation.

― I think I’ll be able to remember those three laws. Now that I’ve passed through the door, I feel it’s what I want and what I must do…

By the way, the more I look at you, your gestures, your voice, your face, I imagine you adopt that face as a projection of my unconscious so as to make me feel at ease, but I feel there’s something more…

― Ha, ha, ha… You’re still looking for a rational explanation, but let’s celebrate that your instinct is beginning to make itself heard. You and I, Elina, have known each other for many years, only we hadn’t seen each other before. In fact, I’m not certain this is the right face, but I think it comes fairly close -I look at him, somewhat lost-. Let it flow… -we fall silent, we look at each other, the images come to my memory. I perceive unknown feelings-. What’s happening?

― I feel… that beside you I’m comforted, complete, that that sense of emptiness fades; I feel a great deal of love and joy… I don’t feel alone… but it isn’t the feeling for a lover, it’s family, a blood tie, the affection for the one you grow up with… -he smiles-. I don’t know why I said that, I can’t make sense of it.

― Mum never told you, but you would have had an older brother if the pregnancy hadn’t gone wrong -I look at him, astounded-. You and I decided to meet as brother and sister and be great friends, but something failed. A pity. Though that hasn’t stopped me watching over you, even disregarding his command -he says, looking at the little boy.

He lets everyone follow the path they decide on, though he hasn’t forbidden us to intercede subtly either. I hope you don’t mind…

― … -all I can do is get up and throw my arms around him-. Thank you.

― I’m very glad to be able to be with you today, Elina. But you mustn’t be sad; life, seen from where I am, lasts a sigh. Soon we’ll meet again and next time nothing will fail -my tears fall. He holds me tight. We sit down on the grass.

Be glad, smile, since you have the privilege of feeling as you feel. Many people come and go again and again and never notice the mistake; they even get worse.

That child there is the centre of the universe. God, Allah, Jehovah, Odin, Ifá, Buddha, Shiva, call him what you will. He is the source of life. In the cosmos everything is alive, everything is part of everything through a quantum web; every atom that makes up our being contains the living history of millions of years. I feel your thoughts as mine, and mine are his. Man is beginning to discover the tip of a truly fascinating iceberg.

The consciousness of the universe is infinite and divides into thousands. This energy known as spirit becomes disturbed as it moves away from the core, and many of them decide to take flesh, accepting the hardest means of purification in order to raise their frequency and draw near once more to the great spirit of life.

Neither you nor I are a physical person who is reincarnated in another body; on the contrary, we are immanences that change face unceasingly, and it is the flesh that makes us forget who we really are. If we do not awaken, the effort is vain and the purpose goes astray, subjected to the crude character of the ego of a humanity that has forgotten how to be.

Individualism has disconnected man, and this chaos we suffer will end in a catharsis that will lead to a new rebirth. And though it may seem catastrophic to you, it is not the first time it has happened; we are not the first, nor the only chosen ones. It has been repeating itself for thousands of years.

There where you see him, playing, many think he is an old man, a regal and serious figure, a punisher, who only rewards goodness and punishes evil. It is not like that at all; the cosmos is very young, a child free of worries, happy, who gives all he has again and again so that harmony may reign in his being. He is too sublime. His intervention is not needed for the energies to know when they have gone wrong; if you make mistakes in one life, another comes along in which to improve and make amends for them. Something like the laws of Karma, it’s simple…

I do not know what to say. The information is too condensed.

― Are you happy?

― Of course. Very. Here there are only lovely feelings, and seeing you so sad was what made me decide to intercede. The only concern you need have in your life is to learn to be happy always, and to make those around you happy as far as you are able.

― I’ll try with all my strength.

― I’m glad -an instant of silence.

― What name would Mum have given you?

― Iván.

― It’s lovely… and what shall I do now that I know you exist? I want to go on feeling this fullness when I wake.

― I’ll always be with you, in your heart; you only have to feel me.

You have broken your shell, your mask; you have removed the harmful inheritances and you have understood. Connect with the essence of the universe and all will be well. Free the child and you will be mistress of your destiny. The cosmos will arrange everything you ask of it, if you know how to ask; remember that your hand, your heart and your mind must be in tune, and always believe in yourself; when all is said and done, we have what we believe we deserve.

The little boy stops scampering and comes over to us. Iván kisses my cheek, gets up and stands beside him.

― It’s time for us to go. See you soon.

The child smiles and a warm light floods everything. It disperses and I slowly wake.

I am in bed. My eyes, still closed, can see the last chamber of the labyrinth. The exit is a few steps from me. The goldfinch is there, perched on a branch, saying goodbye. I walk towards the doorway, but just before going out I trip: on the ground there are some plasticine figures. I pick them up without asking further and cross to the outside to open my eyes. My mind is on this side.

Ω

I am completely shaken after the last dream. I would never have imagined such a thing, and many would call it madness. I meet my brother, and the infant cosmos reveals his essence to me.

I feel well, overawed, but well. From now on I shall try to take life with more humour and love. I am still subject to my emotions, and often I will not be able to control them, but I am not going to allow hostile feelings to take control and diminish my being. I am determined to fight, to learn. I am renewed, brimming.

Musing on my mother’s reaction if I mention to her that I have met Iván. It is true that I do not intend to, but imagining her many arguments keeps me entertained until the figures from the final moment come back to my memory. I know they were made of plasticine and colourful, but I am missing details. I tripped over them… I cannot quite understand. My mind goes blank for a long while.

Wow, those figures!! He gave them to me for our fifth anniversary; he had made them himself, a pair of hedgehogs embracing. That very day we quarrelled and they ended up squashed against the floor. I jump up and take a box from the most unreachable corner of the wardrobe; it is full of his letters, of our photographs, covered in memories. A rolled-up handkerchief; I unwrap it, and there is what remains of the hedgehogs. The sensation unsettles me; I sit on the bed with it in my hands. I look at them, remembering so many lovely things.

“I didn’t mean to… I’ve understood, late, but I have…” I murmur with half a smile, rapt.

And suddenly that trip lights up my face. Metaphorically, to trip is to stumble over the same stone again, “ha, ha, ha”. I lie down on the bed laughing, moved. “If it is true that one day we meet again, it will be to start afresh, like two strangers, properly, from the beginning”.

I am on my way to work, riding my bicycle, with my headphones on; on the radio they are commemorating the fall of the Berlin Wall and “Heroes” by David Bowie is playing.

Ω

I learnt and I go on learning. I accept my condition as a human being without aspiring to perfection. I live every minute as if it were the last. I try to make my angers last a sigh and to see life as a celebration.

Now, some time later, even in the moments of greatest loneliness, of unbearable weakness, of exasperating longing, I remember that moment as a before and an after.

The Valley of the Fireflies

At the end of the working day, when the sun caressed those lands and people with its last rays, just as night fell, the fireflies took wing, fluttering everywhere. Such was the glow they gave off in the dark that they seemed stars strolling over the earth, come down from the sky like a fleeting Leonid.

The Grove of the White Blackbird was the name of that small town that rose above the valley, walled in and protected by the high ranges and the full-flowing rivers. In it, generation after generation, its inhabitants had gone on living in harmony since its founding many years before.

Beyond the admiration aroused by the spectacle that arose each nightfall, the town held no value for any foreign eye. They saw in it nothing but a remote, poor village of worthless dwellers. To the Grovefolk such an opinion mattered rather little. In fact, they liked to be of no interest at all, and so avoid seeing the peace in which they lived disturbed. But in truth it was a town where poverty did not exist. None of its citizens lacked food, shelter, home or trade.
