By the common decision of every one of the Grovefolk, a civic balance was kept, and full harmony with the nature that provided for them.

All waste was reused, what broke was mended, the felled tree was replanted, water was managed with wisdom, children were rationed so that the number of inhabitants stayed stable, and the old worked as much as they wished. Money did not exist, and gold was valued the same as any other stone in the earth. Goods in common use were exchanged: food, tools, furniture, knowledge.

As in every village, there was the figure of the counsellor, a citizen of middle age, loved and listened to among his fellows for his wise reasoning and sound advice, which always resolved any possible wrong or troubled occurrence. His chief task consisted in writing, day by day, the history of the town, taking stock of food, of population and of so many other things of which he kept a punctual record. The purpose of these notes lay in future consultation for the balance of the town in the face of any possible doubt or need, so avoiding errors already committed.

Claude, for that was our counsellor’s name, returned home every evening, his labours done, to enjoy his family to the full: his wife Stella and his son Frantisek. Our friend was enormously proud of his wife, who was one of the finest embroiderers the valley had known. Her garments were worn by all the neighbours and, while simple, had an elegant touch that delighted everyone. This innate mastery of thread and her many years of experience had made her the head of the sewing workshop that supplied the town. Frantisek must have been close to twenty-five; he was a musician and composer. From very small he had stood out for his skill with the harp. With his many songs he seasoned the Sundays when the audience gathered in the square to listen to his fantasias.

It may have been a notable family in the village, but it was regarded in the same way as any other citizen. And its privileges and obligations were the same as every neighbour’s.

If The Grove of the White Blackbird took pleasure in anything, it was in knowing itself a town respectful of its surroundings, peaceful, hard-working and cheerful. It was a town truly beautiful to behold, by day as in its magical night. The houses were decorated with broad murals. Sculptures were to be found in the streets and gardens. Art was part of the life of those people. Lines were enjoyed not as in a framed canvas that recalls a distant place, marking off the space between viewer and work. Here every painted wall, every decorated façade, every clipped garden formed part of daily life. Music, letters, medicine, numbers and of course nature were the greatest concerns and the greatest gift the Grovefolk had.

When work was over, the young people went out into the street, danced and played their instruments, until at nightfall all withdrew to rest after the exhausting day.

On those warm nights when the fireflies lit every corner, there was a special place, a garden of rose bushes and jasmine where silence turned to poetry. When everyone was asleep, Frantisek would slip away to this spot, which bordered the forest and lay far from any house. There, sitting on a wooden bench, he sought inspiration for his scores. On his strings he played, softly, a lovely melody that surrounded him with a peculiar audience: the fireflies flew in accompaniment, lighting each note.

Frantisek dreamed awake, and his music, like a dream, was that of a man in love. He had never been with any woman and no one kindled the spark in his heart. He imagined the day when there would come into his life that unique figure whose face could be glimpsed in the sighing bars.

On nights like that one, the rays of the full moon came through the hedges and made the blue eyes of a hind with a silky orange coat shine. Sometimes she arrived before the young man, at other times shortly after the first notes sounded, but she always waited for the last to vibrate before vanishing swiftly into the forest.

That day, however, was different from the rest; a strange feeling eddied in the young man’s chest, and his nerves would not let him settle. He ploughed the kitchen garden and gathered its fruits. The afternoon passed quickly, running errands here and there surrounded by people, but he remained absent. He longed for sunset, for the merry fireflies and for his hands on the harp, playing that unknown feeling conceived in his heart.

He wished to see that woman of whom his poets spoke, to whom the sonatas were dedicated. A maenad able to steal men’s souls, whose presence dwelt in canvases, writings and scores. Entranced, he plucked the strings, telling of that love which seemed to him intangible. Twang! One of the strings snapped under tension, cutting the middle finger of his left hand. Though small, the wound bled freely. He pressed hard, set down the harp, and in the brief instant in which he wondered what to stop the flow with, a noise in the undergrowth. The waxing moon lit the hind’s gaze. For the first time she appeared before him; he did not know what to do; the creature’s almost human eyes hypnotised him. He knelt to stroke her, but the animal drew away, circling him.

― I’m sorry, I didn’t mean… -the boy murmured. The hind came closer to lick his wounded hand. The young man held out his arm-. I’m very grateful for the gesture. I know you listen to me in secret every night, hidden among the bushes, but this cut, small as it seems, will keep me a couple of days without being able to play… -the young man broke off; the hind went on licking the wound, which had already stopped bleeding and was closing with each stroke-. Well, I didn’t think deer’s saliva could do that; it seems more like magic. My most heartfelt thanks.

I let myself be carried away by this molto allegro agitato, but now that you’ve healed me I can go on playing a while longer, one string short -the wound had healed.

The hind looked at him and the young man returned a smile. The animal moved off and disappeared into the foliage. Frantisek remained sitting, thinking over what had happened, absorbed.

Ω

With the dawn the town woke to its tasks. Everything suggested that that day would be like any other, full of normality. But when it was almost noon, a horseman in gallant attire arrived at the doors of the auditorium, where he had himself attended by the town’s counsellor. Claude came out quickly to meet him.

― Good day, Baron. You are welcome to The Grove of the White Blackbird. Your visit pleases me greatly after so long without news of you. Tell me, if you would be so kind, the reason for your presence in so humble a town.

― I am obliged, Claude, though it is no great pleasure to ride two days and a night to present myself before you in so dull a place. Nevertheless, by command of your brother, the king who rules these lands belonging to the region of Dalmatia…

― My brother does not rule over this valley -he interrupted-; no one governs our lives. We are neither greedy nor warlike. And that sets us apart from you, Guglielmo, and from your king, with whom I doubt I any longer share a single drop of blood.

― Your words are worthless when what binds you to Egon goes beyond your person. But I have not come here to converse with you. His Majesty has declared war on the White Lady; he wishes to enlarge his fiefs and his riches. Unfortunately this valley borders her domains. No help is asked of you, of course, you have none to give; only obedience to the following prohibition. No one may pass through the forest from this very moment. The king has summoned the Blind Hunters to beat the woods for that witch.

― It is madness; you do not even know that such riches exist. You will only succeed in ending the lives of hundreds of soldiers; the White Lady has powers that will finish a thousand armies. Leave things as they are; we live in peace with her; do not allow the same madness as before to be committed again.

― Claude, give it up, I have only come to warn you. If you want your citizens to stay alive, let them not cross the edge of the forest, or the hunters will annihilate them, mistaking them for the witch’s acolytes. By the way, should the stalkers ask for shelter, I trust you will treat them correctly. That is all I have to say.

Answer back as much as you like behind my back; it is the will of your king, vassal.

The baron wheeled his horse about and retraced his way, disappearing from the town. Claude spread the word neighbour by neighbour, announcing the king’s words. The bad news went round in less than an hour. The Grovefolk kept calm; no one raised his voice or flung any insult at the greedy and despotic will of the king of Dalmatia. All went on with their daily tasks, taking good heed of the warning. From that instant, no one crossed the edge of the forest.

Frantisek was sitting in the workshop. He was mending the string of his harp when his father came into the house.

― Has your mother arrived?

― Not yet, Father. She is gathering spices and fruit at the edge of the forest before nightfall.

― Then I imagine you know the reason for my visit.

― Baron Guglielmo is truly as I had heard tell: unmistakable.

― In many respects, my son. He is an undesirable person, whose presence here I deeply regret.

― Father… for as long as I can remember we have always lived in peace in this valley. I have been taught that respect is the greatest value, that the word is the road to understanding, and that hostile feelings have no power at all among those whose only possession is intangible between the fingers: love.

I had not heard of strife since the moment of my birth. If the same king as before still rules and the White Lady is no more than a legend, I do not understand what drives him to unleash a war so many years later.

― Not exactly so, my son. The Grove of the White Blackbird is free land, a refuge for good people seeking a different way of life.

Today our family knows happiness in a place like this. Fleeing days gone by, before your birth, when your mother and I worked at court.

― You had never told me that before, Father -he said, thoughtful.

― And it should never have been remembered. Your mother worked as tailor to the royal family. She was charged with weaving every blue-blooded garment. I was the royal counsellor. The monarch came to me whenever some doubt assailed him. If he did not know the answer, or if his decision needed a second opinion, I was the one consulted. I must have been younger than you at the time, when events changed dramatically.

The kingdom was at peace; Egon had inherited it from his father in prosperous times. No natural catastrophe to diminish the fields or the livestock, no disease to reduce the population, and all the neighbouring kingdoms content with the extent of their lands and the volume of their riches. Yet the king’s great fondness for chess ended by being insufficient to satisfy that boredom of which he claimed to be the victim. And so, from one day to the next, the monarch became a wrathful being, eager to increase his power, to extend his borders and enlarge his treasures.

However much I insisted that he cease his bloodthirsty madness, there was no one who could halt his will. War was declared on the three neighbouring kingdoms. The armies recruited every available man. And so began a terrible passage through the kingdoms of Dalmatia. After subduing the adjacent countries, after stealing their riches and imposing his tyranny. Seeing his hosts unbeatable, unsatisfied with what he had won, he resolved to finish off that landmark which escaped his dominions: the enchanted valley of the White Lady, a set of tongues of land flanked by mountain walls, in which our town is settled.

Egon challenged the sorceress; he raised his entire army before the citadel, surrounding it with his most fearsome artillery. But that battle was lost; one cannot fight someone who is no more than a legend and whose powers lie beyond human understanding. The White Lady warned that anyone who crossed the bounds of the citadel would end up turned to rock on breathing the scents of its gardens.

But the warning went unheeded and the king had the bastion stormed. No one knew what its riches and secrets were, but the invasion answered to the pride and vanity of Egon, whose only will was to rule everything his sight could reach. The hosts charged the castle unaware that every breath of air was stealing their lives, turning them to stone. It took no more than a couple of hours for the invincible Dalmatian army to be reduced to dust.

However much I begged and pleaded, Egon turned a deaf ear. And by the following morning he had already ordered every child, woman and old man to be armed against the sorceress. But before this second assault could take place, when all were gathered in the great hall, the Lady appeared out of nowhere, ethereal, and gave warning without ceremony. “Mark my words well, infamous king. Stay your will, or in the attempt your vassals, your fields and your animals will die. You will lose your sovereignty, you will lose your loved ones, your wife and the son you are expecting”.

But Egon did not desist and had his orders carried out. Many of his subjects and friends fled to save their lives. Among them, your mother and I. That same night those who had stayed loyal fell ill, as did the livestock and the crops. There was no one who could lift a sword. The curse of the White Lady came in the form of a fog and stayed for five years.

As the years went by, with great effort the region was repopulated, bringing the kingdom back to life. King Egon continued his reign; he seemed to learn from so severe a punishment, but stupidity would not depose him in favour of reason, and today those mad ambitions of youth seem to be reborn in the last stage of his life. These times of peace have been no more than a deceit, in order to rearm his army and slake his thirst for revenge. The Blind Hunters will lead his new affront, mercenaries half man, half beast, necromancers capable of making Leviathan himself abdicate. They will leave no stone unturned until they find the sorceress. It is said that she is the soul of these forests and that she can take any form at will. That only in the citadel is she seen in her human form, where she defends the ancestral secrets that man has now forgotten. Within the castle walls her power is infinite, which is why they try to hunt her down outside its bounds. I pray only that those monsters be annihilated by the sorceress.

We must take good heed of the warning in order to survive this barbarity. I suspect they mean to finish the White Lady so that afterwards, with no opposition whatever, they may seize our town, visiting their revenge on each one of us.

― It is incredible, Father, what you tell me. How can there be such a ruler, and citizens who let themselves be governed by him? Is man not capable of making his own decisions, is he not free to give his life the most sensible direction, the one in which happiness and balance among equals prosper?

I shall never understand what lies at the root of that debasing feeling called selfishness. It does not work if, for some to grow, many others must sink into the most undesirable misery. I am not going to demand of you an explanation for all this, since you have none and there never will be one.

As you rightly say, the warning will be obeyed and we shall take care not to cross the boundary. Hoping that the White Lady, reality or legend, puts an end to the stupid crusade of him who believes he holds unbreakable borders for free land, of him who confuses respect with fear of steel, of him whose blue blood blinds his intelligence.

Well, Father, let us accept calmly the times it has fallen to us to live in.

― You speak wisely; I rejoice to hear such words. The only wish of your mother and myself has been to instil in you a valuable and cultivated education. Your sensitivity and intelligence have shaped an upright man. Able to decide his own destiny, able to reason and with the will to listen.

― And for that I am tremendously grateful to you. I am happy beside you, living in this valley, living alongside my fellows… -the young man raised his head, his gaze lost in the ceiling; his father watched him-. Father… I know the moment is inappropriate, but lately I have been somewhat bewildered by this feeling that is taking shape in my heart. My fingers long for the forms of woman they imagine, caressing the void, to become tangible. I want a companion to fill, to the end of my days, with this love that overflows me…

― A pretty way of describing your desire, my son. Verse adorns the lash of the storm. I hope that soon your happiness may grow, becoming your will, and if for that you must cross the borders of our town, so be it. We shall always be here awaiting your return.

When I think of love, I always remember that medicine tells us that man harbours the power to fall ill and bring about his own death, sad as it is, but also that we can awaken feelings able to make us live eternally.

― For the present, Father, we shall have to wait to learn the outcome of this quarrel, and when it ends we shall act. Soon I shall be twenty-six; I am glad to live here, and though it terrifies me to venture into a harrowing outside world, there is born in me the desire to walk Europe and carry out my search.

― Well, my son. I hope all this ends in the best possible way, and that your journey may set out soon. By the way, now that the town is encircled, be more careful with the harp; there is no one here who can make so fine a string.

― Do not worry, Father. I have string to spare to repair it for a whole decade.

― I am glad, then. Now I shall go out to make sure all the citizens know the news.

― Very well, Father; as soon as I finish I shall prepare supper for your return.

Frantisek repaired the instrument and did the household chores. The evening with his family went by as usual, and when the hour for sleep came the young man slipped out to the garden, where his music was awaited among the hedges.

The next morning the prohibition came into force. No one would dare go into the beechwoods knowing that the Blind Hunters prowled on their nags in search of their quarry.

So, living under a strange calm, the days went by without news. Only the horns of the necromancers were heard, confirming their presence from dawn to dusk.

Ω

One of those mornings, Claude went up to his son.

― Frantisek, I know you like to play the harp in the furthest part of the garden at midnight. But yesterday I had a nightmare, a bad foreboding that is still awake. Do not take this wish as a whim or an imposition, but I do not want you so close to the forest. Your melody might attract the hunters. Please, my son, knowing as I do how much the silent shadows inspire you, I ask you to play near the houses before everyone goes to bed.

― Do not worry, Father, I accept your wish without reproach. I understand your concern in ill-fated moments like this. I shall use the supper hour to play; trouble yourself no more.

And so the young man did. When the day was done, while the neighbours withdrew into their houses before sleep. Frantisek played his instrument sitting beside his house. But those scores tasted different; they did not vibrate as on earlier nights at the brink of the small hours, bucolic among roses, lit by the fireflies.

The young man waited until his parents were asleep and slipped out to the garden, where he plucked the strings softly so as not to be heard. That night, however, the music did not flow clear; his father’s words echoed whenever his eyes went to the forest. A horn sounded nearby, and he noticed that the hind who visited him every night was absent. He feared for her, and for the first time feared for his own life, knowing that a few yards away he might meet the hunters. Distressed, he turned for home, cursing the monarch’s folly. He was walking in silence, brooding, when a sharp thud sounded behind him; he turned warily and saw an arrow on the ground. In the distance, among the shadows, figures were running. The hind appeared from the undergrowth in terror, with a shaft sunk in her thigh, while the hooves of hurrying horses could be heard. Frantisek threw himself on the animal, bringing her down, dragging her in among the bushes, pressing her against his body to keep her still. Silence. The hunters halted at the boundary; their white eyes under the cloaks scanned for their prey. One of the sightless ones entered the garden; he was walking towards the pair, following the trail of blood. The pulse held. The figure stopped his feet beside them, an eternal moment, his breathing deep and hoarse. He turned on the spot, searching distrustfully, mounted his horse, looked one last time. The riders went back to the forest.

The horn sounded again. Stillness. Fear, taut, slowly released his muscles; the young man let go of the animal; slowly they came out from under the foliage. Frantisek looked for the animal’s tracks and blood, but only the necromancer’s footprints remained. The hind walked limping towards the young man, brushing against him gratefully, and turned for the forest.

― Wait! I do not know what miracle has spared us death. But you are wounded; you will not be able to flee again, and if I do not tend you it will become infected -the boy waited. The animal stopped, seemed to understand those words, came up to him, and they made for the house.

― I have taken out the arrow and dressed the wound. With a little rest you will soon be well. I leave you water and some fruit in case you are hungry. You may stay here as long as you wish. This was the room of Emmanuelle, my dog. I ask only that you make no noise, so that my parents do not wake. I hope you sleep well.

The hind answered with a warm look. The young man put out the candles and pulled the door to, leaving the animal nestled among the blankets.

The next day, at daybreak, the village woke. The parents were at breakfast when the boy came into the kitchen.

― I thought you wanted to sleep a little longer, my son.

― No, Mother, I went to see to our guest.

― What do you mean, Frantisek?

― Every night, when I go out to play the harp, a hind listens to my repertoire from beginning to end. Last night I could not help going out, and I found her wounded, pursued by the Blind Hunters. Fortunately they did not see us, and we came back home, where I tended her. She is asleep now.

― I am glad she is well and recovering, but you disregarded my request and exposed yourself to danger. That encounter could have ended in calamity.

― I know, Father. I answer for my actions; after last night’s encounter there will be no more outings.

― I hope so -said Claude gravely.

― And how has the hind woken, son?

― Well, Mother, the wound looks healthy; I hope it heals soon. I take it you will not mind her staying in the house until she can walk.

― Of course, look after her. Now have your breakfast; work awaits us.

Claude visited the storehouses and cellars, counting reserves, cataloguing qualities so as to assign each material its use according to grade. He readjusted provisions to feed the village for a year, should war lay waste the valley. Stella sewed clothes and made up cloth, mended dresses, curtains and shoes in the workshop. Frantisek worked in the kitchens, where provisions were prepared for the whole neighbourhood. The life of the village went on as usual in spite of the circumstances.

When the afternoon was over, all went home. Frantisek was walking beside his parents, conversing cheerfully with them, when the horns were heard in the distance. The search for the sorceress went on.

As they reached the house, heavy blows sounded on the door from within. The young man went ahead and opened the workshop door. The hind fled, whimpering over her wound, towards the forest. He ran after her; Claude and Stella shouted to catch the young man’s attention, but he, bent on saving the animal, did not heed his parents.

― FRANTISEK, come back!! -they ran after him and stopped at the boundary.

They prayed that nothing bad would happen. The horns bellowed very near. The boy was about to reach the hind when horses’ hooves cracked at his back. An intense pain, the heavy blow against the stones, everything turning dark. He gave a deafening cry and fell unconscious.

Ω

What happened in that instant no one remembers. However, the young man woke some time later, lying on a mat, in a chamber of rock. The whisper of a stream could be heard nearby. He woke aching and dizzy, beside a bowl of water and a candle lighting his face. In the background, the murmur of a song that often sounded on his harp. In the half-light he made out the figure of a woman.

― You have saved me… thank you -the young man stammered.

― You did not do well to go into the forest. That hunter could have finished you. Three arrows poisoned with magic lodged in your back; I do not know how you are still alive. Give thanks to your constitution of oak, and that I was able to draw them almost at once, preventing the poison from tainting your being.

― Tell me, who are you?

― Only a hermit of the forest.

― And the hind, did she escape? Did you see her?

― I found you alone, on the ground, no one else.

― Ahh… -a stifled sigh-. I must go home; my parents are worried about me.

― You must keep to your bed and take the draught I have prepared for you if you want to stay alive. If your parents worry you so much, you should not have left the town. But be easy, I have sent a nightingale as messenger; it carries the word that you are still alive. Someone in the village will read it and your parents will know. Lie down again and recover your strength.

― Yes… thank you again. I owe you my life; I do not know how to repay your kindness.

― The only coin in payment will be rest, so that when you wake your harp may sound again.

― A moment ago I thought I heard the melody of a piece of my own… I must have been delirious. But how do you know I am a harpist?

― You were murmuring harmonies in your sleep, and the hardened tips of your fingers give you away.

― You are perceptive, without doubt…

The young man fell asleep from sheer tiredness. The woman stayed at his side, keeping vigil, attentive to any suffering.

Ω

Meanwhile, in the town, it was already known by all that Frantisek had disappeared in the forest. Worry took hold of every neighbour; no one dared cross the borders of the beechwoods. Not even Claude, who waited beside his wife in the garden, could find the strength to go after his son; he feared certain death.

Stella, unable to wait a second longer, clinging to her husband, said, “we cannot let our son die, and if he is dead already, I want his body. I know you are afraid to go in alone, afraid to leave a woman widowed and orphaned of her son. But I cannot bear this feeling that burns me; let us both go in and find death if it is our fate”.

Resolved, they were taking the step that crossed the border when a nightingale alighted beside them. It carried a message tied to its leg. The man read its words, “father, mother, I am waiting hidden in the forest; I shall return when the hunters ride far off. Frantisek”. The couple dissolved in tears. Relieved, they went home for food and clothing to bear the wait in the garden, accompanied by some of the neighbours.

Ω

The moon was already halfway through the small hours. The candle was almost burnt down when the young man opened his eyelids again; his gaze met the night-blue eyes of the woman. She was young, snow-pale of complexion, and her black wavy hair fell over her shoulders.

― This is the most beautiful awakening I have ever had. You are as fair as you are generous -he gazed, absorbed, at the golden face. His longings, his melodies seemed to find their essence in the woman who was tending him.

― You are very special, boy; I sense that you harbour a great gift… -Frantisek did not answer; he stared, he scrutinised that gaze, those eyes.

― Your eyes… they are…

― What of them?

― Say that I am dazed by the poison. But you look as the hind looks who listens to my music every night. And whom I tried so clumsily to save, finding myself here now -the woman smiled. Frantisek checked himself, overcome; memories flooded his mind, those indigo eyes, the hummed melody, the sorceress’s magic-. …Ahhh… you… you are the hind, the White Lady, which is why you knew my song. Who else could have saved me from certain death? -the young man sat up on the cot, wincing at his wounds.

― You are not yet recovered, Frantisek. Take this infusion; you will sleep without waking until the poison has gone -the boy obeyed, absorbed, and fell asleep.

Ω

Several days passed during that healing sleep. The night was deep and the horns resounded more and more often. The echoes of the army reached the town, where a faint lantern lit the faces of the parents still waiting in the garden.

Egon, impatient, had sent his army of a thousand men to beat the forest, led by the necromancers. Amid that thunderous whisper that silenced the beechwoods, a message made its way through, a sensation that eased the parents’ suspense. From that warm breeze they understood that their son was still alive, hidden beside the White Lady. They went home with half a smile, soothed by that voice so familiar.

Ω

During those days the Blind Hunters advanced their positions. Their black magic cancelled the defences, and the citadel was being besieged by the armies of the king of Dalmatia.

The White Lady remained beside the young man, even though her absence from the forest meant defeat. The secrets she guarded could not fall into malevolent hands; she wished to go out and defend the fortress, but that young man, the harpist, deserved all her care. The decision was made: she would put off parting from him until the last instant. After the fifth day, Frantisek woke from his lethargy, tired but safe. She was smiling; their eyes met. He returned the gesture.

― Now that you are out of danger, I shall go with you to the edge of the town. Once there you must promise me that you will never cross the boundary while the necromancers remain in the forest. Let us go.

― Wait! -he exclaimed, pulling at her hand; she questioned him with her eyes-. I want to stay with you -she paused an instant before answering, and he laid his fingers on her lips, silencing the reply-. Let me go with you; every day and every night, on your return, I shall play my harp to sweeten the bitterness of this war -the warmth of the fingers on her lips; she did not know what to say, and the young man’s gaze overcame everything.

― I will not prevent you from coming with me, but it is not safe for you, Frantisek. I do not even know whether I shall come out of this the victor. Please, go home, play your harp from your window; I shall hear every one of its notes wherever I am, believe me.

― I cannot leave now that I have found you at last. Do not ask me why, but I feel something strange that is born in my chest and runs through my whole body. All these years giving voice to my heart through my notes. Before you I feel each of those melodies become tangible. In your gaze, in your smile, in your cadence. I shall go with you in spite of any danger -the young man declared, fixing his eyes on her, less than a hand’s breadth from her face.

She said no more; she accepted, took the young man’s hands firmly, opened the door, and the two slipped away swiftly, imperceptibly, eluding the armies until they reached the citadel.

― We have arrived. We are in the crystal tower, non-existent for those who look from outside, but a great hall with broad cellars and lovely gardens. From now on this will be your home and, at the same time, your prison. You may not leave here while the hosts lie in wait over the region.

It is my mission to keep alive this sanctuary, the last redoubt of Mother Earth; here the murmurs of the trees can still be heard, the whispering of the beasts; here the spirit of the planet still speaks. In the beginnings of my life, the domains I guarded were lost on the horizon; now they are no more than barren lands at the hands of man’s zeal for destruction. My power wanes as Mother Earth weakens. And if I allow The Grove of the White Blackbird to live among these forests, it is because you have learnt to grow in balance, which is, to my mind, the only way humanity has of surviving. It is sad, but if it goes on like this, the rest of the human race will drown in its own poison.

― I understand your reasoning and I share it. But what is to be done beyond where your hand does not reach? Where Egon’s power is trifling compared with the great empires. Why die for what no longer admits of remedy?

― A fragile position. I have failed in my duty, we have all failed; now only I remain, and I shall go on to the end. Here we were born, under the tutelage of the spirit of the earth; she instilled in us knowledge and beliefs that go beyond human understanding. The valley and I are joined; if it dies, I fall too.

I understand your view; I know this must be the last battle, but I cannot sit and listen to the dying lament of the one who has given me everything. It is a matter of faith.

Now it is time for me to leave. Await my return, and until then do not pry among these walls.

With her last words the woman vanished. The young man was left in the middle of that hall, contemplating the grandeur of everything around him. That tower, tumbledown and archaic on the outside, hid an immense wealth behind the veil of magic. Every shelf full of books: art, philosophy, literature, music, political science, religion, mathematics, natural science, the history of man from the remote civilisations to the present ones, told from every perspective. The amalgam of knowledge that the human race has minted.

Among those books the treatise on miracles stood out; his hand went to it without his willing it, and on touching the spine an uncontained energy ran through him from head to foot. He thought it was the magic that protected those works, but the sensation did not cease; it eased, but it ran through his being; he connected with the Earth fleetingly, yet understanding the sorceress’s wish to give her life for the spirit within.

He went on looking: the high ceilings, where five great oculi opened and lit that space. It was dusty, as though no one had lived in it for many years; in fact, the only visible footprints were the young man’s. A study table with candles, several armchairs and a divan of black velvet. At the far end, the great entrance door, and directly opposite, a small door among the shelves, opened by pulling a knocker with a stag’s face, veiled the garden. The vine grew wild, forming a great dome, and honeysuckle covered the stones of the walled fortress. So lovely a place, bordered with dahlias, roses and violets of heady perfume, took on a taciturn grace under its feigned neglect.

Several stone benches; a small stream flowed from a bronze dolphin. The young man sat down, cradled by that atmosphere, took up the harp and began to play, empty of thought. Overwhelmed by the sensation of having found the face, the voice, the gesture of the woman glimpsed in the gloom. Not only had he found her, but the White Lady herself had come to meet him; she ran through his mind, blazing. She…

The garden was full of fireflies dancing to the rhythm of those notes, free, heightened, jubilant, like a “Rêve d’amour” by Franz Liszt, had it been written by then. For hours he played, entranced; the plucked notes arose of themselves, hypnotic.

On the other side of the wall, the essence of those lands was struggling against the armies of unreason commanded by necromancers. The forest was full of traps that brought the soldiers down by the dozen. But more were those undone by the Blind Hunters, whose dark arts uncovered every stratagem of the White Lady. The day was coming to its end, and with night the hosts rested. They were in no hurry; they knew that winning was only a matter of besieging the sorceress without respite; little by little her strength would wane and the last of her kind would breathe her final breath.

As much the last as those sightless hunters, druids of the forests all of them, but walkers of opposite paths. That everlasting existence was the gift given in exchange for watching over Mother Earth, but the three necromancers, in their craving for that immortality with power free of any yoke, defied the great spirit. As punishment they were left blind and disfigured, obliged to live with that terrible shame to the end of their days. Their revenge: to annihilate all the guardians of the spirit. Not knowing that after the ending of the last of them they would meet death too, extinguishing their race.

The king of Dalmatia did not turn from his obstinate and senseless craving: to dominate every region, to find the grandiose treasures the city kept and the uncertain knowledge that would raise him to emperor. Yet what that despot did not know, and Frantisek would discover shortly afterwards, was that all those treasures Egon so desired, those countless riches and powerful witcheries, did not exist.

The White Lady guarded no tangible plenty; her greatest possession went beyond all human ambition. That treasure was written in ink and contained the deepest knowledge of man and of the universe. Writings that reached back beyond ancient Jericho and Mesopotamia, books that had seen the great lion of the Nile raised. The crystal tower housed the ark of knowledge, the learning that frees the soul, which grows once detached from matter. What most of those handbooks offered did not trouble the druid. The Blind Hunters would fall with her, and the books of sorcery had no value for anyone who was a stranger to the earth. It was the scientific volumes that truly kept her awake. The monarch’s intellectual abilities would never have allowed him to separate salt from water. But among his retinue were hidden gifted minds sunk in malevolence.

If those texts came into cruel hands, the king would soon be overthrown and murdered, the people bent and manipulated at the whim of a new caste. Just as cures and elixirs were written down, so were poisons and great evils. The learned would rule countries, empires and continents, sickening rivers, fields and herds, the whole world at their feet to avoid contagion, to obtain the remedy for the most perverse diseases.

Ω

The days went by between those walls. On her arrival, he played the harp while she rested, and when both were awake she told of the countless secrets stacked on the shelves, the places they came from and the beginnings of each science. Some came from the library of Alexandria, from the temple of Lhasa, others from countless places on the planet.

The ban was lifted. The young man, now that he knew every detail, could read whatever he wished and go down to the cellars to widen his knowledge, whiling away those long solitary hours.

Ω

On the battlefield the struggle continued. The hosts advanced, rendering every effort useless. Reaching the ruins was a matter of days.

In The Grove of the White Blackbird all that commotion did not pass unnoticed. The hard battle being fought in the forests let the howls of pain of the fallen soldiers be heard. Many were the soldiers who arrived wounded in the town to be tended, and in spite of that the Grovefolk feared those winds of misfortune. The White Lady could not defend the region for much longer.

Claude and Stella suffered the worry. Their son’s messages arrived later and later. Though sparing of words, they held enough detail to let them understand that he was safe beside the sorceress. Frantisek’s parents did not know what exactly the young man’s part was in that war, but they trusted in the courage of his ideals and the nobility of his heart.

That conflict would bring the valley to its destruction: too many soldiers forced to die for a chimera, the future of the town and its citizens, their son’s life in danger and a guardian in her death throes.

Faced with such a prospect, it was preferable to pay homage to so stupid a king rather than go on with such madness. Preferable to offer the little gold of those lands, the submission of its people, and leave the fate of that valley to chance. Claude was resolved to mediate with the king of Dalmatia and with the White Lady so as to end the war. The deluded ideals of a stray people of dreamers were not worth the death of all its inhabitants. When all was said and done, by offering themselves they would be no more than slaves under one of the many kings that have been, are and will be.

Stella was coming into the house when she discovered her husband preparing a bundle. The look on his face gave him away; something serious was on his mind.

― I know that face. You are not thinking of going out to look for your son.

― I know well that he is under the White Lady’s keeping; his music gives heart to our benefactress. I am proud of his work, slight as it may be.

But this struggle is turning in favour of the warlocks in spite of every effort. The forest is being razed, and when nothing is left, whoever wins, our town will have no meaning. Surrender is preferable to allowing the death of everything around us. And of course, as a father, I want my son to go on living.

― Then, Claude, speak to her first; she is sure to listen to reason. Egon is an embittered despot; he will not even let you enter the castle, and he will punish you for your daring.

― She has no power, and her surrender will not be enough. If this senseless battle continues it will not be gold, lands or homage. Stella, should those Blind Hunters reach the citadel, there will be something far more important at stake for that stupid monarch.

― However much I beg you, you will not change your mind. I shall go with you to the castle whether you like it or not.

― The road is dangerous and the punishment will be terrible. We shall ride at full trot, a day and a night, and I doubt our success.

― I accept the risk, but perhaps it would be better to walk the path along the precipice. It is hard to reach. There is a full moon and the night will be our ally. If we are fortunate, we shall reach the castle in four nights. I know it is a long time, but if we are going to do this, at least let failure come as late as possible.

The decision taken, Claude and Stella waited until dusk was near. All the neighbours already knew of their departure, and although they agreed indignantly, many wishing to take their place on the journey, they offered all their help for the furtive leaving, and at the end of the day the couple set out.

Ω

When she woke, he was there, before her. His gaze softened her heart. The hidden tower of the citadel gave off warmth. The White Lady slept peacefully, rocked by the music of the young man, who at each return waited for her playing his harp, stopping time. Her grave countenance turned sweet and glad on contemplating Frantisek’s face. That face radiated a feeling the woman had long ago locked away in her depths. The absence eased; she no longer felt alone, and she ended by letting forgotten love into her heart. They barely exchanged words, but the smiles, the caresses were enough for understanding; their gazes ended by melting into a kiss, and though brief, they overflowed in those instants of meeting.

The days passed imperceptibly, as if every yesterday were today. Shut in the citadel, for Frantisek time and that war became visible only in the woman’s tired face. The hunt slackened at dusk and at dawn, the moment when the druids recovered their strength.

The days already numbered more than fifty-one, and the offensive continued. The sorceress showed the young man her knowledge and the secrets of the castle; she trusted him to safeguard the most important things in his memory. The young man matured as his spirit grew richer. He understood all knowledge, finding balance with the universe; now he heeded his lover’s reasons: that knowledge was forbidden to common men; only those in communion with the earth would put it to good use.

The withholding of such wisdom was necessary; it had to be handed over slowly, so avoiding great wars under the command of a dominating society. Had they known of the existence of the citadel, the clans of power would have moved heaven and earth to gain such lordship. Their theatre of shadows, created to deceive humanity, so subtly woven, so elaborated over the centuries, would be meaningless if all the absolute truths came to light, giving man back his spirit. Impossible to allow those books to fall into the wrong hands.

Frantisek was opening his eyes; the force of light struggled against the men who dwelt in darkness. Everything around him was altered. The White Lady did not come from other worlds of legend; she was no demonic witch; her magic was a gift of the earth and her end the most just.

She was one of the last inhabitants of an age-old civilisation that never forgot its roots. Its knowledge was strengthened by fusing with nature, acquiring full power. A race that lived in the golden age of man, very far off in time, disowned by the era of steel and fire; its culture was closer to reality than we are today.

But for the young man such a treasure meant no more than the immeasurable love he felt for her. He did not suffer for that war in search of power, but for the harm befalling the woman he loved. His heart shrank at each of her goings, of her arrivals, at seeing her ever paler, wounded and exhausted. Frantisek lavished her with tenderness and attentions; he wished to appear untouched by the battle, so that she might believe for brief instants that within the castle walls nothing was happening. But his heart, long-suffering, ended by mastering his will.

The next morning, when the woman had gone, as he sat in the garden, the harp sounded angry; the blazing notes spoke of that feeling which seized his heartbeat.

Suddenly the lament fell mute. Briskly he knotted a bundle and filled it with the brightest metals, with the largest precious stones, with treatises on medicine and books of sorcery, all of it capable of sating boundless greed. Nothing important enough to confer power, but enough to silence that war, if to those treasures were added dominion over the valley and the surrender of the White Lady. Frantisek was resolved to travel to the domains of the king of Dalmatia and negotiate the end of the conflict behind his love’s back. He knew the risks and the certain rejection; even so, he preferred to know her alive than to allow a death foretold.

Everything was ready for his departure. When night came, just before the small hours, she returned, kissed his lips and fell asleep amid his caresses, curled up under the sweet melody of the strings. The boy looked at her for the last time, knowing that after the act he was about to commit she would never accept him at her side again. Nevertheless, that decision was already made. The young man took to the forest under a threadbare cloak, at that moment of the day when magic fell silent. Not far off, on the Ram’s Precipice, his parents’ journey continued.

The parents were ahead of the young man by barely a few hours. Claude and Stella waited hidden beside a crag.

The sun stood high at noon; the sulphur of the battle could be smelt in the distance. The hosts were laying the forest waste, setting it ablaze along with all the creatures that lived in it. There was no spell left for the necromancers to undo. The White Lady was besieged; her cosmos expanded, trying to put out the flames that advanced, hemming her in from every point of the compass. Her strength was running out; she would not hold much longer, and her lament resounded on discovering her lover’s departure. Her effort lacked will now that her heart was broken.

Frantisek waited in hiding, his soul constricted, struggling against himself over what he was doing and what he left behind with every step.

The young man had left a brief note beside the harp that rested on the divan: “I am not abandoning you to your fate; I wish your fate to be something other than death. I am no thief in flight; I know there will be no forgiveness. But circumstances are stronger than our desire. Frantisek.”

Those lines… there was no need to search far to know what had happened. After reading the note she knew he had gone to the castle to negotiate the surrender.

― You, too young and too guileless to make any other decision. I, too old and too impetuous to let love make me lose my reason, to set the battle aside. That is why I let you go, but however much I want to, I cannot hold back the lament. It hurts me in the very depths of my heart…

Ω

The road went on, and after two daybreaks with their small hours the boy crossed the bounds of King Egon’s city. Slipping through the bustle, dodging the guards, behind the shadows, wary. He had managed to get as far as the castle. With a little wit and some gold he bought the favour of an old servant, and so learnt which was the balcony of the king’s hall and how to reach it.

He hid in the courtyard behind some barrels. He waited for darkness as an ally. A ladder gave him access to the second gallery, from which with one leap he gained the place he sought; he waited in silence for the right moment.

Ω

During those hours his parents were arriving in the city. They identified themselves to the guard, and Baron Guglielmo himself received them.

― I do not know how important the reason for your visit may be, but I seem to remember that you and your people were forbidden to cross the bounds of your town. Not only have you exposed yourselves to danger, you are also disobeying a direct order of your monarch.

Surviving the Blind Hunters may be a light punishment compared with what Egon can have done to you. I did not think you so foolish as to come this far, but bringing Stella with you passes my belief.

― We have not come so far to justify ourselves before you; our time is running out. It is a capital matter; let your king know that I demand an audience with him, Guglielmo.

― Do not get heated, my friend. You should know that at this moment there is no matter of greater importance to your brother than putting the White Lady to death, so every petition will be denied. What is more, his hearings are delegated to my judgement, so, making use of that honour, I must reject your proposal and have you arrested for contempt of a royal decree.

…However, on reflection, I cannot help thinking of the face Egon will make when he sees you enter the hall of audience and, without letting you utter a word, sentences you to prison for life or to the gallows. Eagerness and mockery invite me to disobey; you are lucky I am in a good mood today. This war has only hours left. When The Grove of the White Blackbird belongs to the crown, I shall be our king’s right hand, and we shall settle accounts then, Claude…

Now follow me; I shall escort you personally.

― Thank you -they both answered.

The baron dismounted and led the way through the castle to the royal hall. When they reached the antechamber the baron ordered the doors opened without announcement. The leaves flung wide. The king was admiring, enraptured, a pair of dancing girls moving to the music. His look of fury at the interruption gave way to a contorted grimace of surprise.

― AAAAHHHH!!! -an unbridled cry of rage. The musicians stopped and left the hall-. Claude!! Stella!! How dare you present yourselves before me?!! I shall have you hanged for such insolence!! And you, Guglielmo, I shall pull out your nails myself!!

Guards! Take these people to the dungeons, I cannot bear their presence a second longer! Come on, did you not hear me?!!

― Be silent and hear what I have to say!! -Claude stepped forward; the guard gripped their lances, stopping him-. We have not come all this way with the sole intention of dying before you have heard what we have come to beg of you, brother -the man took hold of his wife and walked to the centre of the hall.

The shout roused the young man from his daze, hidden in a recess of the balcony. He straightened, alert. His parents’ names rang through the whole castle. A cold feeling pierced him through. Stealing up to the window, he discovered his parents before the monarch; his heart was beating fast; he longed to appear beside them and defend them, but the vehemence with which his father spoke held him back.

― I beg you to hear our message, Egon. Many years ago, more than brothers, we were friends. For the sake of those years, grant us an audience.

― I beseech you -said the woman, kneeling. Claude remained standing.

― Do not speak of those years! I forbid it… -the monarch turned his back on them. Claude pulled at his wife to make her rise; she resisted-. Kneel, you too, Claude. Beg!!

― I ask you to…

― Guards!! -Claude knelt-. Beg!

― We have come…

― BEG!!

― O great king of Dalmatia -he said, with his head resting on the floor-, emperor without equal, adored by his people and worthy of history itself. I, your obedient and loyal subject, beg and entreat you, in your compassion without limits and your knowledge beyond counting, to grant your precious time to this undeserving one, according him an audience at this present moment.

― Please, Egon, hear our words -she said.

― Very good, Claude, I see you still remember the protocol with which a king is addressed. I grant you one minute. Speak and use your time well, but stay on your knees.

― We have come to ask you to stop the war…

― HA, HA, HA! Never; this war will end when I command it, which will be, neither more nor less, when the White Lady perishes! I shall seize her treasures and the valley of the fireflies will lie under my yoke, Grovefolk included.

― You are killing thousands of soldiers and the forest is already barren and ashen! We shall accept your sovereignty without conditions. I myself shall speak to the guardian, but put an end to this madness. What is a fruitless land worth, a king without an army, a disloyal people?

― Silence, fool!! Do I not speak plainly?! My decisions are immovable. My word is the law -he said, exasperated-. If you have nothing more to say, the dungeons await you.

― Wait, Egon!! -Frantisek, at the humiliation, was making up his mind to leap inside and fight. His mother’s voice stopped him-. There is something you must know before you have us locked away. We know your obstinacy and your want of judgement only too well. It was not on a whim that the whole court left your side.

― You are on a bad road, Stella!

― The child we saved from your madness twenty-five years ago is now with the White Lady. He lives with her in the citadel; if the siege continues, those Blind Hunters, whom you believe you command and who feign obedience with the sole aim of using you to seize the treasures you crave, will soon enter the ruins and put to death the druid and your son.

― NOOOO!!! -the monarch screamed, beside himself. The young man, still listening, froze on hearing the words-. I have no son! That monster ended the life of the only woman I loved and who loved me; her death will give me double satisfaction!!

― You are mad, Egon! You committed a wrong that left you alone; you defied nature once, and became a dying king, without a people and without friends.

― SILENCE!!

― Do you really think Ninon loved you?! She feared you, and she would have fled with us had she not died on the day of the birth!!

― I TOLD YOU TO BE SILENT!! That Ninon died was the fault of the White Lady, who poisoned my people and that child! When he and the witch perish, I shall be the great sovereign I always should have been, and your village will disappear from the map along with your absurd ideals.

Guards, take them where I cannot see them!!

― Enough!! -a voice exclaimed; a shadow behind the curtains. Someone had slipped into the hall without anyone noticing.

― Guards, seize the intruder! -the soldiers ran and laid hold of the young man, pushing him to the floor; the boy offered no resistance. They dragged him before the king-. Take off the cloak that hides his face; I want to see the face of this assassin before I end his life -the cloth slid away, revealing his face.

― Frantisek!!! -Stella and Claude exclaimed, overcome. Egon, surprised, drew near them.

― Well…! This was nothing but a subterfuge; you meant to keep me busy with all this chatter so that a criminal could finish me by treachery and so recover the crown -he said, drawing his sword on Claude.

― No, you idiot!! That is your son, and everything we have said is true.

― You lie!! You said he was with the White Lady, and here he is. That gives the lie to your wish to end the war to save his life.

Kill him right here, so they may see how far my power reaches!! -the monarch went up to the young man. With the edge of his sword he stripped away the cloths that covered him. The bundle fell to the floor, spilling the treasures it held. Greed lit up his face-. I would accuse you of theft from the kingdom of Dalmatia if I did not recognise these goods as another’s.

Tell me, boy, is this what the druid keeps behind the walls of the citadel? How much more like this is there?

― The citadel is nothing but ruins -kneeling, with eyes lowered, he answered-; I could scarcely fill another bundle with what I left there. The true treasure is the knowledge the White Lady keeps in her memory. You would do well to let her live and learn from her…

― Enough!! You have answered my question -the king walked round the young man. That deep, melodious voice-. Stand up! -Frantisek obeyed. Egon studied the young man’s features out of the corner of his eye. He came close and lifted his face, pushing up his chin with the sword. Their eyes locked. The sovereign could not hide his surprise. The young man had his mother’s features. That faint memory eased his rage-. Send for the wise men… I want these books read this very night. Take the boy to the tower of laments; I spare his life for now. Take these liars to the dungeons; tomorrow they will be hanged, so that the people may see what happens to traitors.

― NOOO!! -the young man screamed as he was dragged away.

― BASTARD!! Let my son go!

― STOP!! -cried a woman’s voice that rang through the whole palace. Everyone stood motionless.

A falcon was flying over the hall and came to rest on the floor. The bird shook itself, taking the shape of the White Lady.

― KILL HER!!! -the soldiers who were rushing forward stood paralysed; roots were growing through their armour-. Do not take one step more, harpy. I shall send for the Blind Hunters and they will wipe that stupid smile from your face.

― I fear you are in no position to threaten anyone, king of Dalmatia; there is not a single soldier able to run and raise the alarm, and your sightless spawn believe they are hunting a decoy that they will take some time yet to find out.

― Leave this place or I shall kill you myself, witch!!

― I fear not, my friend. Tonight I stand here, at the limit of my strength, to put you to death with my own hands.

― Frantisek, after reading the note I knew you had gone to the castle to negotiate my surrender, but you must know that my duty admits of no renunciation. The worth of the ideal goes beyond my blood; it is greater than the craving of a thousand despots. I meant to set my feelings aside and die, saving the secrets by it, but today I cannot keep my promise to Mother Earth. As soon as I felt you in danger, I devised a plan to draw off the hunters and save you from the hands of this murderer.

But not only that, Egon: you also meant to end the lives of his parents, your own brother. I knew you were foul, yet I swear you surprise me. I should have finished you years ago, when I had the chance. Unhappily we druids are not judges, but defenders. But this time you have passed every bound; you deserve death, then, even if I myself am punished for such an act.

― Do not come near me, White Lady!! -he said, trembling. Quickly he looked for a weapon; from the wall he took down a hunting bow and fitted an arrow to it. He drew the string-. Go, or I shoot!!

― I do not fear death, king; that shaft will do nothing to me. But you do seem to be afraid, Egon.

― Go!! -he screamed, crazed, loosing the string; the arrow buried itself in Stella’s side.

― AAAhhh…!! -she held back the pain.

― MOTHER!!!

― STELLA!!!

― White Lady, please, save her -Frantisek whispered. The druid went to her; she was bleeding freely, and the wound looked grave. She drew out the arrow and laid her hands on her body. The bleeding stopped.

Time that Egon used to find among his clothes a small vial, a liquid he poured over the point of another arrow, a poison given by the necromancers, able to put the sorceress to death. The bow was drawn again, treacherously. The shaft was aimed at the druid. A whistle crossed the hall. Frantisek threw himself into its path and collapsed, pierced through the chest. The White Lady took him in her arms.

― NOOOO!!! -the mother screamed.

― Ooohh!!! Frantisek…! I cannot save you… I have no strength left to fight their magic…

― Save him, druid, do not let my son die!!!

― I can barely feel the power of the earth; I can only keep him from suffering in the few minutes of life left to him! -the sorceress embraced the boy and cradled him in her lap. He stopped shivering; he seemed calm.

― …it no longer hurts… -he said with a smile. His mother wept, stroking his forehead.

― Frantisek… my son.

― Mother, and Father? I cannot see him.

― Here, son, I am beside you -said Claude at his feet.

― I am sorry, my love, but I cannot save your life. That poison was conjured to kill me, beyond remedy…

― It is all right… Hinam, I go happy knowing you are still alive, at my side and sharing my love. I am only sorry I left you; I thought it would turn out well, but I see it has not… Please, go, flee, save everything you fight for, accept this defeat; our ideas go beyond space and time… Kaff, kaff!

Mother… Father… forgive me, I only wanted to save you all… Kaff, kaff!

― Enough, Frantisek -the woman kissed his lips, and they stayed sealed until the last breath.

Claude flung himself forward in fury and with a hard slap knocked the monarch down.

― How dare you, Egon? You have killed your son, the son I have raised and loved as my own. At last you have had your wish. Be satisfied; your wife must be turning in her grave; she will renounce you to the last of days!! -the king looked at him, bewildered-. I curse you, brother. May your body burn in hell, and may you live what remains sunk in the certainty of knowing yourself a slaughterer of peoples. I hope you choke on the gold you find in the citadel!! -Claude stood still, waiting for the sovereign to give the order to lock them up. There he stayed, motionless, looking at the floor, stunned, as though he did not understand.

The White Lady paid no attention; she stayed with the young man in her arms, gazing at him, absorbed, with something broken in her heart. Stella wept inconsolably.

― Come on, do something, coward!! Give the order to your soldiers! -Egon let his weapons fall. Little by little he tilted and ended on the floor, lying on his back. His eyes reddened, full of tears.

― Kill me, brother. I do not deserve to live… -Claude was taken aback-. Take my sword and kill me…

― I am no murderer. Egon… live, now that at last you have understood. Live with this burden and suffer…

― The war is over, White Lady… gather your treasures and go. Stella, thank you for having raised my son. Give him a fine burial.

Let them go.

Claude took his son in his arms and with his wife left the hall, leaving the castle far behind. The sorceress watched the monarch, took a few steps and stood at his back. She knelt beside him and said in his ear:

― You desired power so much that you corrupted your soul, and now, slayer of peoples and of your own child, you weep the sorrow.

I shall grant you the greatest gift the earth bestows, my last enchantment. We shall add to your life a day and a night for every life you took, and I assure you that you will count more than a hundred years. Forget all thought of suicide, for there will be no blade and no disease that can end you. You will walk the rest of your life suffering your own punishment -the woman kissed his forehead, and blood clouded his eyes.

― AAAAAHHHH!!! -he screamed, twisted with pain, writhing across the floor.

The White Lady disappeared through the window in the shape of a barn owl. Egon searched for the sword, howling like a dog; he gripped it, trying to drive it into his heart, to sever his neck, but he could not. He could only go on living, suffering that terrible curse.

Ω

The war had ended. The troops withdrew at the monarch’s order. All went home. The valley was laid waste, but The Grove of the White Blackbird still stood; soon its citizens would help restore that lovely place.

The news lifted their spirits, but the death of the harpist saddened their hearts. That afternoon everyone gathered in the forest. There were no headstones or niches; bodies were buried in the ground, beside a tree on which was carved the name of those who lay there. For Frantisek they chose a very special oak: from a branch of that very tree had been taken the wood from which the young man’s harp was carved. Beneath his name ran the following legend, “He who loves without measure, he who knows true love, is he who gives his life without misgiving”.

So he was buried and long remembered, his smile, his music, his being, in exchange for peace in the valley of the fireflies. At such a gesture the universe listened and gave the road a new course.

Amid the lament a sad melody sounded, like the sound of Vaughan Williams’s Tallis Fantasia arriving from very far away. So the White Lady took leave of her beloved. Sitting in the garden where every evening she used to find Frantisek. Plucking the harp, her eyes closed amid sighs. And though she knew, she went on playing while the Blind Hunters drew near her imperceptibly. They closed round her and, without pity, the three ran her heart through with the edge of a dagger.

She whispered a few words and her body dissolved, rising on the breeze in the form of petals. The three druids stood motionless; their white eyes went out and they fell away, turned to ash, until they disappeared.

With them went the last vestiges of a race born of the earth. With the death of the last druid who walked the light, all those gone over to the dark would fall.

Of the citadel nothing is recorded. It simply disappeared, like the druids, taking all that knowledge with it.

Ω

According to what the people who live in the valley of the fireflies tell to this day, every twenty-five years, on the very day the two first met, in the depths of the forest the laughter of a man and a woman can be heard, running about to the sound of a harp…

The Cartographer of the Crystal Sea

Day 0

My name is Sakura Ikegami. My name means cherry tree; my father gave it to me in honour of my grandfather’s memory. He was fascinated by watching the cherry trees come into blossom at dusk, their birth, the white mantle of scent that covered the ground, the flight of those butterfly wings at every gust of wind.

My grandfather was a man of scientific cast, very rigorous, little given to merriment and humour, a temperament proper to the land of the rising sun, where he had spent a great part of his life. He belonged to a new race, a small group known as the new colonists.

After the Second World War, Hiroshima and Nagasaki, hidden from the population, became a laboratory for genetic tests on humans. Yet it was not the deformities or the unheard-of diseases that poured research and millions in interests into those zones punished by the divine will of man.

What was hoped for among those ruins were men and women whose mutations might confer on them a superhuman value.

The years went by one after another, so many that in time both cities rose again from their ashes, leaving the nuclear holocaust to oblivion. What an agony, so terrible that I cannot manage to imagine it, that of those people, those villagers, peasants who lived off the land doing no harm, people chosen at random to behold the grandeur of the nuclear bomb, defenceless people burnt alive, people who would suffer the stigma of radiation from parents to children.

Japan declared itself neutral; it would never again take part in a war. There was no need to suffer such an experience again to learn the lesson. Though I suppose the fear of the nuclear did not take hold as it should have among those leaders who trusted, perhaps pushed by money, in the energy of fission.

And as if it were a nightmare from which one cannot wake, more than half a century later those islands suffered the lash of the sea; Hokusai’s great wave rose, darkening the sky, hiding the land under the waters of the tsunami that would drown the dreams of thousands of people. The water came in spectral, under the hypnotised gaze of those to whom fear was kind, and whose agony lasted a single instant.

At times death is unjust and snatches life from us as children, as young people with a future. So unjust that it strikes twice on the same people, and grandparents and grandchildren meet in the place where souls are lost. But that natural catastrophe would not be remembered for what it meant in itself.

The tsunami destroyed the Fukushima nuclear power station, and the radiation, though it tried to tiptoe past, reached part of the population. There were people exposed directly, people who breathed that contaminated air thousands of kilometres away, people who ate the food the sea gave. The radiation flew invisible and entered the inheritance of the earth, the roots of the forests, the beasts and men.

The authorities denied it, and the world believed what the know-alls on the subject affirmed, confirmed and assured.

The research teams gathered up their bundles and travelled north in search of what the Enola Gay had not achieved. This time there was no long wait for what was so desired. Those affected by the radiation who were in turn descendants of those exposed to the nuclear bomb dropped like flies: it was what in private circles was known as the double combo. The mutation did not wait to deform the children; it made its victims burst from within. But for every thousand dead, one survivor.

Day 1

They barely came to twenty, but they were enough to keep hundreds of scientists busy, experimenting on the flesh, in situ, with those supernatural gifts. Those men and women were immune to all the diseases so greatly feared: cancer, Alzheimer’s, influenza, cholera…; there was nothing, not even the most virulent strains, capable of altering the strength of those bodies. Not only that: they aged more slowly, wounds healed sooner, physical power was multiplied, their intellectual indices were those of the gifted, and their reactions faster.

The world did not learn of the existence or the christening of a new race, “Homo Rex Sapiens”. Science amused itself breaking down every one of the atoms that make up its anatomy, but the trials to confer the gift on those already born were useless. The gift could only be inherited by being born of a mutant, so the alternative was procreation. Rather than pleasures, they were rapes, which, though inhuman, were by far less painful than the tests.

The procedure was laid down by order of arrival according to purchasing power. The most powerful men and women inseminated or were inseminated so that their line might form part of the future history of humanity. Nevertheless, a small percentage of the children of the new world had been reserved for the most privileged minds, as well as for the most prodigious athletes.

Surprisingly, among those infants there were no great differences beyond the facial features proper to their parents. They were immoderately superhuman. The new race received every kind of pampering and attention. Soon they showed an intelligence far superior to that of the people who looked after them. Such was the brilliance of these new colonists that their existence became common knowledge.

Before long the talents stood out, and humanity acclaimed the values of the new race, in which it placed its faith and hope for a future for the planet.

In spite of being a race born of war, of destruction, of egotism and ambition, their privileged minds were far closer to the spirit than those of any other man.

As on a chessboard, the great ruling families lost their pieces to these men and women, given to saving the planet, whose balance they understood to be necessary to preserve the species. A curious thing, given their upbringing, whose ultimate purpose had been to wrest power from the hand that had rocked the cradle.

The great Global Law was established without ceremony. Zero polluting energies, recycling of absolutely everything, sustainable development, birth control, eradication of the first and third worlds, disarmament of the nations, etc. The utopia so desired by some, hated by many, was simply the only road to follow, and it became tangible in a matter of five years. Undoubtedly the world was a better place, at least for humanity; yet the harm we had done to mother earth was an irreparable evil.

Faced with the imminent threat of a planet unable to maintain the state that allowed us to live, the moon was colonised with the founding of “New Babylon”, a bridge city between Mars and the Earth. In a matter of fifteen years the city of “Valhalla” rose over prairies inhabited by terrestrial creatures long extinct. The purity of the air was breathed lightly among those hundreds of bubbles that drew its architecture.

New Babylon and Valhalla were carried out in the most absolute secrecy. The new colonists began to travel to what would be their new planet; soon only a few remained, and close to the great cataclysm all powers had been delegated. No one knew exactly when the foundations of the earth would shake.

Valhalla watched attentively each of the last heartbeats of an era. Humanity rose that morning with the same routine as anyone who feels death far off. It must have been twelve noon when the Shibuya district shook for the first time, 1.2 on the Richter scale. Fear gave way to calm, and the morning television shows joked about Godzilla snoring under the seas. Nothing could have been further from the truth; no kaiju would lay Japan waste, nor any nuclear force.

The second lash was felt from Tierra del Fuego to Quebec, by way of South Africa, Iceland and Thailand. That ovation of terror was an echo that could be heard from Valhalla. For a few instants silence and stillness; the next tremor split the Atlantic and opened the Mediterranean; the land was dismembered on one side while on the other the sea rose, swallowing everything in its path.

Life, as we knew it, ceased to exist. The continents were redrawn under a black sky cast by volcanic violence. Mother earth, in spite of having been treacherously poisoned by her children, was just, and death lasted a sigh.

The Earth was beginning a process of regeneration, a new era of uncertainty: what would be the future of what had been our home? Would it be again what it was? Could we ever settle on it again? Or would it become a barren rock of ice and fire? How long could the human race live on Mars? Would we be bound to accept that every beginning carries its end, or would we refuse to set aside a second golden age? Will balance be possible on Mars, or will the new humanity repeat the disasters that led it to its own destruction?

It is probably too soon to determine our destiny, and though it will reach its end sooner rather than later, contained in the eternity of the universe, the true value of our stay lies in how we have walked the road.

Day 2

I shall take up the beginning of this account again, just where I was speaking of my grandfather, Raiden Ikegami; his name is that of the god of thunder in Japanese culture. Each of those children, in a display of arrogance, was christened with the name of a divinity.

My grandfather, as I have said, was a sober, frugal man whose greatest pleasure was to enjoy the blossoming of the cherry tree. He lived his work with fervour: biomechanical architecture. His task consisted in developing biomolecular structures assembled in the laboratory and digitally programmed. The bubbles of Valhalla, which made life possible on the red planet, had been the work of his genius.

Beside him stood out my grandmother, Baruna, and her good friend Saga, biotechnologist and anatomical-mechanical engineer respectively; they formed a trinity in research praised among their peers. Biodegradable implements, improvements in crops and beasts, solutions to the new diseases, replacement of any part of the body, etc.

However, my civilisation refuses to create intelligent life in the laboratory. A moral principle, perhaps old-fashioned, and a fear forbid it. Life must be created in the way nature herself has arranged for it. It does not matter whether the new life is raised by two fathers, two mothers, or one alone; the source of the love children receive is not questioned, but we do believe that nature does an unsurpassable job in something as beautiful as the advent of life. All this is sheltered under the principle of creation, sometimes unjust, but respected and observed.

As for the fear: dread of what was once known as high-AI robots. Our technology allows us to create beings of wholly human appearance, biomechs that need to eat, sleep, laugh. Digitally programmed beings that can develop intellectually by themselves, to the point where they could be considered intelligent life. But if the creation of these forms were permitted, besides breaking the principle of creation, it would favour the development of a mechanical civilisation parallel to the human one, capable of banishing or annihilating us.

Simplistic or not, these decisions govern our society along with many others, and it seems to work well. In fact, we have reached the fourth generation of new colonists while the first is dying out.

And it is not that there were only scientists among our people; all the concerns of the human being are represented, and there is a priestess, an amalgam of the many religions and new beliefs, to whom we turn when the spirit torments us.

Nevertheless, I speak of my grandfather, perhaps because he was closer; I resemble him physically, and I was always more attached to him than to my grandmother. And like Raiden, my path had been that of biomechanical architecture, not so much to continue the family wake as because of the tremendous fascination the bubbles aroused in me. Curiosity to discover why they are able to withstand all the climatic vicissitudes of Mars except those of the Crystal Sea, a gigantic surface of quartz and mineral salts that reflect the sunlight as if it were a great pool of water; the gases these stones give off corrode the composition of the bubbles, making any attempt to inhabit the area impossible.

As an architect, I devoted a great part of my effort to creating a bubble capable of surviving the Crystal Sea, but I admit my inability to find the solution. The only way to go into it is with the appropriate suit. They say, since no one has dared go into it unprotected, that its gases produce hallucinations while they poison our organism, causing death 48 hours later. It is peculiar, but the Crystal Sea has developed a micro-atmosphere rich in oxygen, which makes it possible to breathe it and stay alive while the poison acts.
