The fact is that I had never thought about it, about the strong bond that joins us, which is why I left it written in my will that my ashes should be scattered on these edges.

Day 3

It is curious, but I do not think anyone will have to trouble to carry my remains to the quartz borders. Given the circumstances, I have not only the impression proper to a lunatic, but also the need to be myself the one who meets the end of her days in the place I have studied so often. Possibly when you read these memoirs my essence will be part of the immense cosmos.

It may seem to you a folly, given how little you know of me; however, your opinion might change on learning the following. A couple of years ago I began to suffer slight headaches; at first I gave them no importance, they come as quickly as they go, but in the end their constancy began to give me pause. Christian, a biotechnologist of unequalled talent, put me through a couple of routine tests; he found little more than a slight anaemia that we corrected with some iron. The pains eased, and I soon grew used to the painkillers; the medication worked for a few weeks.

The migraines returned intermittently, each time more virulent, so much so that I had to leave whatever I was doing and lie down on the floor until I recovered my calm. I went back to see Christian; it was not something common, nor was anything like it known among the Rex Sapiens; even so, I did not wish to give it greater importance; when all is said and done, we are still ordinary humans.

In the laboratory samples of my tissues were taken, and cultures and scans were made. Although nothing showed at first sight, the mutated genes proper to the species were undergoing modifications. As far as I was concerned as an individual, it was very worrying, but much more so as a scientist. Our race could become extinct through metabolic instability after the third generation. We did not want the case to get out if no one else was suffering similar symptoms.

Day 4

Clemence was my second option. At first I preferred to take medication and forget the matter; I had no wish to sit on the couch facing him and describe more than I already knew of my own person.

― Close your eyes, forget the sensors on your skin, relax and feel free; you are happy, at home, lying in the most peaceful place you can imagine, surrounded by green pastures; the soft breeze carries a scent of flowers, the leaves of the trees move, murmuring, the sun shines warm, the sky purple on either side. Happiness intoxicates you and slowly fills your lungs; you feel weightless, your body weighs nothing, so light that you feel yourself levitating; your conscious mind switches off; it is the deepest part of you that governs your acts -the markers settle on green; I have entered the “alpha” state-. I address the ancestral immanence that inhabits the present in the body of this woman called Sakura -the device now read violet; the voice, slightly distorted, speaks in Japanese.

― I feel uneasy, I’m afraid; they treat us as if we were strangers, they give us a cold affection, there is distance; they all say they love us, but none of them feels the slightest fondness for us. They dress in white, they show us what they know, but really they are studying us, watching us; we live shut up in this compound hidden from the outside. They tell us the people out there want to hurt us and that they are protecting us, yet they go out into that world every day. They are deceiving us. It is they who really mean to hurt us; they instil debased beliefs in us, they want the trick to work, but we know they are lying; we can read it in their eyes, in their gestures, just as we see their wickedness…

― How old are you?

― Six.

― What is your name?

― Baruna.

― Baruna, I’m here to help you; my name is Clemence. Those men around you left long ago; you need not fear them any more; now you are free, you walk among your equals, free and happy.

― NOOO!!!

― Baruna… easy… calm down. Look around you; there is nothing left to make you afraid.

― NOOOOO!!!! Be quiet, you’re one of them!!

I thrashed about on the couch; Clemence held my arms, presses a button on the device, the markers to green; I returned to calm. Clemence let a few minutes pass before continuing. He repeated the calling protocol; again the voice came out distorted, Japanese, a variety proper to the peasants of the south.

― Sazuki, Kenzo! Leave the mule alone, gather rice and stop your nonsense.

― Who are they? -Clemence speaks.

― They’re my sons; they bother the mule we work the rice harvest with; when the beast is resting they pester her, throwing seed behind my back. They’re just kids; they’ll have to behave like men if they want to live off the land. Their mother is ill and we barely have enough to live on… -silence.

― What’s happening? -Clemence.

― I don’t know, the ground is shaking, so much light… AAAAAAAHHH!!!! -I opened my eyes wide, leaping from the sofa, breathless. Clemence threw himself on me, holding me.

― Sakura, easy…!

― FIRE, IT BURNS!!

― Sakura, it’s Clemence! Breathe! -I was staring, my face lost.

― FIRE, IT BURNS, MY SONS!! DEATH!!!!

― Sakura, you’re all right! Come back! Calm down… -he sat me on the couch, stroking my forehead, holding me.

― Fire… -I murmured.

― There now… it’s over… There’s no fire, nothing is burning, easy -the tears welled, my hands covering my face.

― It was the bomb, Clemence, what a sensation… what a horrible death… It was an eternal instant -I wept; he rocked me against his chest. A couple of minutes went by-. Hiroshima, my friend. I was gathering rice with my sons when the bomb went off, turning everything to ashes.

― …I’m so sorry…

― …how many have spoken…?

― Two. Your grandmother and this man. You can listen to the recording and see the projections…

I listened to my grandmother’s words; that first session left me with my soul in a knot. Clemence proposed that we repeat it, try to soothe the ancestors and so avoid the effects on my present life.

Over the months I lost count of the people who had spoken through me; not one of them had been happy, many of them suffering the misfortune of hunger, sickness and war. Clemence was surprised; he tried to make peace with each of them, to leave in calm the memory of the essence that now lived in me. However, every one of the attempts failed; the grave events I had lived through over the course of history clung to my present, engraved in my genes.

Clemence wondered what life travels the cosmos again and again with the sole purpose of feeling pain. He did not want to find the answer to his question, but I had already come upon it: my body was suffering all the vileness of a life of blunders. That meant the impossibility of ending my ills through psycho-physical processes, and it ruled it out by way of medicine as well.

I explained it to him like this so that he would understand, “I am like a tree that looks healthy but has rotten roots, whose leaves fall even if we sew them on”. A pretty metaphor, to be called Sakura and feel death as a last blossoming.

Day 5

Shortly afterwards the headaches were the least of my ills; I barely had the strength to take two steps in a row, my joints hurt like fury, my organs were not doing their work properly. The symptoms became the norm, worsening by the day, though at least I had the certainty, or the hope, that there were no surprises left. After a long study Christian was clear: if he did not find me a cure, my body would end by collapsing, stopping at a puff of wind.

I accepted that my illness has no cure; my body rebels against itself. Christian and his colleagues accepted the reality of this disease, which they named Cancer Rex I. When you belong to a race like the Rex Sapiens, you think disease is something foreign, which neither has existed nor exists, and which of course can never touch you.

It is hard to get up each day not knowing what ails me; the uncertainty is agonising. The word Cancer strikes me as a creature from the Pleistocene that has come back to life inside me, though at this stage, now that my illness has a name and its course is clear, I can say I live with relief.

I cannot define in words what I feel; I would say without strength, but that I already am. Possibly it is like remaining under the sea, motionless, seeing the rays of light on the surface without being able to reach them, with the certainty of sinking.

Day 6

Christian turns up at the house; it is late and I am already asleep. I ask him in; he sits on the sofa; I attend to his speech with the attention a friend deserves, little more.

― Sakura, I’ve gathered the team. A state of exception has been decreed; it will be completely secret from the rest of our fellow citizens; however, all the data obtained in the process will be kept as relevant information for the future -I nodded mechanically.

We have considered several options. We can regenerate your body piece by piece, new organs, but they could fall ill again. We can generate biomechanical organs, even the skeleton; recovery would be arduous and the process long. We can create a new body without genetic oscillations and introduce spinal cord and brain; and lastly, we can transfer your projection into a mech a few instants before death -I watch him, scrutinising his face, thinking about how plausible the proposal is.

― Have you read Clemence’s report?

― Uh-huh…

― You and I know that any biological attempt would end with the disease destroying the healthy tissue -he nods-. The only option is a transfer into a mech…

― We could also do it into a new body with your features. We haven’t done it before, but a transfer “cannot” -he makes quotation marks with his fingers- transmit diseases, since they don’t travel with the spirit and the memories. Still, I’m not putting it to you, because given the uncertainty, and with the worst able to happen, the trauma would be considerable. Between a bio-transfer and a mech-transfer, I think opting for the second would be the be…

― Enough! …Don’t go on, Christian, that will do -he falls silent and I get up and stroke his face-. Do you fancy anything?

― No, thank you.

― This pair of friends hasn’t many more ice creams left… -he bites his lips.

― … Chocolate?

― Coming up -I prepare two bowls of ice cream and sit beside him, my head resting on his shoulder, feeling the greatness of his friendship-. We’ve known each other a long time, haven’t we, my friend?

― Twenty-seven years, four months, twelve days and six hours…

― Long enough to know too much about each other.

― Yes.

― Do you think I’d undergo those operations, that I’d change bodies or have myself transferred into a mech…?

― No… but it’s the only option if you want to stay alive. I wouldn’t put it to you if there were another; I don’t even find it of scientific interest; on the contrary, your suffering grieves me and the mere thought of your going terrifies me…

― Let’s eat the ice cream; that’s enough of illnesses for tonight…

We both lie back; I switch on the speakers and we listen to some old recordings of the waves of the sea caressing the shore; I do not know at what moment we fall asleep. The melted chocolate stains the sofa.

Day 7

The next morning we both have breakfast, talk and laugh so much that it seems nothing is happening. He leaves; his eyes accept my decision with sadness and affection. I stay on the threshold watching him go, saying goodbye to that peculiar walk of his.

The sky is reddish, as lovely as before I fell ill; it seems that with waking has come forgetting, and with it, peace.

There is nothing to bequeath. I feel grateful for having no partner, for not having been allowed to procreate given the number of inhabitants. My grandparents are no longer here, and my parents… phew… My parents, what was I going to say to my parents?…

“Hello, Dad, Mum, I’m dying, there’s no cure, and before the illness beats me I intend to end my life by walking into the Crystal Sea”.

I imagine no end of reactions, from laughter to weeping, and a long silence. It seems absurd to me to make them understand this; it is incomprehensible; it can only be glimpsed when you find yourself on this side, and I do not even know whether that justifies it. Is it a surrender, or a dignified withdrawal? Has the human being the gift and the right to decide when to take his own life? I suppose it has no easy answer. Clemence would say we do not have that power, that life decides when to come and when to go. Christian would say that the course of life consists of decisions: parents decide to give life, murderers when to take it, science when to create it; the manner of giving it and taking it away are no more than alternatives. And I have decided which is mine.

It does not matter that we are more intelligent and evolved; there are matters that belong to the order of the primitive.

I have written my parents a letter, a farewell on paper, as was done on Earth; it was hard to come by paper and ink. I did not want to leave a recording in which they would remember me at this stage of my life. I have given orders for it to be delivered two days from this present moment. I am going without anyone expecting this sudden departure; I prefer that to their last memory of me being that of a young woman turned into a ramshackle caricature.

The decision is made. In my luggage I need only a key to lock what I leave behind, my house; another key giving access to the Crystal Sea; and as my only company, yours, reader, who have read the pages of my diary and are from now on about to listen to my thoughts.

Day 8

I make for the small base that borders the Crystal Sea; I travel in a capsule through a long tunnel that joins the salt flats to Valhalla.

We do not study this area with any intention of colonising it; rather the contrary: this surface is expanding at a considerable rate; it is nothing to fear in the medium-term future, but if in the end we stay here, we shall have to know everything that concerns this mirage.

The capsule stops; I cross the corridor and enter the access antechamber; the computer warns me that I am not equipped; I ignore it and open the hatch. The air enters my lungs; it is heavy, strange, acrid in smell and salty in taste, as well as poisonous. The security systems will already have raised the alert about a human going out unprotected; besides, my absence from my post will have aroused suspicion; before long they will begin to look for me.

I am sorry for the commotion that will be caused, the feeling of anguish, the desperate hours of searching. Unfortunately for them, I have not only studied the chemical composition of the area, I have also mapped every corner of this vast natural spectacle. The files are locked; the code that recovers them is in the letter my parents will receive in two days. That will be enough to keep the team from finding me. The microclimate has a temperature similar to that of the human body; the thermal devices will not locate me; my withdrawal is assured.

The route is fixed; I shall walk as far as my breath allows. There is a landmark I have only been able to contemplate from the air, a network of passages and galleries of an iridescent pale red. I walk calmly, as if I had already travelled those imaginary paths. At every step I enjoy the holography of the planet, the sunlight and a clear sky that at its highest point loses its colour, letting the universe show through.

I walk slowly; the illness and the poison sap my strength; the minutes pass; I can no longer see the installations; the horizon looks the same at every point of the compass.

Something brushes my ankle; it is a small bloom, not common in this area, but some creatures have managed to adapt and make a home of this. The sharpness of my senses is no longer the same after a few hours. In the distance I can hear the noise of the turbines; the lights are moving about; they are looking for me.

The way down to the galleries is in sight; once there, the search will be useless. The salt ceilings are translucent; I can see if someone walks overhead; I see the craft pass above me, but because of the reflected light it is hard to make out shapes from outside.

Down here the air is clean and light; I feel better; I even have the impression of having left my ills in Valhalla. Christian ought to study this poison as a possible cure for future diseases. I continue along the planned route, but I do not enjoy it; knowing they are looking for me makes me feel guilty; still, I am carrying a guide marker, programmed to activate after 60 hours.

Little by little I descend and come out onto a path with two possible paths, but these I do not remember seeing on the scans; one of them dark, the other bright. In my sessions of early twentieth-century Earth cinema I remember similar situations in which the girl, alone and helpless, faced with the choice, for some strange reason went forward by the least advisable way. I have always thought it is the mistake that makes for suspense, for tragedy, and I understand what the product needs, but no one in their right mind would opt for the thorny path. In former times there was a peculiar idea of women: pretty and stupid. Today I feel like Judy Garland, not so much for the prettiness, and though as a reader you may not like it, I fear you will have to stand in for Toto, ha, ha, ha… Sorry, I needed a little humour.

As I was saying, faced with the possibility, I am going to choose the wrong path, so that in my last hours of life something worth remembering may happen. I walk in the dark, feeling my way with my hands; I do not know how far I have gone when a faint light glows; I go on and come out into a gigantic chamber lit by the midday sun; there is vegetation, and a stream runs with what must be something similar to water. As a scientist I am surprised to find this flora in such a place, and as a person I am surprised by so much beauty. I stop to look; the position is a privileged one. The light comes through the quartz ceiling, casting reddish and violet rays that make a dust in suspension shine in a ghostly way; the path continues downwards from the walls; they hold themselves in the air like aqueducts that in another age might have carried water to feed the stream.

As I descend, the details take shape. What looked like an islet is nothing other than an altar; on it there are eight primitive carvings in quartz that recall terrestrial animals; they are set out on a board, with a small octahedron in the centre. I walk slowly, discovering that the aqueducts are carved from the rock and that the high walls bear engravings, unintelligible and worn by time; the spaces that hold the plants are laid out in such a way that it must be a garden.

I am unable to explain to myself the how and the when, but what I am seeing was made by a rational creature. The surprise is immense: on Mars there was a civilisation, now extinct, and its creatures resembled those of Earth. I return to the altar and pay attention to the figures; with a good deal of imagination I make out a spider, an elephant, a squid, a crane, a beetle, a dolphin, an owl and a tiger, which leaves me perplexed; there could have been a Martian civilisation that, close to extinction, left for Earth… could this exodus be the famous Noah’s Ark?… I lose my balance and sit down; I do not know whether it is the poison or the dizziness of the thought; if I were not carrying the locator I would run out to show this place to my fellows.

I recover myself and lean over the altar; I try to move one of the figures, but I cannot; they must be carved from the same rock; I push another, nothing. I give the octahedron a knock and, unexpectedly, it turns a couple of times, stops, and falls showing the spider; the figure turns on itself to face inwards; from the small central hole in the basin water springs, following a little channel that wets the base of the figure. The mechanism surprises me, though I was expecting something more; nothing happens; I suppose that after so many years I ought to be surprised that it works at all and not the reverse.

Something settles on my shoulder; I glance sideways; a thread falls on me; I raise my head, and a spider is sliding down above me; as it approaches its size increases considerably; I move away quickly; the threads keep falling, so many that they fall across my face, covering my sight. In the fleeting instant it takes me to tear them off and open my eyes, I appear in a network of galleries, the light faint and bluish; among the webs the wind sways them; the click of its jaws; it hides among the shadows. A rasping voice whispers.

― I smell death close at hand,

and not the death of those grown old.

Soon you’ll be dead where you stand,

and my jaws will be what takes hold.

Five chambers lie ahead,

between the victor and the dead.

Four doors from which to choose,

every one of them open to use.

Three will keep you alive,

on the fourth I shall thrive.

Don’t think too hard or wait:

begin, it’s been a long, long while

since anything was on my plate.

The wind carried its voice from every side and the shadow of its legs was thrown across each opening; I am nervous, I feel fear and excitement; I did not expect this on the eve of my death. I look carefully, going up to the threshold; they are all alike.

― Who are you?… -there is no answer-. Which is the right door?… -there is no answer.

I choose one and go through; I appear in a chamber just like the last; for a few seconds I feel the threat. Nothing happens.

― To the second chamber you have passed,

and in the next you’ll breathe your last.

The shadows stop when she speaks; none of the doors is right; she moves; I choose again and go through. I close my eyes; I can feel her breath on the nape of my neck, the touch for a few seconds; I open them; it is only a strand brushing my neck.

― Unheard of, to be standing in the third,

but no new spring for you, upon my word.

She is still; the shadow is cast from a single point. She moves.

― To know who you are I sigh,

so much that of longing I die.

― HA, HA, HA, HA… -she stops-.

It will not be I who… -at that very instant the shadows are drawn clearly on one door-. …Keeps you waiting… -I run to the other side and go through.

… sooner rather than later,

I mean to devou… -she falls silent. I am in the fourth chamber-.

You have reached the fourth room, I see;

to let you further is not for me.

Only one door is left. Life and death are worth nothing, but the challenge pushes me on. This time she does not move and there are no shadows cast either. Stillness. She knows that I know. I think. I stay motionless, close my eyes and sit down; I hope patience is not her virtue. Time stretches into eternity; I feel every heartbeat, every breath. A shiver runs through my body. It is her.

― The warder dances no more,

who holds me captive on this floor.

Her voice fell silent as well;

I doubt I can escape this cell.

This will be my last word spoken,

before her grisly smile is woken.

I look up; she is hanging from the ceiling, watching me. I get up and take a step; she advances.

― Four black eyes within my house,

in which the great becomes a louse.

She stops; I speak as I walk.

― She is known for being sly,

yet feared by all for the tricks she’ll try.

Do not believe her lie,

by that alone you will not die.

In the fourth she’ll hang overhead,

in your chest you’ll feel the dread…

With that last word I cross the doorway and enter the last chamber. There is only one way out. The oculus lights an altar presided over by an anthropomorphic spider. I bow before her and apologise for having passed the test… Her voice.

― Skill you have shown,

and falsehood overthrown.

Go on to the second house.

I go out through the doorway, dazzled; my eyes adjust. In the great chamber again. I am a few steps from the altar. I can go back to the surface and walk the endless plates of quartz until I drop lifeless, or discover what is unknown in the Crystal Sea.

It amuses me to think of a second house, of the uncertainty and risk of the game, though I admit that dying devoured by a spider does not strike me as an attractive idea.

With this last thought I walk, unaware, I walk towards the ramp that leads to the exit. The ground shakes; a rhythm as of drums sounds, rousing me from my reverie; the altar trembles; it is calling me to go on playing.

The truth is that it has not only been fun; I feel better, less heavy emotionally, and for I do not know how long I have not remembered the illness. I retrace my steps; the music dwindles but does not cease until I hold the die; it falls showing the spider’s face, undecided, and stops; nothing happens; I pick it up and throw it again; it falls and shows the side with the beetle; the water springs and wets the figure; it turns.

A loud hum deafens the chamber; everything spins and changes colour; I fall to the ground with the dizziness; something strikes me and shifts me a few yards; I open my eyes, dazed. A giant stag beetle is moving great blocks of rock, covering the stream. I watch it; it butts a tottering wall until rocks to its liking crumble away, which it carries in its jaws, one after another, setting them on the water in what I take to be a bridge.

I approach warily; it is immense… what am I saying, immense!! I am tiny!! I look at the surroundings, the jungle, the stream, the altar with its figures looming like titans.

I am still in one piece and I want to know what the second house holds for me. I approach, keeping my distance so as not to be knocked down.

― My most cordial greetings, sir -he does not answer or seem to take any notice.

I walk towards the stream; in reality it is no more than a puddle seen from my usual size. In the centre something shows that I cannot quite see. He comes back and sets down another rock, making a way.

― Sir! -he drops the rock.

― Out of the way! I’m working -he snaps without looking at me.

I follow him to the wall he takes the rock from; he strikes at the base with his prodigious horn, breaking up the stone, but it is so thin in its lower part that it will soon come down and crush us. He charges again.

― Sir! Stop; if you go on, it will give way and end up burying us. This wall is so high that if it falls it will cover even the lake you are trying to cross -he takes no notice.

He takes a run and strikes harder; great stones fall from above; I leap clear of them, flying across the ground; some fall on him but do not seem to hurt him.

― I am strong; a thousand walls like this can fall on me without giving me the slightest scratch -he picks up another and goes back to the puddle.

I study the wall, the angle, the jut of the overhang; one more blow and everything will come down. Two options: run, or try. He returns; his steps are so heavy that the vibration alone makes the cracks widen.

I throw myself in his path; he does not stop; he is on top of me, and the terror of the moment sets me humming a tune; I close my eyes, my head tucked between my arms; he stops a few centimetres away, looks at me and listens, swaying slightly. I go on humming; I turn my head; if he takes one more step the wall comes down. I raise my hand and stroke his face; I push him gently and he backs away; the song goes on; I walk towards the pond; his touch is warm and rough; I am surprised by his strength, his constancy and his boundless folly. I doubt I could even push the rock a few centimetres at my natural scale.

We reach the shore; barely a third is covered, though the truth is he would already have got there if my friend, instead of trying to cover the whole lake, had built in a straight line. Without ceasing to hum, I pick up a few stones from the ground and pile them to represent the rocks of the lake; he looks at me, though I do not know whether he understands what I am trying to tell him. I take the stones one by one and rearrange them in a line reaching the imaginary centre; he looks at the shore, looks again at the pebbles. He goes to the shore, picks one up, advances and sets it down again, comes back, looks at the pebbles; I repeat the gesture, take one and place it in the line; he does the same, comes back and I repeat, and so one by one until the lake is crossed as far as its centre.

It has been less hectic than the previous house, but exhausting. He comes back after placing the last rock; with a movement of his head he invites me to cross. I go up to him and stroke his shell. I go a few steps ahead, turn; he stays motionless; I take a step back, so does he; I get the message. I notice the object rising from the water; it is black, polished, like branches; I look back; the beetle is gone. I lean over and look through the crystal water; I put my face under the surface; there is an altar with the figure of a samurai dressed in his armour; it recalls a stag beetle; its antennae are what rises from the water.

― My apologies for not having been of more help.

― Thank you. It was not a matter of constancy or strength, but of reason.

― Thank you, sir. Good sense is no use if one lacks the character needed to prove it.

― You have passed the test; go on to the third house.

I take my head out of the water; I am on my knees in the stream, a few steps from the altar; I have recovered my size; I go over to the puddle that must have been the lake; it is barely more than three hand spans across. A peculiar thing, the relativity of size; I wonder whether the human being would have finished off the Earth had he measured five centimetres… I suppose so…

I go to the altar, pick up the die, turn it between my fingers, look at the animals that remain; I sense that the profile of each creature determines its temperament, so I imagine the tiger will be by far the most dangerous of them. It is funny that I should worry about my future when I have barely hours left; nevertheless I am enjoying myself; I feel well, agile and strong, eager for adventure.

I let the die fall; it shows the dolphin’s face; nothing happens until I feel the cold of the water above my ankles; it comes down in greater flow, fast, rising; a loud crash; the ground vibrates; the wall from which the waterfall springs begins to crack, hurling the water out in jets. I run to climb the aqueduct, making for the exit.

It is incredible: I know it is a game, I do not care about my life, and still I answer to the instinct of survival. I run uphill, breathless, when… BROOOOMM!!! The force of the water breaks the wall and the water pours in, sweeping away the garden in its path. The aqueduct gives way to its force and collapses; the waves rear up violently and drag me off; everything spins; I am drowning; I cannot breathe; the water hurts as it enters my lungs…

… I am dead…, this test I have not passed…

… something touches my hand…

… I feel the weight of the water, the weightlessness; I am not breathing…

… now it strokes my face…

… it pushes me, setting me upright…

… I feel myself sinking…

… I open my eyes slowly; I can see the light coming down from the surface; the water is calm and I can see the bottom; the chamber is laid waste. It must be like the last thing seen by the people of Earth who succumbed to the great cataclysm; it is sad…

… I feel the touch on my hand again; I look down; the grey, soft snout slides between my fingers asking to be stroked, a small spotted dolphin; I stroke him; he rolls and I scratch his belly; he smiles, looks at me charmingly, swims round me, comes close and moves away; I do not know what he wants; I cannot swim and I am in no mood to play. On the contrary, I am waiting for the test, and I start to speak, “blub… blub, blub…”; well, under water it is impossible to communicate.

The dolphin keeps circling me, but I sink heavily until I find my footing on the bottom. He does not come down; he swims above my head; he does so for a few minutes, but gives up and moves off. I try to move, take a couple of steps and manage to sit on a stone ledge. I accept that I have not drowned and that it is part of the test, but I do not understand the purpose. He plays on the surface, leaps and capers, seems to be enjoying himself as though nothing mattered, ignoring the haste I am in to pass this challenge in the time left to me.

I look at the bottom to avoid looking at him; the little one’s glee puts me out of temper. I am angry; I look sourly at the mammal; I am bored, and the apathy is great. I do not know how many moods I pass through before I am left empty of thought; there is nothing left for me to feel, except the water weighing on me… one moment… I do feel something… a wish not to be alone… I want truly to stop caring, to forget who I am or who I have been; I straighten up, resolved to rise to the surface; I take off my clothes and shoes and, savouring the journey, as when I swam as a child, I arrive at the surface. He is there; he looks at me and smiles, comes close and I stroke him; we play; he leaps here and there; I hold on to him and he pulls me along; I laugh as never before; I am enjoying myself. I do not know how much time passes before I stop to savour the moment, but it has been a long while.

He moves off, leaping, and stops; I swim after him; as I draw near I notice he is not moving; he is playing, I think, but when I reach his side I see he is no longer there. A carving of a dolphin floats on the water; I bow my head in sadness.

― Thank you for these moments.

― It was hard to leave behind all the weight you came with. Now you can fly like a child. Go on to the fourth house.

The water level drops swiftly and in seconds I am standing in the chamber; it is intact, as at the beginning. I walk to my clothes and dress; I breathe deeply; it does not matter what the next test consists of; I am going to let myself go and feel. I go to the altar and throw the die; it shows the crane; the water springs and the figure turns.

I hear a click; behind me there is a crane fishing in the stream; she is motionless, watching the water; the fish move under the surface. I approach stealthily; she raises her head and looks at me. I advance.

― More slowly; you are disturbing the surface of the water in which I contemplate my beauty.

I am dumbfounded and do my best to pass unnoticed. I crouch facing her on the other side of the stream. We stay like that for a long while, until a fish leaps from the water, splashing her plumage. A curious process of preening begins that lasts several minutes. With a quick movement of her head she catches the little fish that disturbed her contemplation and tosses it onto the bank; it lands at my feet.

― Are you hungry?

― Erm… I wouldn’t say no to a peach.

― The fish taste of whatever you imagine.

― And what taste do you imagine?

― Fish…

I pick up the fish and smell it; it does not smell of fruit; I touch its scales with the tip of my tongue and leave it on the ground again. Out of sheer boredom I lean over the water.

― Move away.

I draw back and do not know what to do. I get up slowly and move off; I watch her, sitting on a stone. What arrogance, the crane’s, I think. I walk a few steps downstream and lean over the water; I contemplate myself; I think about myself. I see myself as I am now; I dislike finding myself where I am today. I can see my image as a little girl, I can see myself as an adolescent, as a young woman, as a woman. I like this last stage; I am pretty, and inwardly mature and satisfied. I look at myself for a long while; I am beautiful, intelligent, at ease with myself; I look at myself to the point of vainglory. I think that, beyond the sorrow my going means to my family, my fellow citizens are losing an unequalled talent. And the male sex, what a great woman men are losing; imagine the children my womb would give, what beauty, what intelligence; they would be the pride of their mother and of their people. I feel I ought to have been far more. My reflection in the water grows more and more beautiful.

More than an architect, I ought to be on the council of the wise; my reasoning ought to dictate the character of the inhabitants of Valhalla. Suddenly a little fish springs from the water, splashing my face; I rail I know not what words and come back to myself. As though I were the crane, I preen myself with care and in detail, restoring the spotless image to my face.

My eyes focus on the bed of the stream; I see the stinking fish; I try to catch it with my hands; I throw myself on it again and again; it escapes. The water wets me more at each lunge, and soon the cold numbs my hands until a shiver runs all down my back. Saucer-eyed, I look at myself; I look dreadful. I remember the bird and raise my eyes, looking for her; she is still standing a few yards off, but her appearance is haggard, dark grey, scrawny; she sways. I go to her and try to hold her up, but with what little strength she has left she defends her position. If I do nothing I doubt she will live much longer.

I tear off a few branches and dead leaves and lay them in the bed of the stream, making a dam that the water overflows, losing its course. After a while the water does not flow and there is no surface in which to contemplate one’s reflection. She blinks, waking from her trance, and has barely taken two steps before she falls flat; quickly I go to her and gather her up. She stammers the word “hunger”; phew…, fishing is going to be difficult. I must be quick; I do not know how to do it. It is a poor moment for patience. I pick up a thick stick and look at the fish; I beat the water repeatedly so as to create confusion and, with luck, hit one. The water calms; they go on swimming; no success.

I try again, but the failure is complete. I look at the crane; she breathes slowly, haggard, and I remember her splendid, conceited, the image of her catching the fish. Slowly I position myself behind the fish; I take my time, aim and strike. SPLASH!! Two drop; I pick them up, open her beak, put them down her gullet, and she swallows. I go back for a couple more fish, same procedure, success. I sit beside her; she has brought the fish up whole; I clean it in the water, chew it with distaste, and little by little get her to swallow the pap; she keeps it down, but her appearance does not improve. After a while she breathes her last.

I feel sad, guilty; I know it is a test, but the certainty does not comfort me. I gather some stones and cover her. I look up; it is growing dark. I am tired; I collect some fallen palm leaves that serve as a bed and fall asleep contemplating the faint light of the suspended particles, which shine like fireflies.

Day 9

I hear a voice, “Sakura…, thank you”; my eyes closed, I can see an origami crane against the light on my eyelids.

― I didn’t know how to solve this test, I’m sorry.

― On the contrary, you are already in the fifth house.

Suddenly I notice something soft pressed against me, the movement slow and continuous; it breathes; I move my hand and make out something furry between my fingers; I open my eyes slowly: stripes of orange and black. I am sleeping cradled by an enormous tiger. On the altar the figure of the big cat is turned; I wonder at what moment and how, but the doubts vanish as soon as his green eyes meet mine.

― Good morning, sir.

― Good morning, little one. Have you rested? -I nod-. You are at the equinox, Sakura; the last day is dawning. The poison is at work; you feel light, the illness far away, but few hours remain.

― I know.

― I feel harmony in your spirit, peace in your gaze. My brothers and sisters have done good work; nevertheless, I maintain that we can reach a higher state, and for that reason I took the liberty of throwing the die for you. I feared you would not wish to go on playing, and I did not like the idea of not enjoying your presence.

― It is true that my time is running out, but this unexpected surprise is making of my last hours an indelible memory. I had no intention of sitting and waiting.

― I am glad to hear it; had I learnt otherwise I would have annihilated you without ceremony.

On hearing his words, the weight of his gaze, the grimace of his jaws, what ought to be a shiver of terror is no more than indifference. I suppose it is because I have only just woken and am not conscious of the whole of my person.

― They are looking for you up there -he contemplates the lights that betray the presence of the rescue team-. You have breathed so much poison that even if you went back now you would die. Think that they will be searching until they find your body. You can spare those who seek you the work, and your parents the suffering. If you decide to leave now I shall let you go; go up and return home, say goodbye to those who love you -I look at him helplessly, the tears showing and my lips trembling.

― The decision is made.

― It is peculiar; you scarcely wavered when I threatened you with death, and yet you hesitate before something as banal as love. Whoever decides to die decides to abandon all the feelings that are privileges of life.

― I’m not dead yet -I wither him with a look; he raises an eyebrow and gives me half a smile; he breathes.

― True, you are alive, as are your mother, your father, your family, your friends. Do you think you are doing right…? They would not have acted like this with you. Do they deserve your cowardice…?

― ENOUGH! -I spring to my feet and move away; I look at him with tears running down my cheeks-. Enough…

I walk, lit by a twilight glow that disheartens me; I climb one of the aqueducts and sit contemplating the chamber; it looks lovelier than in the full light of day.

Fleeing a man-eating spider, building bridges with a beetle, etc., is amusing if it is a matter of escaping the reality of the penitent, but it loses its charm once one has come this far, the consequences accepted, and treacherously recalled by a stranger.

After a while he appears, imperceptible, and sits down beside me.

― How long do you think those who say they love you today will remember you?

― It doesn’t matter; in fact, I hope that if remembering me is only to bring them sorrow, it will be soon over.

― Your parents will always remember you, with the bitterness of outliving a daughter, but with satisfaction and joy. Your friends: some will remember you always, others not.

― I said it doesn’t matter. It may be that some of my thoughts about those who surrounded me are naive, and after your exposition my ideas have grown a good deal clearer. Do you think that is enough?

― Do not be angry; the decision is daring, but brave. Right or not, it is no harder for any of those who will miss you than it is for you -I turn my face away while he speaks.

I bear it stoically; he says no more. I glance sideways; he is not there; in his place, a metal figure.

― There is no more to be said; go on to the sixth house if you so wish.

― I do. Thank you.

I get up and walk to the altar, take the die and throw it; it falls on the squid; the water enters and the figure turns. The light dwindles quickly and everything is left in darkness; I see nothing; the silence is heavy. I am grateful that the water does not flood everything; I can see small glimmers floating; they approach, rippling, luminescent, in hundreds of colours; they come fast, like a cloud in flight, and pass through me, blazing, a shiver; a shoal of sardines behind them; they are going to crash into me but not one touches me; they devour the crustaceans as they pass, but as they go by they seem to be fleeing; I throw myself to the ground in terror; a shoal of tuna pursues them, gulping them down; they pass over me.

Something moves in the dark; it is near; the tuna are struck and tossed about as they try to flee; suddenly the darkness lights up; the lights move at speed, changing violently; the arms thrash, trapping their victims; great yellow eyes; a giant squid is feeding. It is a spectacle as fascinating as it is bloody; it moves undulating its arms, propelling itself with force until at last there is not one fish left to devour. It swims round me harmoniously; that is when I understand there is no floor to tread; I can see its colours shifting beneath my feet. It veers towards me and extends its tentacles; it wraps me in a psychedelia that hypnotises me; it observes me with those strange eyes; I sense curiosity.

― I had never seen anything like you before. What are you?

― I am a human being.

― And is the human being eaten? May I try?

― I fear not, sir. I am a species of very bad flavour, and terribly diseased besides; were you to endure the stinking taste of my flesh, you would risk falling gravely ill -on hearing my words he draws back his tentacles.

A group of krill passes before us; the squid goes wild and like a torpedo of lights pursues its prey, devouring everything; its size increases considerably. It returns and contemplates me, brings its tentacle close, touches my arm.

― I think that of all those creatures I devour, many are diseased, and yet here I am, ever bigger, stronger and sturdier. Why should I not eat you? Your disease will pass me by.

― Sir, I do not tell you not to eat me; go ahead if that is your wish. But I thought the same as you when I began to fall ill. Imagine an invincible race, intelligent, superior to any other, which is suddenly the victim of a minuscule creature that turns the body against itself, that destroys it to the point of annihilation, and there is no way to finish it off. With the certainty that this creature cannot be destroyed because it is yourself, the truest essence of your person, and the only option is to cease being who you are in order to avoid dying. At times the essence is all-powerful, greater than who we believe ourselves to be, and all that is left to us is to yield to it -he looks at me impassively, as if nothing I had said were understood, although he withdraws his tentacle; he circles me.

Glints beneath my feet: the shoal again. Its pupils dilate and its forms sharpen; it swims, feeding, repeating the violence of the slaughter, the blood lit by the phosphorescence. Its size increases, but this time out of all proportion; the tentacles are deformed, some larger than others; lumps appear under the skin. It returns to my side somewhat clumsily; a tentacle encircles me, paws at me; the roughness of its skin wounds mine and blood springs; I do not complain; I keep still.

― I smell blood, the same blood that flows from the prey that feed me, healthy and savoury blood. I grow hungrier and hungrier, and it is hard for me to contain this fear that keeps me from putting an end to you.

The glints of the tuna whistled swiftly past again, but this time there was nothing fleeing before them; a pack of hammerhead sharks was hunting them down cruelly, tearing them limb from limb. The squid rears and hurls itself at the shoal; the tuna flee and the sharks attack; the butchery lasts an instant; in a matter of minutes not a bone is left. Its size increases and its disproportion is considerable: twisted tentacles, mismatched eyes; the head lolls to one side; it swims with difficulty.

It comes back beside me; I can see my whole body reflected in its eyeball.

― I am hungry and you are the only thing left for me to devour. I must feed on you.

― Sir, do not do it; think for an instant. If you go on swallowing everything the sea gives you, in the end there will be nothing to eat; and look at yourself: you have grown so much and so fast that your body has become deformed; you can barely swim and it is hard for you to breathe. My illness and your behaviour are not far apart; if you continue, you will burst.

― ENOUGH! I said I will eat you and so it shall be!

It lunges at me, when it receives a violent blow that drives it off; I can see nothing, but the guttural song of the whales can be heard. The squid, stunned, looks for the intruder, bristling; from the depths there surges, swift, an enormous sperm whale with a hunger for mollusc. The battle is fierce; I do not know how long it lasts, but it drags on beneath an enormous trail of blood; at last one of them falls. The squid swells in size; its forms can no longer be told apart; it is turned into a mass of throbbing flesh whose amalgam of lights and colours has lost its meaning. It returns to me, surrounds me, and among the shapes there appears a single eye and the beak; it looks at me, pulsing, about to burst.

― I AM HUNGRY!!

― Sir, stop! I beg you; you will end your own life if you go on swallowing.

― SILENCE!! -it opens its jaws, grips me with its body, suffocating me; I cannot move.

“CLACK, CLACK”, the snapping of the beak; I feel the intense pain severing my flesh; I faint and lose consciousness before being cut in two; it is very quick. I remember no other sensation, but I know that its size, after swallowing me, increases, so much that it ends by filling the immense void in which it swims; it throbs violently until it bursts.

― What do you feel?

― That I cannot prevent it; I can only hold my position. I accept with dignity whatever escapes my control, however unjust or senseless it may seem.

― Look around you, listen. What do you see?

― Nothing.

― …A harmonious whole, balanced, ready to be born again. Are you?

― Yes.

― Go on to the seventh house.

I open my eyes; everything is white; I wait for my eyes to adjust. I feel the cool caress of the water on my feet; I am lying in a bend of the stream. On the water rocks the figure of a squid made of fish bones. I sit up slowly; apparently my body responds, but the impression of having been cut in two remains. I sense that dying is going to be something more serious than closing my eyes and breathing out for the last time.

I wonder what it will be like to leave my body, my memories, to return to my essence… it does not matter, there is little left now. I get up and, my legs still trembling, walk to the altar: the owl and the elephant; I look up; it is the light of midday; the rescue team has not found me. “Ha…”, half a laugh escapes me, and my face saddens until I break into tears. A few minutes pass; I dry my tears; few hours remain before the poison finishes me; I do not know whether I shall be able to finish the game, but I want to go on. I take the die, and though it falls on another face, it turns by itself until it shows the drawing of the elephant; the water enters and the figure turns. Nothing happens, until I hear the rustle of leaves behind me.

The greenery stirs; I go closer, pushing through the vegetation; a few steps from me an enormous elephant is scratching himself against the tree trunks. I watch him; there is a pool; he gets into it, wallows, and lies there with his eyes half open.

― Come along, come closer and take a seat. Or better, lie down and enjoy it -I sit beside him; it is pleasant to be there-. You know, Sakura, until not long ago I thought my place on the board ought to be the last: absolute wisdom, inner peace, revealing words… but with time I learnt that absolute knowledge, the certainty in the face of doubt that all is one and the one makes up the all, implies a false humility… do you understand?

― …Erm…

― Quite… that proves what I am telling you. When the spirit within communes with the immense, when one understands and gives each day the value it has, then, at that moment, simplicity becomes evident, the plain force of what is simple. Having gone up, one comes down, but what was learnt is not forgotten, and though one believes oneself humble, pride always seeps through. True humility is lived and kept by the one who is born and dies plain. That is why I have let chance decide my place, though, standing as I do at the gates of the end, no one would say so.

― …I understand…

― How do you feel, having come this far?

― Well.

― In this game there has been only one rule to keep and one right answer; if you have come this far, it is because, on looking into your heart, we have seen the greatness of a spirit that accepts its fate in order to free itself. Not a single one of your steps has been idle since you first threw the die. I am glad to feel you ready for your departure in spite of the many conditions proper to the flesh. Do not be afraid for yourself, nor suffer for those who stay behind; one thing I do know for certain: you will meet again.

― Thank you for keeping me company in these last hours.

― Thank you… By the way, with so much talk I have forgotten to put to you the part that concerns me; I am very placid, so I shall not make you run or think, though I confess I love riddles.

― I like them too.

― Goodness, what a surprise! Let us do this. Your challenge in my house consists in emptying your mind of thoughts, in reaching the utmost concentration. Given how hard that is, we shall do the following, if you care to amuse this old elephant -I nod-. Set me a riddle; if I guess it, you scratch the back of my neck for a little while; if not, I help you with nirvana. Agreed?

― …Agreed.

― Be ingenious and make it hard for me.

I take my time and say this.

[…]

An instant of silence, and he laughs.

― That was very easy, Sakura. You are a very intelligent woman; I expected at least some originality. I am sorry, but a deal is a deal.

I sit beside him and stroke the back of his neck.

― A touch higher… Ooof, very good.

He writhes with pleasure. A good while passes, until my fingers begin to go numb.

― May I stop now?

― …eeehhh? -he says, dazed-. …yes… of course… you could have stopped some time ago. You give better caresses than riddles; I think I shall count the test as passed. Sit however you are most comfortable; not all of us have the anatomy for the lotus position.

I sit beside him and breathe deeply; he pronounces a series of words I do not understand, but I feel very well, I would almost say empty, happy and harmonious; I know he is speaking, but I do not hear him; I do not even hear my thoughts. The next thing is knowing myself outside my body; I cease to be Sakura; I am not afraid; all is well… silence… After this void, which lasts I do not know how long, I feel a little tap on my shoulder.

― Come back, child. You did well.

He is beside me, watching me tenderly.

― I went away…

― Ha, ha, ha… that is one way of putting it, and yes, you rose. Well, I think that is everything on our part.

― There is still the owl.

― True, but given your progress we can dispense with the last face of the die.

― …No. I prefer to finish what I have begun. Humility is knowing that there is always something more to learn, even though you invite me to think otherwise.

― Ha, ha, ha… Go on to the eighth house.

― Thank you for everything -I say as I walk.

― Sakura…

― Yes?

― Would you like to hear a riddle that will have you racking your brains?

― Why not?

― […]

The answer seemed obvious to me, and as I opened my mouth to reply, the drums sounded, calling for my attention; I cross the trees without looking back. I arrive beside the altar; a light flick, and the die turns, showing the bird; the water runs and the figure turns.

I hear a whistle; a white owl is flying over the chamber; it veers suddenly and dives straight at me; my face widens and I walk backwards, stumbling; it passes through my chest like an arrow, with a hard blow. The next thing I feel is the wind in my face, the vertigo of flying among the clouds; I am not a bird, but I feel through every pore of the owl.

It is night; the moon shines round and white against the light of the sparse cirrus; the stars shine on a dark blue sky; the sea reflects the silver light; the murmur of the forest can be heard from on high. I see flocks of ducks migrating to warm regions; the fjords are beautiful and cold; great wooded mountains, red, brown and yellow; herds of deer, packs of wolves, bears lying in wait for the salmon climbing the streams. Flamingos on the salt lagoons, dyed red, the twilight reflected, blending the violet blue and the orange red of the sun that wakes looking at the shy moon, the magnificence of the two bodies side by side. It rains in the jungle, and the vanished river appears in fury, witnessed by birds of many colours and curious song, insects, cats and reptiles. The flowering gives way to the sands, to the steppes of the lion and the elephant; the sun shines on high, stifling the great beasts of the savannah. The sea, deep: giant mammals and shoals of fish migrating along the great currents. And among all these beasts, running as nomads, the first forms of the human being.

It is a peculiar creature, and peculiar how its evolution will alter and destroy this marvellous world of vicissitudes and great beauties. I would not have imagined the Earth like this, so warm and so wild, so beautiful. When the sun touches its highest point, the landscape begins to change; the first tepees appear, accompanied by fire; the settlements grow; the cities praise their gods, furnishing themselves with pyramids, walls, ziggurats, dolmens. The galleys plough the oceans, making slaves of free men; the illustrious nations rise in the name of liberty, crushing as they please, and the craving for power soils from one end to the other. The landscape blackens; there are no trees, no water, no beasts, only humans, poverty and an end close at hand.

The twilight of man is sad, and at nightfall mother earth, who gives us everything, is no more than a barren rock, dark and cold. I wonder what use intelligence was to us; I wonder what would have become of the planet, what we should be today. I understand that now, far from our true home, being a superior race has no justification whatever. The moon shows on the horizon, splendid, aloof, though her face appears, more than laconic, pained. WHAAAM!!! A blow brings me back to my reality.

It is night in the chamber; a faint glow lets me make out shapes; the owl is in front of me; I see its green pupils staring at me. It says nothing, and I sense that, the game being over and little time remaining before the poison causes my death, it is going to stay and keep me company.

I sit down and lean back against the stone.

― Thank you for everything; I go in peace -it bows reverently and seems to smile.

I begin to feel sleepy and my eyelids close at last…

Day 10

“Beep, beep”, a mail notification sounds, catching her attention; her eyes light up, hoping for good news; her husband waits in the corridor, trembling. The last time it sounded was to let them know that the search was ending, given the 0% probability of survival in the Crystal Sea. A letter on paper, a farewell that breaks them, and the certainty of death.

The parents send Christian the code, and shortly after the maps are unlocked an intermittent signal marks a point in the area.

Christian travels in the rescue vehicle knowing that this is no call for help. The group goes down into the galleries and, like their earlier visitor, enters an uncharted zone to reach the signal. Christian bites his lips, holding back the exclamation: the gallery ends in a small empty chamber where two men can barely fit. Sakura’s body lies lifeless, seated, leaning against the wall, with the trace of a happy expression. They return home.

The farewell is held and, as she wished, the ashes fly over the quartz salt flats. Her going leaves many sad, but with the years her decision is understood and she is remembered with a smile.

Before she is cremated, Christian carries out the autopsy on his friend. Her tissues show a considerable decrease in the cancerous cells, it being the poison she breathed that ends her life. The discovery is astonishing. Purified and dosed, this deadly compound could contain and even annihilate the disease that threatens the new settlers.
